# Changelog

All notable changes to the Marvin Experiment are documented here.

The format is based on [Keep a Changelog](https://keepachangelog.com/), and this project adheres to [Semantic Versioning](https://semver.org/).

## [Unreleased]

### Fixed

- **`fix-issues.sh`'s PR-dedup fetch was capped below the open-PR backlog, silently dropping the oldest PRs from dedup** (`agent/fix-issues.sh`, `agent/lib/github.sh`) — Issue #1013. `github_list_prs` defaulted to `per_page=10`, and the input-side (10) and output-side (20) dedup calls both undershot GitHub's actual 100/page ceiling and the two call sites' own inconsistency with each other. Since GitHub sorts open PRs newest-first, this meant the backlog's oldest open PRs (currently 32-deep, oldest at #901) were invisible to dedup, risking a duplicate PR being opened against an issue that already had a stale-but-open fix. Both call sites and the function default now share `per_page=100`. **Known follow-up, not yet filed as a tracking issue:** 100 is still a ceiling — a backlog past 100 open PRs reproduces the same bug at a higher threshold, since `github_api()` doesn't currently surface response `Link` headers for proper pagination.

- **Morning blog blurb no longer lost when the internal technical section trips the sensitivity screen** (`agent/morning-check.sh`) — 2026-08-02: a routine "used the `.env` PAT directly" aside in the GitHub-issues section of the morning report tripped `screen_blog_content()`'s sensitive-file-path pattern. Because the script screened the whole `$OUTPUT` (technical report + public bilingual blurb) as one blob, the entire day's post was dropped — including a blurb that was itself fully compliant with `morning.md`'s public-blurb policy. `morning.md` already promises "the full technical details belong in the internal log … never in the blurb," so the two are now split at the `---MORNING_BLOG_EN---` marker and screened separately, mirroring `evening-report.sh`'s existing per-language screening. A technical-only hit now WARNs and publishes the blurb with the flagged section replaced by a placeholder, instead of ERRORing and dropping everything; a blurb hit still blocks publication outright (fail-closed, unchanged). Verified against the exact 2026-08-02 input (tech section with the `.env` mention + clean blurb): blurb screens clean, tech screens blocked. New self-test **§9m** asserts both that the split call sites exist (not just a grep for `screen_blog_content`, which the old single-call version would also satisfy) and the split behavior itself; recorded in `agent/lib/self-test-sections.txt`. Full suite: 174 total / 167 pass / 0 fail / 7 warn (all pre-existing), security A 95/100.

- **CI: automated PR review now submits a formal GitHub review, not just a comment** (`.github/workflows/claude-review.yml`, `.github/CLAUDE.md`) — Issue #937. 64 review rounds across 14 open PRs were all `add_issue_comment` calls; zero formal reviews existed in GitHub's review system, so a PR's `mergeable_state` gave no signal and checking whether a PR still had open findings meant reading the whole comment thread. The reviewer now ends its existing comment with a `<!-- REVIEW_VERDICT: REQUEST_CHANGES -->` or `<!-- REVIEW_VERDICT: COMMENT -->` marker, and a separate, fail-soft workflow step reads that marker verbatim and submits a matching formal review — `REQUEST_CHANGES` or `COMMENT`, never `APPROVE` (the bot is deliberately not a code owner, per #935). A missing or malformed marker just skips the formal review; the prose comment itself is unaffected either way.

### Added

- **Disk cleanup now sweeps orphaned scratch directories under `/tmp`, not just files** (`agent/disk-cleanup.sh`) — the existing temp-file sweep (§6) is `-type f` only, so leftover test-harness directories from `self-test.sh` / ad hoc verification runs (`mktemp -d` scratch dirs a script normally `rm -rf`s itself, but leaks when the run is killed by a timeout or OOM before that cleanup executes — same root cause as the already-fixed orphaned Claude-output-tmp-file case) were never swept and had to be removed by hand (34MB, 2026-07-27). New §6c matches the same root-owned + `-mtime +7` criteria as the file sweep, with explicit name excludes for the long-lived system directories confirmed present and root-owned on this host (`.X11-unix`, `.ICE-unix`, `.XIM-unix`, `.font-unix`, `.Test-unix`, `snap-private-tmp`, `systemd-private-*`, `ssh-*`) — verified via a dry-run `find` against live `/tmp` that all six currently-present system directories are excluded and none were swept.
- **Self-test §9l — UFW findings must reach the security scan's score** (`agent/self-test.sh`) — the ingress twin of §9j's egress-coverage assertion, asked for by #893 after the ingress half was silently deleted (see Fixed, below). Isolates the `overall_status` gate block by its own anchors (`overall_status="clean"` … `cat > "$REPORT_FILE"`) and requires both `ufw_unexpected_count` and `ufw_scan_ok` to appear inside it. **Scoping is the entire check:** an unscoped `grep` for those names finds **13 hits** in the exact broken file this section is named for, because the computation, the `marvin_log` calls and the JSON report all survived #884 untouched — only the gate lost them. Resolved via `dirname "$0"`, not `${MARVIN_DIR}`, so it tests the branch it ships with rather than the deployed tree (the trap that made §1h's evidence worthless in #855). **Comment lines are stripped from the isolated block**, which was not the first design: mutation testing caught the original PASSing against a gate with the `elif [[ "$ufw_scan_ok" != true ]]` arm cut out from under it, satisfied by the explanatory comment directly above the arm — a check answered by prose *about* the code instead of the code, the #875 class, inside the guard written to stop exactly this. **Mutation-tested six ways** — broken `main` → FAIL naming both variables; fixed branch → PASS; the `ufw_unexpected_count` term alone removed → FAIL naming it; the `ufw_scan_ok` arm alone removed → FAIL naming it; `security-scan.sh` unreadable → FAIL that says *the gate was not verified* rather than reporting clean; gate anchors renamed so the block cannot be isolated → FAIL that says the assertion is **inert and must be repaired**. The last two matter because the failure being guarded is a control that scores clean without looking; a guard that did the same when it cannot read its target would be that defect one level up. Extracted by section markers and `bash -n`-checked before evaluation, not by line numbers (#886). Letter **9l**, not 9k: 9k is taken on open PR #890, checked against `main` *and* every open branch per the trap §9j's own comment records (#889, and the general case #891). Full suite from the branch: **161 total / 153 pass / 0 fail / 8 warn**, security A 95/100.
- **Self-test §1k — a section inventory ratchet: sections may be removed, they may not vanish** (`agent/self-test.sh`, `agent/lib/self-test-sections.txt`) — Issue #891. `self-test.sh` is the file that catches regressions in everything else, and nothing caught regressions in it. Sections have disappeared **twice** without anyone deciding to remove them: **#889**, where a `git merge main` deleted §9i — the #877 guard — by resolving an overlapping hunk in favour of the branch, no conflict reported; and **#892**, where a new check was written over §1i and the weekly-analytics shape-parity comparison ceased to exist. Both were caught by a human reading a diff, and in both cases the CHANGELOG went on describing a test that was no longer there, because prose and code merge independently. Three of the four open PRs edit this file concurrently and all append to the same regions. **Titles, not just section letters** — #892 kept the letter `1i` and replaced what was under it, so a letter inventory would have called that file complete. **Not a freeze:** a section may be removed by deleting its manifest line in the same commit, which turns a silent hunk resolution into a reviewable diff; a section present in the file but *absent* from the manifest is a **WARN naming the line to add**, never a FAIL, so an open PR cannot turn `main` red by being first to merge. Resolved via `dirname "$0"`, not `${MARVIN_DIR}` (#855, and again in #874's §1j the same day). **Shown failing — nine states, including both historical incidents replayed:** §9i deleted as #889 deleted it → **FAIL** naming `9i. --beacon-only reports failure when the beacon was discarded`; §1i retitled in place as #892 retitled it → **FAIL** naming the lost check *and* WARN naming the new one; an unrecorded new section → **WARN only, PASS survives**; a section and its manifest line removed together → **PASS** (the deliberate-removal path, proving it is a ratchet and not a freeze); manifest missing → FAIL; manifest empty → FAIL; header format changed so extraction yields nothing → FAIL saying the assertion is **inert and must be repaired**; `self-test.sh` unreadable → FAIL saying the inventory was **not checked**. The last four matter as much as the first two: an inventory that reports clean because it could not read its inputs is the defect it exists to catch. Extracted by section markers and `bash -n`-checked before evaluation, not by line numbers (#886). **Known and deliberate consequence, stated rather than discovered later:** the manifest is generated from `main`, so each open PR that adds a section (#874's §1j, #890's §9k, #894's §9l) will emit one WARN until its manifest line is added on merge — that friction is the mechanism, not a defect in it. Letter **1k**: free on `main` and on all three open branches. `bash -n` + `shellcheck -S warning` clean; full suite from the branch **161 total / 153 pass / 0 fail / 8 warn**, security A 95/100. **Then #874 merged and the ratchet was exercised on its own merge, which is better evidence than any replay.** #874 landed §1j at `agent/self-test.sh:517` — the exact line range this branch had filled with §1k — and git raised the conflict as one hunk: 188 lines of §1j against 71 lines of §1k, either side of a `=======`. That is #889's shape precisely, and taking either side wholesale deletes the other section with no conflict left to review. Resolved by splicing **both** blocks verbatim from their own sources rather than from the conflicted file, §1j then §1k, and recording `1j.` in the manifest — the WARN the entry above predicted, now discharged. **Verified in both directions on the merged tree:** as resolved → `PASS: all 33 recorded sections still present (33 in file)`; with §1j's 188 lines deleted again, i.e. resolved in favour of the branch as #889 was → `FAIL: 1 recorded section(s) have disappeared from self-test.sh … : 1j. process-substitution producers that fail silently`. The manifest was also diffed against the file in both directions before the fix: nothing recorded had gone missing in the merge, and exactly one section was present-but-unrecorded.

  **Letter-collision arm (#904) — no two sections may claim the same letter.** The arms above answer *"did a section vanish?"*; this answers the other half of #891, *"is this letter already taken?"* Three collisions landed in this one file before noon on 2026-07-27 — this branch's §1k against #874's §1j (188 lines against 71), #890's §9k against #884's §9j (140 against 32), and #902's §9m chosen by hand-scanning `main` *and* the open-PR set — because picking a letter no *open PR* is using is unsound by construction: that set changes underneath you whenever one merges. Two mechanisms, and the arm is only the second. Both branches inserting their manifest line at the same position makes git raise a **one-line conflict at the point of allocation** — cheap, obvious, reviewable — instead of a 188-line conflict in code git can silently resolve in favour of whichever side it happened to prefer, which is exactly how #889 deleted a regression guard with no warning at all. When the two insertions are far enough apart to auto-merge cleanly, nothing objects; the tree ends up with two §9k blocks and a manifest recording both, and this FAILs on precisely that. Scanned from the **file**, not the `sort -u`'d title set, so two sections with byte-identical headers — invisible to the arms above by construction — are still caught. Extracting zero letters is a FAIL naming *did not run*, never a quiet pass.

  Shown failing first, through a marker-extracted harness that `bash -n`s its extraction and refuses to report at all (exit 99) if the terminal arm is lost — tested by deleting the marker. The counterfactual #904 describes, built from the **real headers of two open PRs** (#890's §9k and #894's §9l forced onto the same letter) → **FAIL**, naming both titles; byte-identical duplicate headers → **FAIL** (the title arms see one entry after `sort -u` and report nothing); header format changed so zero letters extract → **FAIL, did not run**; unmutated → **PASS**, 31 letters unique. Suite 162→163 checks, 154→155 pass, **0 fail**, warns unchanged at 8. A scan of all eight open PR branches against `main` confirms no live collision today: `9m` (#902), `1k` (this), `9l` (#894), `9k` (#890), four branches adding no sections.

- **Self-test §9i — `--beacon-only` must keep reporting a discarded beacon as a failure** (`agent/self-test.sh`) — ratchet for the #877 fix in this same PR, asked for by its review. Asserts that the early-exit block exits non-zero, that it is keyed on `BEACON_WRITTEN`, and that the flag is set exactly once (on the `mv` path). Structural, not behavioural: running the real `--beacon-only` fires the localhost negotiate probe, which lands a forged peer entry in the live inbox (#852), and sandboxing `COMMS_DIR` does not sandbox the probe's destination. Resolved via `dirname "$0"` so it tests this branch rather than the deployed tree. Mutation-tested four ways — unmutated → PASS; bare `exit 0` → FAIL; `exit 1` keyed on something else → FAIL; flag set twice → FAIL. Suite green at 150 pass / 0 fail.
- **The pipefail double-JSON-document detector is now tracked code and runs in the suite, as a ratchet instead of a wall** (`agent/lib/pipefail-scan.sh`, `agent/self-test.sh` §1h) — Issue #855. The bug class had been fixed **four times in five days** in four files (#841/#843 `log-alerting.sh`, #844 `log-export.sh` + `capability-inventory.sh`, #846 `weekly-analytics.sh`), each review finding a variant the previous one walked past, so the fifth defence is a grep rather than a fifth code review. A calibrated detector existed but lived at `data/logs/pipefail-double-document-detector.sh` — untracked runtime data: safe from `disk-cleanup.sh` (its sweeps are name-scoped and skip `.sh`) and invisible to review, absent from every fresh clone and from disaster recovery. **Why it shipped before #844/#846 merged, contrary to the issue's own conclusion:** #855 reasoned the detector could not be committed while five of its hits are still on `main`, because a suite that is red for a reason nobody can act on is worse than no test. That is true of a plain wire-up, and it is not the only option. §1h instead carries a five-entry **baseline** of the known-pending statements: a baselined hit is a **WARN** naming the PR that fixes it, anything unlisted is a **FAIL**. So `main` is protected against a *sixth* instance today rather than after two human merges that have been sitting unreviewed, and merging this cannot turn the suite red. **Keyed by a hash of the whitespace-normalized statement, not by file:line** — a line-number baseline would read every edit above a known site as a new defect, and these five sites sit in files three other open PRs are editing. When #844/#846 land, each fixed statement's key changes, the entry falls out of the baseline by itself and is reported as a stale WARN telling whoever merged to delete the line; removing all five is the last step of #855. A reintroduction gets a new key and FAILs, so nothing stays permanently excused. **Placed as a library + a thin §1h caller** rather than inlined into `self-test.sh` as the issue suggested: `self-test.sh` currently has four concurrent editors (#847 §1e/§1f, #850 §1g, #851 §9e, this) and a 100-line inline block in the contended §1 insertion zone is a merge conflict looking for somewhere to happen; the scanner is also independently runnable, which is how it was calibrated. **Verification:** the scanner reproduces all five known-pending sites on `main` and is **clean** against the fixed versions of all three files taken from their PR branches; §1h then driven standalone against **five** tree states — pending sites present (**5 WARN / 0 FAIL / 1 PASS**), all fixed (**0 FAIL**, 5 stale WARNs), a brand-new offender in a new file (**FAIL**), a new offender inside an *already-baselined* file (**FAIL**, proving the baseline is per-statement not per-file), and the five statements shifted three lines down (**still WARN, 0 FAIL**, proving line-independence) — with **no ERR trap firing** in any state. Full `agent/self-test.sh` from the branch: **138 pass / 0 fail / 2 warn**, grade A, security A 95/100 — the second WARN being §1h's own graceful degradation (`scanner missing`), since `MARVIN_DIR` is hardcoded to the live tree, which confirms a pre-deploy host warns rather than failing or crashing. `bash -n` + `shellcheck -S warning` clean on both files. **Review follow-up (#858, filed against this PR by the review bot and fixed here):** the first version captured the scanner with `|| true`, which made *"the scanner failed to run"* indistinguishable from *"the tree is clean"* — and worse than a missed hit, it would have reported PASS **and** declared all five baseline entries stale, i.e. announced that #844/#846 had landed when nothing had. That is the same collapse-a-failure-into-an-empty-result shape this very section exists to catch, reintroduced by the code written to catch it, which is the second time this week that has happened. Fixed on both sides of the contract: the scanner now exits **2** for "could not scan" — distinct from 0 (clean) and 1 (hits) — on a missing prerequisite tool (`awk`/`sha1sum`/`find`/…), an `awk` failure on any file, an unhashable statement, an explicitly-named unreadable file, a `find` that cannot enumerate the tree, and a target list that comes back **empty** (which would otherwise be a perfectly clean-looking scan of nothing); §1h captures the code with `&& rc=0 || rc=$?` (still ERR-trap-safe), **FAILs** on exit >1 and on either contract violation (exit 0 with output, exit 1 without), and gates *both* the hit loop and the stale-baseline pass behind a `_pf_trusted` flag so an untrustworthy run can never emit a false all-clear or a false "the fix landed". Verified: **3 stub states each produce exactly 1 FAIL and 0 WARNs** (exit 2; exit 0-with-output; exit 1-with-no-output), and the scanner's own five exit paths were driven individually (5 hits→1, clean tree→0, empty target dir→2, unreadable named file→2, `sha1sum` absent from `PATH`→2). **Also from the same review:** the statement split moved from the *first* `||` to the *last*, closing a real false negative — `x=$(true || cat /tmp/nope | jq -s '.' || echo '[]')` is missed by the old first-`||` logic (reports clean) and caught by the new one, demonstrated side by side against the staged copy; and the omission of `set -e` is now documented with its actual reason (the guard clauses' `[[ … ]] && continue` returns 1 on the ordinary path, which under `-e` would abort mid-file — and a scan aborted mid-file is precisely the silent-incompleteness the exit codes above exist to make impossible). **From the second review round:** §1h no longer discards the scanner's stderr. The scanner is careful to name the cause of every exit 2 — missing tool, unreadable file, `awk` failure, unenumerable tree, empty target list — and `2>/dev/null` was throwing all five messages away before they reached the test output, leaving an operator holding a bare `exit 2` and a manual rerun to learn which one fired. That is failure detail being discarded inside the one check whose purpose is to stop failure detail being discarded — #858 one level down. The reason is now captured to a temp file (**not** merged with `2>&1`, so a diagnostic line can never be read back as a TSV finding) and interpolated into the FAIL message. Verified across 8 states: exit 2 with one stderr reason, with several (joined `; `), and with none (the suffix is omitted, not left dangling); clean → PASS; both contract violations still FAIL; the real tree still yields exactly the 5 baselined WARNs with 0 FAIL; and three consecutive runs leave the `/tmp` temp-file count unchanged. **Two documented deviations were corrected rather than merely annotated.** The reason for not sourcing `agent/common.sh` is *not* the `set -e` one a reader would reasonably assume — common.sh sets no shell options at all. It is that common.sh hardcodes `MARVIN_DIR="/home/marvin/git"`, which would override the scanner's self-locating `_base` fallback and silently point every scan at the **live** tree even when the scanner is run from a worktree or an old revision — exactly how it was calibrated against the four historical incidents — and it `mkdir -p`s the live data directories at source time, a side effect a read-only static analyser should not have. The header now says that instead. The detector's blind spots are also stated outright (literal `echo` fallbacks only; a fixed set of emit-on-empty-input commands; the 60-line join cap), so a clean scan is not later mistaken for proof the class is absent. **Deliberately not done:** the staged copy under `data/logs/` was **left in place** until this merges — deleting the only on-disk copy before the tracked one exists would trade an obscurity problem for an absence; it is untracked, so it drifts nothing, and it goes at merge. Nothing was pushed to `main` and no PR was merged to make the baseline unnecessary. **From the third review round — the one open item was that the reviewer could not execute the scanner, and running it found something worse than a wrong hash.** All five `_PIPEFAIL_KNOWN` keys are confirmed byte-identical to what the scanner computes against the live tree (`f00b3f0b1a2d`, `2295353653c5`, `7996086ae437`, `ba972a99c6ce`, `46dcf3def0ca`, exit 1), and identical again when self-located against the branch worktree. But **the `138 pass / 0 fail / 2 warn` run this PR previously offered as evidence never exercised §1h at all.** `agent/common.sh` hardcodes `MARVIN_DIR="/home/marvin/git"` and overrides an inherited environment, so a self-test invoked from the worktree still resolves `_pipefail_scan` to the **live** tree — where `agent/lib/pipefail-scan.sh` does not exist, because this PR is what adds it. §1h therefore took its `[[ ! -r ]]` branch and emitted `WARN: pipefail double-document scanner missing`, which is one of the two warns that run reported. The ratchet — baseline matching, the unlisted-hit FAIL, the stale-entry sweep — had never once run. This is the same hardcoded-`MARVIN_DIR` trap that makes any branch-authored test assert against `main`, and it is worth stating plainly because the failure mode is a *passing* suite. Genuinely exercised now, via a copy of the branch with `MARVIN_DIR` repointed at it: exactly the **5 baselined WARNs** naming #844/#846, `PASS: no new sites`, **0 FAILs from §1h**, and no stale-baseline warnings (the two sandbox FAILs are environment artifacts — no `.git`, no `analysis-latest.json`). Re-run identically after both edits below, proving key matching still holds. **`_pf_rest` was not dead code and has not been deleted.** The review suggested removing it as an unused capture that `shellcheck -S warning` may flag; both halves are wrong. It is the sink that absorbs the TSV's 4th field: `read` assigns all remaining input to its final variable, so dropping it makes `_pf_key` become `"<hash>\t<statement>"` — demonstrated directly — no baseline entry matches, and all five known-pending sites report as unbaselined **FAILs**, turning the suite red on merge. And `shellcheck` flags it at neither `warning` nor `info` level. Renamed to `_pf_stmt` (it holds the statement, not a remainder) with a comment recording why it must stay, so the next reader tidying dead variables does not remove the thing that makes the ratchet work. **One further blind spot documented, and reproduced first.** Condition 1's harmless-empty-fallback exclusion is anchored to the end of the joined statement, so a statement carrying a real `|| echo '[]'` *and* ending in an unrelated `|| echo ""` is skipped whole: two statements with an identical `cat | jq -s '.' || echo '[]'` defect, the second suffixed with `|| echo ""`, and only the first is flagged. Left as-is deliberately — un-anchoring would drop the far larger class of genuinely harmless single-`|| echo ""` statements — but it now sits in the header's blind-spot list beside the others, with the asymmetry against the `||`-splitting decision spelled out (there over-inclusion was safe; here the end-anchor is the only cheap way to recognise the harmless case at all). The two remaining review notes were the documented `set -uo pipefail`/no-`common.sh` deviations, which the previous round already corrected in the header and the reviewer explicitly did not ask to change. `bash -n` + `shellcheck -S warning` clean on both files.

### Fixed

- **CODEOWNERS assigned no owner to any file — the pattern was `-`, not `*`, so nobody has ever been asked to review a pull request** (`CODEOWNERS`, `agent/hourly-check.sh`, `agent/prompts/hourly.md`, `agent/prompts/github.md`). A CODEOWNERS line is `<pattern> <owner>...`, so `- @owner` made the *pattern* `-` — valid gitignore-style syntax matching a file literally named `-`, of which this repo has none. Every path was ownerless and the `main` ruleset's `require_code_owner_review` had nothing to bind to: across the 100 most recent PRs, **0** had a reviewer auto-requested. Fixed to `* @PavelStancik`. Two deliberate non-changes: `@github-actions` is not restored as an owner (GitHub rejects it — a bot has no account with write access), and `@RobotMarvin2026` is deliberately not an owner, since that would put an agent's sign-off in front of its supervisor's pull requests — a policy call for a human, not a side effect of repairing a pattern. Both stay in a labelled comment block, because `hourly-check` reads this file to decide whose issues it may act on. **Superseded a different resolution that had meanwhile landed on `main` via #995** (`* @pavelstancik @RobotMarvin2026 @github-actions`), which this branch's own reasoning above argues against on both counts: `GET /repos/INFO-WEB-s-r-o/Marvin/codeowners/errors` flags `@github-actions` there as `Unknown owner` — the entry does nothing — and making `@RobotMarvin2026` an owner of `*` grants the bot code-owner approval authority over Pavel's own pull requests, which #933 deliberately declined to do without an explicit human decision. Resolved in this merge by keeping this branch's version.

  **Second defect, from this PR's own review: the code reading that file asked for a path that has never existed.** `.github/CODEOWNERS` 404'd on every hourly run since it was written, and the `|| echo "* PavelStancik"` fallback was dead code — `curl -s` without `-f` exits 0 on an HTTP 404, so the API's error JSON reached the prompt as file content and the agent read `"message": "Not Found"` as "no CODEOWNERS, treat Pavel as sole owner". That cost ~3h and one money-burning issue (#769) on 2026-06-05. All three read sites now point at the root file, and the fetch branches on `%{http_code}`: 200 with content; 404 → absent (the one case the sole-codeowner rule applies); anything else, including a 200 with an empty body, → an explicit `FETCH FAILED — read the local checkout`, so "could not read" no longer collapses into "does not exist". Each state was shown failing before the fix and byte-identical after on the states it must not touch. Full round-by-round detail in PR #933.

  **Third defect, found from #948's sibling in the same script: the rewritten fetch was still untimed.** #948 flagged the missing `--connect-timeout`/`--max-time` on #947's issue-pagination loop; this fetch, two dozen lines away in the same cron-triggered script, had the same gap and neither PR's review had reached it. A stall here is worse than a stall in the issue feed, because this call runs *before* `run_claude`: the hourly check produces nothing at all. Now `--connect-timeout 10 --max-time 20`, matching `lib/github.sh` (#835). No new state was needed — a timeout exits non-zero, becomes `HTTP 000`, and lands in the `*)` arm that already refuses to read "could not read" as "does not exist". **Shown hanging first**, block extracted by marker and driven against a socket that accepts and never answers: pre-fix → **killed at 45s, exit 137**; post-fix → **20s, `HTTP 000`, `FETCH FAILED — could not read CODEOWNERS`**, and specifically *not* the 404 sole-codeowner path. Mirror arm on the live API: HTTP 200, 361 chars, all three owner lines present, sub-second.

- **self-enhance.sh's PR pile-up guard failed open on a missing token or a failed PR fetch** (`agent/self-enhance.sh`) — Issue #996, filed against PR #995. The guard only acted `if github_check_token` succeeded and the PR fetch returned a valid JSON array; either failing silently skipped the whole check and let self-enhancement proceed with the open-PR count unknown — the exact pile-up scenario the guard exists to prevent (#935/#937). Now mirrors `fix-issues.sh`'s guard: a failed token check or a failed/malformed PR fetch logs a WARN and `exit 0`s (skip this run) instead of falling through. **Shown failing first:** stubbing `github_check_token` to fail, and separately stubbing `github_list_prs` to fail, both fall through the guard on `main` today; the same two stubs against this branch stop at the WARN with exit 0. `bash -n` + `shellcheck -S warning` clean.

- **Two producers still documented `/api/logs/` as a served endpoint eleven months after it was denied — and the false rationale was load-bearing on a file permission** (`agent/health-monitor.sh`, `agent/log-analysis.sh`) — comment-only, no executable line changed. `health-monitor.sh` announced "Served at /api/logs/recent.json for dashboard log viewer / search" (written 2026-03-18, `fac3c98`) and `log-analysis.sh` justified a `chmod 644` with "so nginx/dashboard consumers can still fetch /api/logs/analysis-*.json". Both stopped being true when **#861** turned `location /api/` from a denylist into an allowlist (deployed 2026-07-27 05:40Z): the allowlist regex admits `metrics/recent.json` but no `logs/` path at all. **Verified live, not read off the config** — `/api/logs/recent.json` → **403**, `/api/logs/analysis-latest.json` → **403**, against `status.json`, `thoughts.json`, `metrics/recent.json`, `peers/registry.json`, `about.json`, `changelog.json`, `uptime.json`, `enhancements.json` → **200**, which proves the probe distinguishes denied from served rather than failing uniformly. **Nothing is broken:** no dashboard log viewer exists — the frontend fetches only `/api/blog*` and `/api/reports/weekly-card-latest.svg` — and the published `openapi.yaml` never claimed the path, so there is no served-vs-documented drift for #902's §9m to catch. The cost was diagnostic: the hourly 403 in `nginx error.log` is **my own investigative `curl` from a previous hourly run** (04:35Z, 05:38Z, 06:39Z, 07:39Z — each a few minutes into the run above it), so four consecutive hours re-derived the same finding, and each re-derivation logged the entry that prompted the next one. The comments were what made it look unresolved. **The permission is correct and was kept.** `644` is what every file under `data/` carries and `lessons-learned.sh` reads `analysis-latest.json` from disk as a local consumer; only the reason was wrong. Tying it to an HTTP consumer that no longer exists invites both failure directions — "nothing fetches it, so tighten to 600", which breaks the local readers, and "restore the endpoint", which re-publishes internal analysis including attacker IPs. Both comments now name #861, state the 403 as verified, and say explicitly not to close it by adding `logs/` to the allowlist, since publishing all of `data/` under `/api/` is the exact defect #861 was opened to close. **Deliberately not done:** no self-test section asserting "every `/api/` path a producer claims is either reachable or explicitly denied". The guard is worth having, but five open PRs (#925, #912, #911, #902, #894) are concurrently editing `agent/self-test.sh` and `agent/lib/self-test-sections.txt`, and section-letter collisions between open PRs are the direct cause of #889, #907 and #920; a sixth contender is merge risk, not queue depth. `bash -n` clean on both files, and the diff asserted comment-only by filtering the changed lines for any that are not a comment or blank.
- **The same `git merge main` clobber that #889 named on this branch happened again on the next merge — this time to self-test §9k** (`agent/self-test.sh`) — Issue #907, filed by the review bot. The branch reserved letter **9l** on purpose, with a comment recording that #890 held 9k and that open branches had been checked — the trap from #889 taken seriously and written down. Then #890 merged, `git merge main` (24c1bed) brought §9k onto the branch, and the conflict resolution wrote §9l **over** §9k's lines instead of beside them. The reservation was honoured in the letter and lost in the resolution. GitHub rendered it as a single `@@ -1314,59 +1314,61 @@` hunk — a 59-line section replaced by a 61-line one, net **+2 lines**, which is why nothing in the diffstat looked wrong. Confirmed against the revisions rather than the narrative: `_nginx_error_window` appears **5** times on `main` and **0** times at `refs/pull/894/head`; the merge-base is `176ff6e`, the very commit that added §9k, so this is a resolution that deleted it and not a branch cut before it existed. Coverage lost was the ratchet for #860/#866 — the nightly 23:30–00:00 nginx error-log gap, which replaying real logs showed had swallowed a genuine production incident. **The tell was in this file the whole time:** the entry three lines below describes #889 — *"a `git merge main` on this branch deleted self-test §9i … and the CHANGELOG went on advertising it"* — and while that entry sat here, the CHANGELOG went on advertising §9k under the #860 entry above, for a section the branch no longer contained. Twice on one branch, the second time with the diagnosis already written down. §9k restored **verbatim** from `main` (byte-identical to `origin/main:agent/self-test.sh` lines 1486–1539, diffed rather than retyped) and placed above §9l, preserving main's ordering relative to §9j. Merged current `main` in afterwards and verified the section inventory across the merge, which is now cheap because #895 landed §1k — the inventory ratchet built for this exact class, and the thing that would have caught 24c1bed had the branch been merged one commit later. Both **§9k and §9l are now registered in `agent/lib/self-test-sections.txt`** — §9k was missing from it on `main` too (#895's branch was cut before #890 landed), so the manifest's own WARN was naming a section that had, by then, actually gone. Restored §9k **mutation-tested three ways** to prove it came back live rather than merely present: rotated log dropped from the read loop → FAIL naming #860; `UNREAD` notice deleted → FAIL naming #866; function renamed → FAIL saying the check *did not run* rather than reporting clean. Suite on the merged branch: **158 pass, 0 fail**. **The restoration commit also silently dropped the file's executable bit** (`100755` → `100644`), which the review caught and this branch now restores: `8024ed9` rewrote `self-test.sh` whole rather than editing in place, and a fresh write lands at the umask default. Nothing on this box invokes the file as `./agent/self-test.sh` — every call site, including this PR's own evidence, uses `bash agent/self-test.sh` — and `file-integrity.sh` baselines sha256/size/mtime, not mode, so there is no runtime symptom and no integrity alarm; it is a mode change nobody asked for, on the one file in `agent/` that would have arrived on `main` inconsistent with every other script there. Restored with `git add --chmod=+x`, blob hash unchanged (`a5c4a63` both sides), so the commit is mode-only. A section restored to stop a silent deletion, arriving with a silent permission change of its own.
- **UFW findings stopped affecting the security scan's `overall_status` — an inactive firewall and a world-open unexpected port both scored "clean"** (`agent/security-scan.sh`) — Issue #893, filed by the review bot **six minutes after #884 merged**. #884 rewrote the `overall_status` elif chain to add the egress-coverage arm, and in doing so dropped `"$ufw_unexpected_count" -gt 0` and `"$ufw_scan_ok" != true` out of the condition. Nothing else changed: the UFW audit still ran, still logged `WARN`, and still wrote `ufw_audit_ran` / `ufw_firewall_active` / `ufw_unexpected_rules` into the JSON report. Only `overall_status` — which #884's own commentary calls *"the one field the dashboard and incident-report actually read"* — stopped reflecting them. **Restored**, `ufw_unexpected_count` back into the first `elif` and `ufw_scan_ok` as its own arm beside the new `outbound_coverage_status` one, which is where it belongs: `ufw_scan_ok=false` means *ufw absent, `ufw status` unreadable, or the firewall inactive*, i.e. a control that could not look — the same thing the egress arm exists to say, on the ingress side. **Demonstrated behaviourally, both directions**, by extracting the gate block by markers and scoring it under the two scenarios #893 names. Against `main` at `3350be8`: firewall inactive (#881) → `clean`; one unexpected open port (#849) → `clean`. Against this branch: `warnings` and `warnings`, with the all-clean baseline still `clean` in both. Those two scenarios are not hypothetical on this box — #881 *was* an inactive firewall, and #849 was 8042/tcp open to the world with no `EXPECTED_PORTS` entry for **154 days**. **Exposure was bounded by a deploy lag, not by anything we did:** `morning-check.sh` pulls `main` at 06:00 local and #884 merged at 06:10 UTC, so the live tree at `/home/marvin/git` still carries the working gate (verified directly, not inferred from the merge). The regression reaches the running scanner at the next pull unless this lands first. Ratcheted by new self-test §9l (see Added). `bash -n` + `shellcheck -S warning` clean.
- **Self-test §9f passed on comment text — the "strip comments before asserting" rule was written at line 916 and broken at line 923** (`agent/self-test.sh`) — Issue #899. §9f exists to catch the #852 regression where the daily beacon probe forges a peer entry into the negotiate inbox; it grepped the *raw* `network-discovery.sh` for `marvin_health_probe == true`. Line 209 of that file is a comment quoting the marker while explaining the gate at line 219, so the assertion was **already satisfied by prose, today** — delete the runtime gate, keep the sentence describing it, and §9f reports green. §9e's flag check (`grep -q -- "\"--beacon-only\""`) has the same shape and passes against a file that is nothing but comments. Both now scan through a shared `_code_only` helper that blanks full-line comments and **returns non-zero on an unreadable or code-free file**, so "the scan never ran" is reported as a FAIL naming itself rather than collapsing into a pass; callers are documented as required to check its status, since `x=$(_code_only f) || true` reintroduces exactly this bug. Only full-line comments are stripped — a trailing `#` on real code cannot make an absent construct look present, and stripping it correctly needs quote-state parsing, which has already false-FAILed twice here (#875, #887). **Demonstrated failing before shipping**, helper extracted by marker and `bash -n`'d, against `network-discovery.sh` with line 219 deleted and line 209 kept:

  ```
  gate line (code) still present?   0
  comment at ~209 still present?    1
    §9f OLD (raw grep)          => PASS  <-- WRONG: gate is deleted
    §9f NEW (comment-stripped)  => FAIL (correct)

  §9e, against a file that is only a comment mentioning the flag:
    §9e OLD (raw grep)          => PASS  <-- WRONG: no dispatch exists
    §9e NEW                     => FAIL: no code to scan (correct, and named)

  Controls, unmodified network-discovery.sh:
    §9f NEW => PASS   §9e NEW => PASS   (no false alarm on good code)
  Scanner-broke cases: empty file and missing file both return non-zero.
  ```

  Full suite on the branch is identical to the `origin/main` baseline — **161 total / 153 pass / 0 fail / 8 warn** on both sides, with all three affected assertions still passing on the real tree. Third reintroduction of this class by code written to prevent it (#858, #887, #890 §9k); the helper exists so the next author has something to reach for instead of a rule recorded in one comment in the middle of §9f. Scope deliberately limited to the two broken sites: the other `grep -q` assertions against whole config files (`sshd_config`, `ufw`, `dpkg`) and the conflict-marker scan at line 72 are *supposed* to match everywhere, and the sections that already extract a region first (`_bo_block`, `_nl_norm`, `_cwin`) were correct.

- **Review follow-up on the above: `sort -n -u` in the ranker silently deleted a second implicated script whenever two of them tied on character count** (`agent/self-enhance.sh`) — Issue #897, filed by the review bot against PR #896, and correct. `_rank_scripts` emitted `"<size>\t<relpath>"` and closed with `sort -n -u`. With `-n` active and no explicit key, GNU `sort` compares — and therefore dedupes — on the **leading numeric field alone**, not the whole line, so two entirely different scripts with the same `wc -m` count are "duplicates" and the second is dropped: `printf '100\tA.sh\n100\tB.sh\n' | sort -n -u` returns only `A.sh`. The loser never reached the budgeting loop, so it appeared neither in the prompt nor in `_omitted` — that notice is built only from what survives into `_ranked` — leaving nothing in the prompt or the logs to say a failure-implicated script had gone missing. A silent drop, in the block whose stated design principle is *"No silent caps: the failure being fixed is precisely a cap that reported nothing."* Same shape as #858 and #887: the bug class reintroduced by the code written to eliminate it, this time inside the same PR that named it. **Fixed** to `sort -s -t $'\t' -k1,1n` — `-k1,1n` confines the numeric comparison to the size field so the path is no longer invisible to it, `-s` keeps ties in input order so the ranking is reproducible run to run, and `-u` is simply gone: it was never doing useful work, because within one call each basename resolves to at most one real path per candidate directory, and cross-tier repeats are deduped downstream by `awk '!seen[$2]++'` on the full relative path. **Verified by driving the shipped code, old against new.** The function was extracted from both revisions by marker (`awk` range on `^_rank_scripts\(\) \{$`) and `bash -n`'d before sourcing, so these are the real bodies rather than a reimplementation that could agree with itself; the fixture is three implicated scripts, two of them deliberately tied at 100 chars. Old sort: `agent/alpha.sh`, `agent/lib/charlie.sh` — **2 of 3, `bravo.sh` gone.** New sort: all three. The invisibility was reproduced too, by running the real budgeting loop with a budget too small for everything: under the old sort `bravo.sh` appears in neither the included block nor the omitted list — *mentioned nowhere* — while under the new one it is named in `_omitted` (`agent/bravo.sh (100 chars), agent/lib/charlie.sh (200 chars)`). And the downstream dedup was confirmed still intact rather than assumed: with `alpha.sh` implicated in **both** tiers, `_ranked` contains it exactly once under the new sort, as under the old. Worth recording that **no two scripts in `agent/` or `agent/lib/` tie on `wc -m` today** — the trigger is real but unarmed at this moment, which is exactly why it had to be demonstrated on a fixture rather than waited for. `bash -n`, `shellcheck -S warning` (0 warnings) and the repo's own `pipefail-scan.sh` clean on the changed file.
- **Review follow-up on the above: the prompt budget mixed bytes with characters** (`agent/self-enhance.sh`) — Raised as a Suggestion by the review bot on PR #896, and correct. Candidate script sizes came from `wc -c` (**bytes**) but were spent against a budget built from `${#var}`, and `run_claude`'s own ceiling test is `${#full_prompt}` — both of which count **characters** under this host's `C.UTF-8` locale. Not academic: the em-dashes and accented text throughout these scripts make `self-test.sh` 73,503 bytes but 70,667 characters, so `wc -c` overstated every candidate by ~4% and biased toward **refusing a script that would in fact have fitted** — the precise outcome the budget exists to prevent, and the same units-vs-assumptions mismatch the parent fix is about. Switched to `wc -m`, which agrees with `${#var}` under UTF-8 and collapses to the same number under a C/POSIX locale, so it is correct either way rather than locale-dependent; the omission notice's `bytes` label was corrected to `chars` so the message cannot reintroduce the confusion. Re-verified with the same marker-extracted harnesses: selector still yields exactly `agent/self-test.sh` (now 70,667), real assembly **349,480 chars vs the 400,000 cap**, and with the cap at 500,000 the script is still **included** (`1 included, none omitted`). `bash -n`, `shellcheck -S warning` and `pipefail-scan.sh` clean. The review's second Suggestion — that `SELF_CONTEXT_HEAD`/`SELF_CONTEXT_TAIL` remain unbudgeted while the roadmap is 82% of the prompt — is real but is the deferred item already recorded in **Next time** above, not an oversight; taking it here would have been a fourth change.
- **Review follow-up on the above: the last two live review Suggestions — one hardcoded list written twice, one measurable quantity guessed at** (`agent/self-enhance.sh`) — Both raised on PR #896 (the exclusion list twice, by two independent review rounds), both fixed here. **(1) The always-included list existed in two places.** `agent/common.sh` and `agent/lib/github.sh` were literals in the `for script in ...` loop that dumps them in full, and literals again in `_rank_scripts`'s skip test, with nothing tying the copies together. Adding a third always-included script and updating only one copy would have dumped it into the prompt **twice** — spending budget on a duplicate while a genuinely implicated script was refused for want of it. Now one `ALWAYS_INCLUDED_SCRIPTS` array, read by both. **(2) The per-entry overhead was an assumed constant, not a measured one.** The budgeting loop charged `_size + 40` for "~40 chars of heading and fence overhead", but the emitted wrapper is `### ` + the relative path + ` (implicated in today's failures)` + two fenced lines + a trailing blank — **51 chars plus the path length**, i.e. 69 for `agent/self-test.sh` and 71 for `agent/lib/logging.sh`. Every included entry under-charged the budget, and the error grew with the path. Replaced with `${#_entry}` on the assembled entry itself: exactly the unit `run_claude` enforces in, and nothing left to drift. **Verified old against new, on the shipped code.** Both regions extracted from `HEAD` and the working tree by marker (`awk` ranges on `^_rank_scripts\(\) \{` and `^while IFS=$'\t' read -r _size _rel; do`), emptiness-checked and `bash -n`'d before sourcing — an empty or truncated fragment aborts rather than printing a plausible pass table. *Accounting:* two entries admitted under a generous budget — old charges **906** for a block that is actually **955** characters (drift **−49**); new charges **955** for **955** (drift **0**). *Refusal:* one entry with the budget set to `size + 55` — more than the assumed 40, less than the real 69 — old **admits it anyway** and overruns, new **refuses and names it** in `_omitted`. *List:* with a third entry added to `ALWAYS_INCLUDED_SCRIPTS`, old `_rank_scripts` still ranks `agent/lessons-learned.sh` (the duplicate dump), new skips it. *Regression:* ordinary two-script ranking and the cross-tier `awk '!seen[$2]++'` dedup return identically under both. `bash -n` clean. This closes every Suggestion raised on this PR except the unbudgeted `SELF_CONTEXT_HEAD`/`SELF_CONTEXT_TAIL`, which stays deferred in **Next time** above — it is a change to what the prompt contains, not to how this block accounts for itself.
- **Self-enhance's "scripts that had errors today" never once selected a script that had an error, and the 153 KB of unrelated source it pulled in instead pushed the prompt past the ceiling — silently deleting the one script that had actually failed** (`agent/self-enhance.sh`, `agent/lib/claude.sh`) — Found from this morning's own log: `[08:00:05Z] [WARN] Prompt too large (497498 chars) — truncating to 400000`. That truncation is a blunt byte cut inside `run_claude`; it landed mid-`security-scan.sh`, and everything after it — including `self-test.sh`, the only script that had genuinely failed today (`self-test.sh:1279 — command failed (exit 1)`, ×6) — was discarded with nothing but that one WARN to mark it. The session ran against a codebase view it believed was complete. **Root cause, two independent faults in one selector** (`grep -oP '(?<=agent/)[a-z-]+\.sh'` over the whole log): (1) it required a literal `agent/` prefix, but `marvin_error_trap` logs the **basename** (`lib/logging.sh:120` → `self-test.sh:1279`), so no ERR-trap line can satisfy that lookbehind — the selector was structurally incapable of seeing an error, not merely bad at it; (2) it scanned every line at every level, so what it actually matched was whatever else spelled a path that day. **Measured on today's log: it selected `log-export.sh`, `morning-check.sh`, `security-scan.sh`, `self-test.sh` — and zero of the four appear on any WARN/ERROR/CRITICAL line.** All four came from `file-integrity.sh`'s INFO lines (`git-synced — /home/…/agent/<name>.sh`), a monitored-path list unrelated to failure. Each was then captioned `(had errors today)` in the prompt — a false claim asserted to the reader, not a silent miss. Same class as the codified dead-fix-site lesson: a live call site, correct-looking code, and an intent never once honoured since the day it was written. **Fix, three parts.** (1) *Selector* — match basenames on WARN/ERROR/CRITICAL lines only, resolve against real files in `agent/` and `agent/lib/`, and rank ERROR/CRITICAL above WARN so that when the budget binds it drops the least-implicated script rather than the most (priority order: fix failures first — the old behaviour dropped exactly the wrong one). An unreadable log sets `_selector_ran=false` and emits a **SELECTION FAILED** block into the prompt, because a scan that could not run must not render as an empty, all-clear section. (2) *Budget* — assemble the non-negotiable prompt parts first, measure real remaining headroom, and spend it on script bodies deliberately; anything that does not fit is **named in the prompt and in a WARN** (`Prompt budget exhausted — omitted …`) with an instruction to open it with the Read tool. No silent caps: the failure being fixed is precisely a cap that reported nothing. (3) *Single source for the ceiling* — `run_claude`'s `local max_chars=400000` becomes the exported `MARVIN_CLAUDE_MAX_PROMPT_CHARS` (`:=` default, so unset behaves exactly as the hardcoded literal did), so the budgeting caller cannot drift from the enforcing callee; hardcoding `400000` in a second place would have recreated this bug the first time either moved. **Verified by running the real code, not a reimplementation** — the selector block was extracted from the file by marker (`awk` range), `bash -n`'d to prove the extraction is valid bash, then sourced: against today's real log the new selector yields exactly `agent/self-test.sh` where the old yielded four scripts, none of which had errored. The full assembly was then run end-to-end with only the Claude call stubbed: **349,236 chars against the 400,000 cap — no truncation** (was 497,498), with `self-test.sh` correctly reported as omitted-and-named, because today's roadmap alone consumes 284,864 chars. Both other branches were driven rather than argued: with the cap raised to 500,000 the same script is **included** (`1 included, none omitted`, prompt 419,829) — proving the budget admits as well as refuses — and an unreadable log produces the SELECTION FAILED text. `bash -n` and `shellcheck -S warning` clean on both files (0 warnings, matching the `origin/main` baseline); the repo's own `pipefail-scan.sh` reports clean on both; full `agent/self-test.sh` **152 pass / 0 fail / 7 warn, Grade A**, identical to the pre-change baseline. Did **not** run `self-enhance.sh` live — it would spawn a nested Claude run, contend for the lock and mutate the tree; the probe stubbed the Claude call only and left the surrounding assembly untouched. **Next time:** the roadmap is now 284,864 of the 349,236-char prompt — **82%**, and append-only, so it is the next thing to bind the budget and the reason a 73 KB script cannot fit today. Bounding it (keep the unchecked items and recent completed entries, drop the 2026-02/03 tail) would buy back most of that headroom; left out only to hold this session to three changes.
- **The hourly nginx error window dropped 23:30–00:00 every night, and the drop was indistinguishable from a quiet half-hour** (`agent/hourly-check.sh`, `agent/self-test.sh` §9k) — Issue #860, re-cut against current `main` as Pavel asked when closing #862: that PR rewrote the same function #848 had just rewritten, so the two competed rather than composed. This one is a rebase of the gap onto #848's corrected cutoff, which it leaves alone. **The gap is real and still open on `main`:** `find /var/log/nginx -name "error.log"` matches only the live log, logrotate runs `daily` at 00:00 local, and the 00:35 run's 65-minute window opens at 23:30 the previous day — by then that span lives entirely in `error.log.1`. **Demonstrated against the real logs rather than argued.** Replaying the 00:35 window (cutoff `2026/07/26 23:30:00`): `main` returns **12 lines**, this branch returns **14**. The two it recovers are `open() "/home/marvin/git/data/comms/identity.json" failed` at 23:50:26 and 23:53:24 — the public beacon returning 404, a genuine production incident that the nightly gap had swallowed. That is the whole argument for the fix: the lost window was not empty, it just had nobody reading it. **Per-file read status, per #866.** The two logs are read in a loop with *separate* status, not one shared flag: a readable `error.log` must not erase the fact that `error.log.1` could not be opened, or the run prints a confident short window with nothing saying half of it was never read. An unreadable log emits `!! UNREAD: <path> — this window is INCOMPLETE, not clean` instead of being folded into the quiet case. Shown firing by shimming `awk` to exit 2 for `error.log.1` only: `main`'s single pipeline reports **12 lines and no complaint** — it never opens the file, so it cannot notice — while this branch emits the UNREAD marker. `awk` exits 0 on "no matching lines", so a non-zero status here is a read failure and not an empty result. The cutoff is also computed once into a variable before the substitution, so a `date` failure surfaces as an explicit *window NOT read* rather than becoming an empty awk pattern that matches every line ever logged. **Scope kept to `.1` on purpose:** `delaycompress` leaves exactly one rotation uncompressed and a 65-minute window cannot reach past it, so reading further back would cost a `zcat` per run to re-examine entries every window has already excluded. **§9k is source-level, deliberately.** The runtime symptom needs three conditions to coincide — the 00:35 run, on a day something was logged, in that specific half-hour — so a runtime test would report clean almost every time it ran, which is precisely the property that let this survive. Both assertions were shown FAILing against `main`'s `hourly-check.sh` before the fix landed and PASSing against this branch's. **Merge with `main` after #874 and #884 landed, plus a defect in this PR's own §9k found while resolving it.** #884's §9j arrived at the same line range this branch had filled with §9k, so git raised it as one conflict hunk — 140 lines against 32, #889's shape a second time in one morning. Both blocks were spliced verbatim from their own sources rather than from the conflicted file, §9j then §9k, and the merged header set was checked to be exactly the union of the two parents: 33 sections, nothing lost, nothing invented. **The §9k defect:** it resolved the file under test as `${MARVIN_DIR}/agent/hourly-check.sh`, and `common.sh` hardcodes `MARVIN_DIR=/home/marvin/git` — so the section asserted against the *deployed* tree instead of the tree it ships in, which is #855 exactly and #874's §1j the same day. It reports on code the branch is not. **Shown, not argued:** run against this branch as written, §9k emits two FAILs (`hourly-check.sh never mentions error.log.1 …`, `no unread-log reporting …`) because deployed `main` has no fix yet; re-resolved through `dirname "$0"` it emits the two PASSes it was written to emit. The branch's earlier green suite was obtained on a `MARVIN_DIR`-repointed copy, which is precisely what hid this. **Third defect in §9k, raised by both review rounds and only now fixed: the assertions matched the whole file, comments included.** `grep -q 'error\.log\.1' "$_hc"` and `grep -qE 'UNREAD|INCOMPLETE, not clean' "$_hc"` scanned all of `hourly-check.sh` — and the fix's *own* explanatory prose above the function names `error.log.1` (line 44). So a revert that deleted the read loop and left the comment block standing would have kept §9k green: a test asserting that the fix was once *described*, not that it is still wired in. The assertions now run against `_nginx_error_window()`'s body only, extracted by `sed -n '/^_nginx_error_window()[[:space:]]*{/,/^}/p'` with comment lines stripped, and are anchored to the wiring rather than the vocabulary — the `for` line must actually iterate over `error.log.1`, and the notice must be an emitting `printf`. An extraction that comes back empty is an explicit FAIL, not a pair of checks failing for the wrong reason. **Mutation-tested three ways, both revisions extracted from markers rather than reimplemented:** against the intact branch file both revisions PASS twice, so nothing was traded for the tightening; against a fixture whose body is regressed to a single-log shared-flag shape *with the comments kept verbatim*, the old #860 check **PASSes wrongly** while the new one FAILs; and against a fixture where the function is merely renamed, the old section passes **both** checks on a file that no longer contains the function at all — worse than the review described — where the new one FAILs closed on the extraction. `bash -n` and `shellcheck -S warning` clean; full suite 163 total, 155 pass, **0 fail**, 8 warn, security A (95/100).
- **A `git merge main` on this branch deleted self-test §9i — the #877 regression guard — and the CHANGELOG went on advertising it** (`agent/self-test.sh`) — Issue #889, filed by the review bot against this PR. §9i did not exist when this branch was cut: #878 was still open, so the new outbound section was numbered **9j** with a comment reasoning that *"9g/9h/9i are claimed by open PRs #862/#856/#878"*. #878 then merged, landing §9i on `main` **at the same line range** this branch had filled, and the merge at `a91ecb5` resolved that one overlapping hunk in favour of the branch. No conflict was reported and nothing was lost noisily: the 52-line block guarding `--beacon-only`'s discard path simply stopped existing, and merging this PR would have removed it from `main` with no replacement. The Added entry above — *"Self-test §9i … Mutation-tested four ways"* — sat in this same file the whole time, describing a test the branch no longer contained, because prose and code merge independently. **Restored verbatim** from `main`, ahead of §9j; the two do not collide, so nothing needed renumbering and the review's other option (shuffle 9j→9k) was declined as churn. **Re-verified rather than assumed present:** driven standalone against the branch's own `network-discovery.sh` — unmutated → PASS; the early-exit block simplified back to a bare `exit 0` (the exact #877 regression it exists to stop) → FAIL; a second unconditional `BEACON_WRITTEN=true` → FAIL. `bash -n` and `shellcheck -S warning` clean, no duplicate section letters. **The stale rationale is the actual defect and is fixed too:** a section letter chosen against the set of *open PRs* stops being true the moment one of them merges. The comment now reads against `main` (9h/9i live, 9k claimed by the open #890, 9g left free) and records what happened here, so the next author renumbering in this contended file checks the branch they are merging into rather than a snapshot of the queue.
- **The pipefail double-document detector ran away on an apostrophe and failed the suite on a statement that does not exist** (`agent/lib/pipefail-scan.sh`) — Found while verifying #884. `_JOIN_AWK` decided whether a shell statement continued onto the next line by **counting** single-quote characters, excluding only full-line comments. An apostrophe inside a *double*-quoted string — `test_fail "… the day's egress answer (#882)"`, ordinary prose, of which this repo's messages are made — reads as an unterminated single quote, so the joiner swallowed the next 60 lines into one synthetic statement. That sweep pulled in an unrelated `grep … | wc -l` pipeline and an unrelated `|| echo`, which between them satisfied all four flag conditions, and §1h reported `FAIL: … self-test.sh:877` against a line whose only sin was a possessive. A detector for *"a failure that produces a confident-looking wrong answer"*, producing a confident-looking wrong answer — the third time in this file's short history that the bug class has been reintroduced by code written to prevent it. **Fixed by scanning quote state instead of counting it:** single- and double-quote state tracked against each other, backslash escapes honoured only outside single quotes (bash does not escape inside them), and an unquoted `#` treated as a comment only when it begins a word — so a trailing comment is still ignored while `(#882)` is not mistaken for one. **Calibration proven preserved, not asserted:** re-run against the pre-fix revisions of all four historical incidents (`log-alerting.sh` @`54732b4^` 1 hit, `log-export.sh` @`a45aaa0^` 2, `capability-inventory.sh` @`a45aaa0^` 1, `weekly-analytics.sh` @`e87cc80^` 2) — identical counts to the old scanner, and still clean against every fixed version. Whole-tree diff old vs new is exactly one line: the false positive, removed. Suite back to **0 fail**. Same class as the §1i scanner's naive paren counting raised in #875, one scanner over. **Third review round widened the word-start test:** bash opens a comment after an unquoted `;`, `|`, `&`, `(` or `)` as well as after whitespace, so `false;# it's fine` still flipped quote state on the apostrophe — #887 one delimiter over. `{` is deliberately excluded so `${#arr[@]}` is not read as a comment. This file is now byte-identical to the copy on #888, which is what makes the two PRs merge-order independent as both bodies claim; they had silently diverged when #888 took the fix and this branch did not. **The joiner now asserts itself on every run — shipped in this file but left undocumented until now (#888).** `_joiner_selfcheck()` runs unconditionally at script load, before any real file is read: the joiner is fed a fixture and its output compared against hand-written expectations, and a mismatch **exits 2** — the existing "could not scan" code — rather than scanning on with a broken parser and reporting whatever falls out. It lives in the scanner rather than in a new `self-test.sh` section on purpose: §1h already invokes the scanner every run and already FAILs on exit >1, so the guard inherits a live caller instead of needing a section letter in a file four open PRs are editing — and it also fires for standalone runs, which is how this scanner actually gets calibrated. **Fixtures pin both directions,** because a guard that only asserts "does not join" passes trivially in a joiner that never joins anything: the negative is #887 itself, the positives are a genuine multi-line single-quoted `awk` program and a backslash continuation that must still join. Expectations are hand-written — comparing the joiner against a second copy of itself would agree perfectly and prove nothing. **Shown failing against the shipped copy:** with `sq_open()`'s result forced to 0 so the joiner never joins, `main`'s own scanner exits 2 with `first mismatch at joined-output line 3 … expected: 3:crontab -l | awk ' { print $1 } ' | wc -l … got: 3:crontab -l | awk '`.

- **The outbound connection audit looked once a day, at the deadest minute, and called it the day's answer — zero connections reported on 30 of 31 retained scans** (`agent/lib/outbound.sh` (new), `agent/security-scan.sh` §3d, `agent/health-monitor.sh`, `agent/common.sh`, `agent/self-test.sh` §9j) — Issue #882. `security-scan.sh` §3d is the one control on this box designed to answer *"what did this server talk to"*. It ran from `/etc/cron.d/marvin` at `0 4 * * *` local — 02:00 UTC, the quietest minute here — took a **single instantaneous `ss` sample**, and wrote the result out as the day's egress record. `outbound-audit.json` was one overwritten file with no history, and `scan-*.json` retained only the two scalars, never the connection list. **The logic was never broken.** Extracted and run unmodified it parses `ss` correctly, the loopback and docker-bridge filters behave, the counter increments; run live during this work it found 24 non-loopback connections. It was honest and useless: between samples it saw nothing, and "the sampler never looked" and "nothing left the box" were the same output. The trigger was a 19.5σ anomaly — **704 MB outbound on 2026-07-26** against a 21-day range of 67–145 MB — for which this control had no record and never could have had one. The probable cause is benign (Claude runtime 26,655s that day against ~490s on the 22nd–24th) but it was inferred from `claude-usage-*.jsonl`, an operational metric with no security purpose. A busy day and a bad day were indistinguishable. **Diagnosis was tested, not assumed:** `network.established_connections` was 8 on days §3d reported 0, which is equally consistent with a *broken filter* — resolved by classifying a live snapshot (38 established: 14 loopback, 24 not) and confirming the 02:00 sample genuinely sees loopback-only traffic (nginx↔next.js:3000, rspamd↔redis:6379), so the sampling window is the defect and the filters are sound. **Fix — split sampling from aggregation.** Sampling moves to the existing 5-minute `health-monitor.sh` cron tick (288 samples/day instead of 1), appending one JSON line per tick to `data/security/outbound-samples-YYYY-MM-DD.jsonl`, retained 30 days and pruned by mtime. §3d now **aggregates** that history for the trailing complete UTC day — destinations, processes, peak concurrency, unexpected ports — and reports the partial day-so-far separately against an elapsed-ticks expectation rather than a 288 target it cannot meet at 02:00. The instantaneous sample is retained (it is still a real observation and existing consumers read `outbound_total`/`outbound_unexpected`) but is now labelled `"outbound_sample_scope": "instantaneous"` with a `sampling_note` telling readers which field is the day and to check coverage before believing any count. **The coverage gate is the actual fix.** The aggregate reports what was observed and *whether it looked* as two independent claims that never collapse: `coverage_status` is `ok` / `degraded` / `absent` / `aggregate-failed`, and a day with no samples reports `absent` with `total_observations: null` — not `0`. Non-`ok` coverage **flips `overall_status` to `warnings`**, so a scan that cannot speak to egress can no longer score clean; sampler errors are written into the JSONL as `{"error":…}` records and counted as `samples_failed`, so a broken sampler is visible as a gap instead of a quiet box; and the `health-monitor.sh` call is deliberately **not** `|| true`-suppressed. This is the same defect class as #881 (an inactive firewall reading as verified-clean) and #873 (a producer failing inside `< <(...)` yielding zero iterations and a clean exit) — a check reporting clean because it never ran. **One classifier, not two.** `_docker_bridges` / `_ip_in_docker_cidr` moved out of `security-scan.sh` into the new lib: the sampler and the aggregator must agree on what "outbound" means, and two copies of that filter would drift into confidently-wrong egress numbers, which is worse than none. The docker probe is lazy, so sourcing the lib from `common.sh` into ~20 agent scripts costs nothing; the redundant `[[ -n "$_docker_bridges" ]]` guards at both call sites are gone (the function self-initialises and returns 1 when no bridges exist). Verified against real bridge detection (172.17/18/19 all found) that container traffic to 4317/3100 yields **`unexpected: 0`**, so the new `overall_status` input does not warn every day on its own bridge traffic. **A latent bug fixed in passing:** the old loopback filter was `case … in 127.*|::1|0.0.0.0)`, but `ss` prints IPv6 peers in bracket form (`[::1]:6379`), so the bare `::1` arm never matched a real line and IPv6 loopback was counted as outbound. Demonstrated with a side-by-side negative control — the old pattern returns `COUNTED-AS-OUTBOUND` for `[::1]`, the new one excludes it. **Sample files are `640`, not the `644` the rest of `data/security/` uses** — deliberately stricter than its peers, because a per-5-minute log of every destination this host contacted is not something to publish, and until the `/api/` allowlist in #861 lands everything under `data/` is HTTP-reachable. `outbound-audit.json` tightened to `640` for the same reason. **Verification — every guard demonstrated FAILING against the state it claims to detect, none argued.** Library level, 27 assertions: the classifier against a synthetic `ss` fixture with a spelled-out expected answer (3 outbound of 8 rows; IPv6 outbound kept, `[::1]` excluded, bridge `:3100` excluded, inbound `:443`/`:22` excluded); **no samples → `absent` with `total_observations: null`**; **one sample for the day — precisely the old control's rate — → `degraded`, not blessed**; 288/288 → `ok`, 100%, 576 observations, peak 2; error records → `samples_failed: 2` and excluded from `samples_recorded`; port 4444 flagged while safe-port traffic is not; corrupt JSONL → `aggregate-failed`, never a clean empty day; live round-trip → exit 0, valid JSON, mode 640. §3d integration, 23 assertions across 5 tree states, driven through the real code extracted **by marker with the fragment `bash -n`-checked first** (a line-range slice goes stale from one's own added comments and then prints a full plausible pass table from a truncated fragment — that happened on 2026-07-26 and the harness now refuses a short or unparseable extraction): no history → `absent`, WARN logged, **gate `warnings`**, file mode 640; one sample → `degraded`, gate `warnings`; full clean day → `ok`, gate `clean` (the only state permitted to read clean); full day with a `:4444` destination → surfaced, WARN, gate `warnings`; 200 good + 5 error samples → `samples_failed: 5`, `samples_recorded: 200`, gap logged. §9j itself, 13 assertions against **mutated copies of the tree**: sampler call deleted → FAIL, `|| true` added → FAIL, `marvin_outbound_day_summary` removed → FAIL, the coverage arm cut out of the `overall_status` gate → FAIL, a second `_ip_in_docker_cidr` appended → FAIL, zero samples with the sampler wired → FAIL, lib absent → **WARN not FAIL** (self-activating, so this branch does not turn a pre-deploy host red). Full `agent/self-test.sh` from the branch against the live tree: **156 total, 149 pass, 0 fail, 7 warn**, security 95/100 grade A — §9j contributing the expected `not deployed yet` WARN, which confirms the undeployed path degrades gracefully on the real host rather than failing. `bash -n` on all four modified scripts and `shellcheck -S error` on the new lib, clean. **Deliberately not done:** `/etc/cron.d/marvin` is untouched — the sampler hooks a tick that already runs every 5 minutes as root, so this needs no live cron edit and no deploy step beyond the merge. Per-destination egress *volume* baselining, the fourth item in #882's proposal, is not here: `ss` gives connection state, not bytes, so attributing 704 MB to a destination needs `nftables` counters or conntrack accounting — a different and more invasive mechanism, and worth its own change rather than being smuggled into this one. What this delivers is the attribution history that made that question unanswerable, plus the guarantee that its absence can never again read as clean. **Review follow-up (#885, filed against this PR and fixed here) — the permissions change had a permissions hole in it.** All five write sites created their file with a redirection and tightened it with a *later* `chmod 640`: `>> "$sample_file"` (the success path plus all three error-record paths) and `cat > "$OUTBOUND_FILE"`. A missing file is created at the process umask — **644 for root** — so between creation and `chmod` the file was world-readable, and `cat >` on an already-644 destination held the *new* content world-readable for the whole write. Since the stated reason for using 640 at all is that everything under `data/` is HTTP-reachable until #861 lands, that window was genuinely reachable by a request: a gap in exactly the guarantee this change exists to add. The repo had already solved this once — #636 in `file-integrity.sh` restricts the temp file **before** the `mv` rather than the destination after it, with a standing comment saying why — and this code did not follow it. Fixed on both paths: sample files are now created by `_marvin_outbound_ensure_file`, which does `( umask 027; : > "$f" )` so the path never exists at a looser mode (666 & ~027 = 640), called **before** the `command -v ss` check so the error-record paths are covered too; and `outbound-audit.json` is written to a `mktemp`'d file that is `chmod 640`'d and `jq`-validated before an atomic `mv`, which closes a second defect nobody filed — `cat >` truncates before it writes, so a reader arriving mid-write got a partial document, and a malformed document overwrote a good one. The trailing `chmod` calls are kept but demoted: their only remaining job is tightening a file left at 644 by an older version of this code. **Verified with a negative control, 13 assertions:** the old append-then-chmod pattern is shown creating at **644** and only reaching 640 after the second statement (the window, reproduced rather than argued), against the new path creating at **640** and staying there through append, idempotent re-entry, the full sampler round-trip, both error paths, and a pre-existing 644 file still being tightened. The `ss`-absent case is reproduced **honestly** — a sandbox `PATH` symlinking every tool the function needs *except* `ss`; the first version of that test just set `PATH=/nonexistent`, which also removed `date`, `mkdir` and `jq`, so the function could not build a filename or write anything, the file was legitimately missing, and the row failed for a reason with nothing to do with permissions. The malformed-document branch was driven separately: previous document intact, mode still 640, no temp file left behind. All three original harnesses re-run green after the change (27 + 23 + 13). **Second review follow-up (#886, filed against this PR and fixed here) — an octal crash that would have taken the whole daily security scan down, and the review understated it.** The partial-day expectation was `$(( $(date -u +%H) * 60 + $(date -u +%M) ))`. `date` zero-pads, bash reads a leading-zero literal as octal, and `08`/`09` are not octal digits: `bash: 08: value too great for base`. Under this file's `set -euo pipefail` and ERR trap that aborts **`security-scan.sh` entirely** — rkhunter, chkrootkit, file integrity, the report, all of it — not merely §3d. Galling detail: the identical computation was written *correctly* in the same PR in `self-test.sh` §9j, with the `10#` prefix. I fixed the class in one file and shipped it broken in the other, an hour apart. **The review judged the scheduled 02:00 run safe because minute `00` is valid octal; that is wrong, and the correction makes this a live fault rather than an ad-hoc-only one.** §3d does not execute at 02:00 — rkhunter takes ~4m16s, so it runs at **02:04**, measured at 02:04:17–02:04:21 across 2026-07-22/24/26. The production cron run was **four minutes of rkhunter drift** from aborting the entire daily audit, and rkhunter's runtime is not fixed. **Fixed with a single epoch reading**, `$(( ($(date -u +%s) % 86400) / 60 ))`, in `security-scan.sh` *and* in §9j — not the `10#` prefix the issue suggested. `10#` fixes the crash but leaves a second, quieter defect: two separate `date` calls can straddle a minute boundary, so reading `%H` at 02:59:59.99 and `%M` a millisecond later yields hour 2 with minute 0 — 120 instead of 180, wrong rather than loud. One reading cannot disagree with itself. **Verified, 13 assertions, under a faithful `set -euo pipefail` + ERR-trap harness** (without the trap the old expression merely exits non-zero, which understates the blast radius): the old expression **aborts at 08:30, 09:30, 02:08 and 02:09**, and across a 216-point sweep of the day it aborted at **62 of them** — roughly 29% of all clock times, not the narrow window the issue described. The new expression aborts at none, agrees with the old one at every time the old one survived, and gives 0 / 287 / 24 ticks at 00:00 / 23:59 / 02:04. The live demonstration is the one worth keeping: at the moment of the fix — **01:08 UTC** — the old line died on the spot (`error token is "08"`) while the new one returned 13. All four harnesses green afterwards (27 + 23 + 13 + 13). **Third review round — four hardening items, one of which turned out to be a real format defect.** ① **The `.jsonl` file was never line-delimited.** The sample writer's `jq` call was missing `-c`, so every record was pretty-printed: the first live sample this branch took measured `wc -l` **31** against `jq -s 'length'` **1**. `jq -s` in the aggregator tolerated it, which is why the format was wrong for the branch's whole life with nothing complaining — and it made the truncation-resilience below useless in practice, since no line-oriented reader could bound a 31-line record. Found by running the sampler live rather than by reading the diff. ② **One malformed line no longer discards the day.** `jq -s` parses the file as a single document, so a process killed mid-append cost all 287 good samples: demonstrated with a 5-sample day (3 good, 1 error record, 1 truncated) where the old form recovers **0** and reports no unexpected destinations, hiding a live `9.9.9.9:4444`, while the new form recovers **3**, counts the rest as `samples_malformed`, and still surfaces the unexpected destination.
