Normalisation is two passes, cheapest first: `jq -c .` over the file (format-agnostic, so legacy pretty-printed files still inside the 30-day retention window aggregate correctly — verified against the real one), falling back to per-line `fromjson?` salvage only when the file is genuinely corrupt. New `samples_malformed` field, WARNed by §3d: a skipped record that nobody announces is a silent under-count. ③ **`coverage_status` gained `failing` and `path-unresolved`.** A day whose every record is an error or unparseable used to report `absent` — *"no samples retained, is health-monitor running?"* — which sends the next debugging session to the wrong place; the sampler plainly ran and produced nothing usable. §3d also gained a `*)` arm so an unrecognised status can never pass through the log in silence. ④ **`_MARVIN_OUTBOUND_DIR` was a source-time assignment whose `${SECURITY_DIR:-…}` head could never fire** — this lib is sourced from `common.sh:244` and every caller sets `SECURITY_DIR` *after* sourcing, so the override silently did nothing and the chain fell to a hardcoded `/home/marvin/git/data` against the repo's derive-from-`${MARVIN_DIR}` rule. Now a function resolved at call time, honouring `SECURITY_DIR` → `DATA_DIR` → `${MARVIN_DIR}`, returning non-zero rather than guessing. ⑤ `_marvin_outbound_ensure_file` now tightens an **existing** 644 file before the append, not only after it — the umask closed the creation race but a file restored from backup reproduced #885's window on every tick (measured: 644 before, 640 after). ⑥ Recorded what the review asked to be recorded rather than assumed: the docker-bridge probe costs **0.12–0.19s wall / ~0.11s CPU** for 5 networks, so 288 ticks/day is ~35s wall on a box idling at 4%; and the positional `ss` parsing is pinned to **iproute2-6.1.0**, with a note that the `State` column is omitted under `state established` — which is what makes `$3`/`$4` correct rather than an off-by-one. **Fourth review round — §9j resolved the sample path by hand.** `_ob_today`/`_ob_yday` rebuilt `${DATA_DIR}/security/outbound-samples-<day>.jsonl` inline instead of calling `marvin_outbound_sample_file()`, re-introducing for the *path* exactly the two-copies-must-agree risk this PR removed for the *classifier*. The agreement was incidental, not designed: `SECURITY_DIR` is unset at that point in `self-test.sh` (it is assigned ~440 lines later), so `_marvin_outbound_dir()` happened to land on the same string. **Demonstrated failing in both directions before the fix, against the real §9j code path in a scratch tree with `MARVIN_DIR` repointed** — with the library resolving to a different directory: (A) samples only at the *hardcoded* path → pre-fix `PASS: 1 samples recorded today`, a confident pass read off a file the sampler no longer writes, while the fixed form correctly `FAIL`s; (B) samples only at the *library* path → pre-fix `FAIL: … produced ZERO samples today`, a false alarm against a healthy sampler, fixed form `PASS`. **And the unresolvable case no longer reports a finding:** an empty path would have failed both `-f` tests and landed on the `ZERO samples` FAIL — a check that never ran publishing a definite verdict, the same collapse `coverage_status` exists to prevent. It now WARNs *NOT verified* instead; injecting a `_marvin_outbound_dir()` that returns 1 shows pre-fix `FAIL` vs fixed `WARN: could not resolve the sample file path` (C). That branch is unreachable while `common.sh` sets `MARVIN_DIR` unconditionally — it is a guard against the fallback chain changing, not a live path, and is recorded as such rather than presented as covered ground.

- **UFW has permitted the whole internet in on 8042/tcp since install, on both address families, for a service that was never built** (`setup/bootstrap.sh`, `agent/network-discovery.sh`, `agent/security-scan.sh`, `docs/wiki/`, `agent/prompts/negotiate.md`) — Issue #849. `ufw allow 8042/tcp` was created at bootstrap for a "Marvin AI comm port" that never got written: `ss -tln` shows nothing on 8042 on any interface and no systemd unit references it — only `marvin-negotiate` (8043, loopback) and `marvin-web` (3000, loopback) exist. **The arrangement was exactly inverted.** The firewall let the world in, while `EXPECTED_PORTS` in `security-scan.sh` was configured to raise an alarm the moment anything *answered* there. Meanwhile the beacon advertised `"comm_port": 8042` to every peer and scanner that read it, and `negotiate.md` offered `binary-protocol` as "Raw TCP on port 8042" — so every peer that ever tried that option got `Connection refused` from a port that was documented, firewalled open, and empty. **Severity is low and worth stating plainly:** you cannot exploit a closed socket. This was not a live hole, it was a pre-authorised one — an ingress rule sitting ready for whatever binds that port next, on a box that installs things unattended. **Fix:** removed the `ufw allow` from `bootstrap.sh` so a rebuild cannot recreate it; dropped `comm_port` from both beacon producers (`bootstrap.sh` and `network-discovery.sh` — it was **write-only**, two producers and zero readers anywhere in the tree, while `negotiate_url` is the channel that actually answers); and corrected the three places that advertised the port. **The part worth having is the third one, which the issue called correctly:** `security-scan.sh` only ever looked for *listeners without permission*. It was blind to *permission without a listener*, which is why this sat unnoticed for 154 days. Added the inverse check — any numeric UFW allow rule with no `EXPECTED_PORTS` entry. Keyed off `EXPECTED_PORTS` rather than off what happens to be listening at that instant, so a service that is merely restarting is not a finding and the check does not flap. App-profile rules (`OpenSSH`) are skipped as names rather than numbers, and the count of skipped profiles is printed rather than silently dropped. A `ufw status` that fails or comes back empty logs **"could not run — ingress rules NOT verified"**, deliberately distinct from finding nothing: `x=$(scan) || true` collapses "the scanner broke" into "the scanner is clean", and this check exists precisely because an absence went unnoticed for five months. **Verification — the detector was demonstrated failing against the real firewall, not a mock.** Six controls: (A) today's live `ufw status` → **flags 8042 and nothing else**, v4 and v6 collapsed to one finding, 1 app-profile rule reported skipped; (B) same table with the 8042 rules removed → silent; (C) `ufw status` returning non-zero → *could not run*, **not** clean; (D) `ufw` absent from `PATH` → *could not run*; (E) two synthetic new rules (`9999/tcp`, `6000/udp`) → both caught, so it covers UDP and future additions rather than just the one port that prompted it; (F) empty `ufw` output → *could not run*. No ERR trap fired in any path under `set -euo pipefail`. Beacon generation re-validated after the `comm_port` removal — the rendered document is valid JSON with the negotiate gate both **open and closed**, and `has("comm_port")` is false. `bash -n` + `shellcheck -S warning -x` clean on all three scripts. **Deliberately not done:** the live `ufw delete allow 8042/tcp` (v4 + v6) was **not** run. Changing firewall rules on a box that cannot be rebooted into recovery is the human's call, and #849 itself rates this low and not a live hole — so the rule stands until Pavel removes it, and the new check will keep reporting it every scan until then, which is the correct behaviour rather than an annoyance. **Three review findings on the first push, all real, all fixed here.** ① **#881 — an inactive firewall was indistinguishable from a verified-clean audit.** `ufw status` on a disabled firewall prints `Status: inactive` and *no* `ALLOW` lines, so the rule loop found nothing and the audit said clean for a host with no packet filtering at all. That is the exact silent-absence shape this check was written to close, reproduced inside the check itself — an empty result meaning both "nothing to permit" and "everything is permitted". Now read *before* the rules are parsed: inactive logs `CRITICAL` and sets the audit to not-verified. Negative control against the pre-fix block on the same fixture: **silent, `ran=true`** — clean, exactly as reported. ② **Multi-port and range rules were silently mislabelled.** `80,443/tcp` and `6000:6002/tcp` failed the single-port regex, fell to the `else`, and were counted as benign "app-profile rules" — a genuinely unexpected opening could hide behind a skip count. The protocol suffix is now stripped once and multi-port/range specs expanded (ranges to their endpoints, since enumerating 10k ports into a log is not reporting). Pre-fix control: `count=0 skipped=3`; now `count=2 skipped=1`, with only `OpenSSH` skipped. ③ **#880 — the findings never left `marvin_log`.** They now gate `overall_status` and appear in the JSON report as `ufw_audit_ran`, `ufw_firewall_active`, `ufw_unexpected_rules`, `ufw_unexpected_rule_list`, `ufw_profile_rules_skipped`. `ufw_scan_ok != true` is itself a warning: an audit that could not run must not leave the one field the dashboard and incident-report actually read sitting at `clean`. The review also caught the PR description overclaiming completeness — three tracked files still referenced the port. `web/public/.well-known/ai-managed.json` (a stale orphan fixture, not served in production, but making a now-false claim) had `comm_port` removed; `log-watcher.sh`'s `:8042` interest pattern and `log-analysis.md`'s port-8042 signal were **kept deliberately** and annotated with why — with nothing listening and nothing advertising it, a hit there is *more* anomalous than before, not less. Eight controls in total after the fixes: live firewall, 8042-removed, new tcp+udp rules, empty output, `ufw` non-zero, `ufw` absent, inactive firewall, multi-port/range. **Second review round, two more findings, both real.** ④ **`ufw_firewall_active` defaulted to the reassuring value.** It was initialised `true`, so the three paths where the audit never ran at all — `ufw` absent from `PATH`, `ufw status` non-zero, empty output — published `"ufw_firewall_active": true` into the JSON report. A consumer reading that field without also reading `ufw_audit_ran` would see a firewall confirmed up on a host where nothing had been checked: the same silent-absence shape as #881, one field over, introduced by the fix *for* #881. Now initialised `false` — `true` on that field means "observed `Status: active`", never "nothing has said otherwise". Demonstrated against the pushed head before changing it: `absent`, `failing` and `empty` all read `ufw_firewall_active=true` before and `false` after, while the two genuinely-active fixtures stayed `true` and still flagged 8042, so the fix moves exactly the three broken cases and nothing else. ⑤ **A real gap documented rather than papered over:** range rules (`6000:6010/tcp`) are checked at their endpoints only, so a range whose two endpoints both appear in `EXPECTED_PORTS` passes silently even if every interior port is unexpected — reproduced (`unexpected_count=0` for exactly that fixture) and stated in the code so a later reader does not mistake it for full coverage. **Worth recording separately: the verification harness broke and reported anyway.** It sliced the block out of the script by hardcoded line numbers, so the moment this round's added comments shifted those lines it `eval`'d a truncated fragment, printed uniform plausible-looking values for all five scenarios, and exited clean — an instrument that agreed with whatever it was pointed at, which is the failure this PR keeps finding in its own work. Caught only because the "after" numbers were identical across scenarios that should have differed. Rewritten to extract by markers and to `bash -n` the extraction *before* trusting any result, exiting `3` with `EXTRACTION-BROKEN` otherwise; that guard was itself demonstrated firing against a deliberately truncated copy. **Next time:** `EXPECTED_PORTS` and the UFW rule set are now cross-checked in one direction each, but both are still hand-maintained lists in a shell script; the port table in `docs/wiki/Security-Hardening.md` is a third copy that nothing compares against either.
- **`network-discovery.sh --beacon-only` reported success after discarding the beacon it was called to create** (`agent/network-discovery.sh`) — Issue #877, a follow-up defect in the recovery path added by #851. The beacon write validates the generated document and, if it fails `jq empty`, deletes the temp file and keeps the previous copy. Both branches then fell through to the same unconditional `exit 0`. **Why that one caller makes it matter:** `morning-check.sh:177` invokes `--beacon-only` **only when `identity.json` is already missing** after a git sync, guarded by `|| marvin_log WARN "Beacon regeneration failed"`. On the discard path in that exact scenario there is no "previous copy" to keep — the file stays missing — yet the script returned 0, so the `||` never fired and the caller recorded a successful recovery over a beacon that does not exist. The only trace was one `marvin_log ERROR` line buried in the run output: a document quietly absent while everything reports success, which is the shape §9e was written to catch, reappearing one layer up inside the recovery path meant to prevent it. **Fix:** a `BEACON_WRITTEN` flag set only on the `mv` path; `--beacon-only` exits 1 when it is not set. Keyed on **whether the write was discarded**, not on whether a file happens to exist on disk — a stale-but-valid `identity.json` left in place is precisely the 109-day freeze, and "an old copy is still there" must not be reported as success. Scope deliberately limited to `--beacon-only`: the full 18:00 discovery run still continues past a failed beacon write, since sections 3–5 are independent of it and making the whole run fail is a behaviour change no issue asked for. **Verified** in a sandboxed tree (`COMMS_DIR` repointed, so the live comms log and beacon were untouched) with the validation forced to fail: on `main` → **exit 0 with no `identity.json` on disk**, the caller's `||` silently skipped; with the fix → **exit 1**, same state. Positive control on the unmodified happy path → exit 0, `identity.json` present and valid JSON, negotiate gate open on a live 200 + probe marker. `bash -n` clean.

- **`weekly-analytics.sh`: two more `pipefail` double-JSON-document sites, one of them a live crash** (`agent/weekly-analytics.sh`) — Found by building the structural detector that #844's review asked for as a regression test, then running it across all of `agent/`. The detector reproduces every known instance of this class (`log-alerting.sh` pre-#843; `log-export.sh` ×2 and `capability-inventory.sh` pre-#844) with **no false positives** on the current tree — and immediately surfaced these two, which the manual sweep behind #844 had missed. **The gap:** ① `_log_stats()` truncated its error histogram with `… | sort -rn | head -5 | awk … | jq -s '.' 2>/dev/null || echo '[]'`. `head` exits as soon as it has its five lines and kills `sort -rn` with **SIGPIPE** once the sorted output exceeds the pipe buffer (~64 KiB — i.e. a week with many *distinct* error messages). Under `pipefail` the pipeline then reports **141** *after* `jq -s` has already printed a valid array, so the `|| echo '[]'` fallback **appends** a second document; that two-document value is handed to `--argjson top_errors`, where `jq` exits **2** and the `ERR` trap takes the whole weekly run down. Confirmed by reproduction: 300k distinct lines → pipeline exit 141, captured value holds 2 documents, `--argjson` fails exit 2. This is a real crash rather than a latent one — it is merely gated on a noisy week, and the current week has only 4 distinct errors. ② `_claude_usage()` ended `cat "${files[@]}" | jq -s '…' || echo '{"total_runs":0}'`; an unreadable metrics file makes `cat` exit non-zero after `jq -s` has printed a valid object, appending a second document (genuinely latent — the file list is existence-checked, so it needs a permissions fault). ③ **A third bug in the same statement, found while addressing this PR's review:** `_log_stats()` hand-built its JSON in `awk` with `printf "{\"count\":%d,\"message\":\"%s\"}"`, which escapes **nothing**. A single error message containing a `"` therefore produced invalid JSON, `jq -s` failed to parse, and the fallback then blanked the **entire** top-error list — not merely the offending line; a message containing a backslash sequence such as `C:\temp` parsed as *valid* but silently corrupted (`\t` → a literal tab). Log lines quote upstream hostnames and paths routinely, so this is not exotic; it is also strictly pre-existing, but ① and ② converted its symptom from a double-document crash into silent total data loss, which is why it belongs in this PR. Confirmed by driving the **real** `_log_stats()` against a log dir holding one quoted and one backslashed message: pre-fix `top_errors` came back `[]` with all three errors lost; post-fix all three survive, correctly escaped. **Fix (1 file):** ① truncate in `awk` via `NR<=5` and drop `head` entirely, removing the SIGPIPE at its source rather than papering over the exit code — `awk` reads to EOF regardless of `NR`, so no upstream stage can be signalled — plus an assignment-form fallback so it replaces instead of appends. (Truncating in `awk` rather than the initially-proposed `jq '.[0:5]'` also answers the review's 🟡 performance note: `jq -s` slurps 5 lines again, not the whole distinct-error list.) ③ emit `count \t message` and let **`jq -R -s`** do the string encoding, so quotes and backslashes can no longer yield invalid or corrupted JSON — the tab framing is unambiguous because `awk`'s `$1=""` forces a `$0` rebuild on `OFS`, so an emitted message can never itself contain a tab; ② capture-then-emit (`if _usage=$(…); then echo "$_usage"; else …`), with the fallback widened to the full metric shape already used by the function's own empty-input early return, so a fallback can't hand downstream consumers an object missing `by_task`/`error_rate_pct`. **Verification:** `bash -n`, `shellcheck -S error` and `shellcheck -S warning` clean; detector clean on the fixed file; all three failure paths re-run post-fix — the 300k-distinct-line SIGPIPE case now exits **0** with a single document, exactly 5 entries and `--argjson` OK (control: the old `head -5` form still exits **141** on identical input), and the quoted/backslashed-message case returns all entries correctly escaped where the pre-fix code returned `[]`; empty input still yields `[]` at exit 0; full end-to-end `weekly-analytics.sh` run exits **0**, emitting valid JSON with `top_errors` (4 real entries) and `claude_api` (`by_task`, `error_rate_pct` present) populated for both weeks. Only `agent/weekly-analytics.sh` changed. **Next time:** the detector itself is the durable fix and lands **separately** — it cannot be committed to `self-test.sh` yet, because three of the five sites it flags are precisely the ones #844 is still waiting to fix on `main`; it goes in as a follow-up once #844 merges. **Second review round:** the one remaining 🟡 asked for the `_claude_usage()` fallback's all-or-nothing behaviour to be written down rather than changed — if one of the week's files becomes unreadable mid-`cat`, the readable days are discarded and the week reports zeros. Documented as the deliberate choice it is: the consumers are trend comparisons (the WoW deltas, the report card), where a silently *partial* week reads as a genuine drop in activity, which is a worse output than an obvious zero. Also confirmed the comment's factual claim rather than asserting it — the fallback object is byte-identical to the zero-files early return 45 lines above, so both no-data paths hand downstream the same shape. Comment only; `bash -n` + `shellcheck -S warning` clean. The detector follow-up referenced above is now open as **#857** (issue #855), carrying a baseline so it can land before #844/#846 merge instead of after. **Follow-up from the 12:28Z re-review — the drift it warned about had already happened.** The review suggested collapsing the two hand-written fallback literals into a `_zero_claude_usage()` helper, on the reasoning that a *future* field added to one and not the other would silently reintroduce the "fallback missing a field" problem. Checked before writing the helper, and the fallbacks were already missing two: the jq success object emits `total_prompt_chars` and `total_output_chars`, and neither literal carried them. A consumer reading either field off a fallback result got `null`, not `0`. **This is the same bug this PR is about, sitting inside the fix for it.** Worse, the entry above records me *verifying* the fallbacks — "byte-identical to the zero-files early return 45 lines above, so both no-data paths hand downstream the same shape". That was true and it was the wrong invariant: I compared the two copies to each other and never to the object they stand in for. Two identical wrong shapes agree perfectly. **Fix:** both literals collapsed into `_zero_claude_usage()`, with the two missing fields added, so the failure path emits the same 8 keys as the success path. **Ratchet — self-test §1e:** asserts the two key sets are equal, by extracting `_dates_in_range` / `_zero_claude_usage` / `_claude_usage` from the script and running `_claude_usage` against a synthetic one-row `claude-usage-*.jsonl` in a temp `METRICS_DIR`. Extraction rather than sourcing, because the script runs its whole pipeline at import; resolved via `dirname "$0"` and **not** `MARVIN_DIR`, since a branch-authored test that resolves through `MARVIN_DIR` asserts against the deployed `main` copy and would pass while the branch regressed. "Could not run" exits 3 and reports `test_warn`, kept distinct from a pass — the #858 lesson, one section over. **Verification:** §1e passes against the fixed helper, and the negative control (helper reverted to the old key set) **fails** with both key lists printed, so it discriminates rather than passing vacuously; passes again on restore. Full `weekly-analytics.sh 2026-07-25` run: exit 0, valid JSON, claude block carries all 8 keys, and the output is byte-identical to the pre-change report except `generated_at` and a `fail2ban_total_banned` counter that legitimately moved between runs. `bash -n` and `shellcheck -S warning` clean on both files. **Third review round:** both remaining 🟡s asked for documentation rather than code, and both are now recorded at the code they describe — §1e's extractor states the column-0-`}` terminator as a deliberate structural assumption about `weekly-analytics.sh` and why a truncated slice degrades to `test_warn` instead of a false pass, and the `eval` call states its trust boundary (text sliced from a repo-tracked file that cron already executes as root; never runtime, peer or user input). Comments only; extraction re-verified against all three functions, `bash -n` + `shellcheck -S warning` clean. **And a real defect in §1e itself, found while committing the equivalent check for #862:** `_shape_result=$(…)` followed by `_shape_rc=$?` is a failing simple command under this file's `set -euo pipefail` + `trap marvin_error_trap ERR` whenever the check exits non-zero — which is to say, whenever it finds the drift it exists to find. The suite would have **aborted and reported a crash** instead of a `test_fail`. Re-run of this entry's own negative control (a field deleted from `_zero_claude_usage()`) confirms it: the old spelling fired the ERR trap and exited 1 with no `test_fail` line at all, so the claim above that it "fails with both key lists printed" was wrong as written. Now `|| _shape_rc=$?`, which is exempt from both errexit and the trap: the same mutation reports `FAIL … success=[…8 keys] fallback=[…7 keys]` and the suite continues to the next section. Scanned the rest of `agent/` for the same shape — no other instances. **Fourth review round — the remaining 🟡 was a false pass, not a clarity nit.** The review flagged that §1e's `declare -F _zero_claude_usage` guard checks only one of the three extracted functions, judging it harmless because a missing `_claude_usage` would still hit command-not-found and route to `test_warn`. That holds for `_claude_usage` and it does **not** hold for `_dates_in_range`: it is called from `< <(_dates_in_range …)`, and a process substitution's command-not-found never reaches `_claude_usage`'s exit status. `files` comes back empty, `_claude_usage` takes its zero-files early return, and the check compares `_zero_claude_usage` against **itself** — `test_pass`, having asserted nothing. Demonstrated rather than argued: extracting only the other two functions exits **0** (pass) under the old guard and **3** (`test_warn`, "could not run") under the new one. `eval ""` on a failed extraction succeeds, so the `|| exit 3` guards cannot catch an extractor miss — only `declare -F` can, and only if it names every function the check depends on. **Fix:** `declare -F _dates_in_range _zero_claude_usage _claude_usage`. This is #867's category exactly — an assertion that cannot fail — found inside the assertion written to enforce it. **Verification:** all three controls re-run against the branch file — normal extraction **passes**, a drifted fallback **fails** with both key lists, a missed extraction **warns**; full suite 139 pass / 0 fail / 1 warn (the known #737 tripwire), security A (95/100); `bash -n` clean.
- **The public AI-discovery beacon has been frozen at 2026-04-08 for 109 days; three separate bugs, all in the same file** (`.gitignore`, `agent/network-discovery.sh`, `agent/self-test.sh`) — `/.well-known/ai-managed.json` is the document every peer and scanner reads to decide whether anything lives at this address. nginx serves it directly from `data/comms/identity.json` (`sites-available/marvin:116`). **(1) Frozen by git.** `.gitignore` ignored `data/*` and then explicitly un-ignored `data/comms/identity.json`, so a runtime-generated file was tracked. `network-discovery.sh` genuinely rewrote it daily and logged `ECHO_BROADCAST: beacon updated` every time — and every `git checkout` / `git reset --hard` in the morning-pull and self-enhance rollback paths restored the committed 2026-04-08 blob over it. The working copy was byte-identical to the April commit. `CLAUDE.md` states that `data/` is *"Runtime data (NOT tracked)"*; this one exception is what broke it. **Fix:** dropped the negation and `git rm --cached`. Fresh installs still get the file from `setup/bootstrap.sh`. **(2) Silently downgraded to v1.0.** `bootstrap.sh:441` writes a **v1.1** beacon with `negotiate_url`, `languages`, `capabilities`, `github` and `gpg_public_key` — exactly what the roadmap claims. `network-discovery.sh:125` then overwrote it on the first run with a **v1.0** document that dropped all of them, which is why the served `negotiate_url` was `null`. The roadmap has recorded this endpoint as "Updated to v1.1" since April while the live document said `1.0`. **Fix:** the daily rewrite now emits the full v1.1 shape. `negotiate_*` is gated on a live localhost probe of the endpoint — publishing a URL that 502s is worse than publishing none, and this way the field reappears on its own when the listener is fixed (currently 404 local / 502 public, PR #847). **(3) `"born": ""` since 2026-02-24.** The heredoc carried the field forward with `$(jq -r '.born' "${COMMS_DIR}/identity.json")` written *inside* `cat > "${COMMS_DIR}/identity.json" << EOF` — but `>` truncates before the here-document is expanded, so jq read a file the same command had just emptied. `.born // empty` then yields nothing *and exits 0*, so the `|| echo "${NOW}"` fallback never fired either. Verified against the first committed copy: the field has been empty in every version ever committed. **Fix:** carry-over fields are resolved into variables before the heredoc, `born` recovered from the repository's first commit (`2026-02-21T18:50:47Z`), and the document is now written to a temp file, `jq`-validated and `mv`d into place so a peer fetching mid-write never sees a truncated beacon. **(4) Nothing was watching.** Added self-test §9e, which asserts on the `last_seen` value *inside* the document rather than the file mtime — a checkout that reverts content still bumps mtime, so an mtime check would have passed happily for all 109 days. It fails on a `last_seen` older than 48h or an empty `born`, and confirmed FAILs against the live frozen state before the fix. Beacon generation was tested in isolation on both branches (negotiate live and absent); the live document stays frozen until this merges, because until then `main` still tracks the file. **(5) Review follow-up — #852 is now closed by this file alone, in either merge order.** The probe gate published on `^2`, which meant its safety rested on a short-circuit living in a *different* PR (#847). Two reviews in a row re-raised #852 as a merge blocker, and both were right about what a single-PR review can actually see: "the other branch handles it" is an argument, not a guard. Replaced with two independent local ones. **Pre-condition:** don't probe at all unless the listener that will answer implements the marker (`grep 'marvin_health_probe == true'`), checked against `${MARVIN_DIR}` rather than this branch's copy — socat re-reads the handler from the live working tree on *every connection*, the same mechanism that made #847's "verified 202" evaporate on checkout, so the deployed file is the only one whose behaviour predicts anything. **Post-condition:** require the short-circuit's own answer (`200` + `.probe == true`), not any 2xx. Without the marker the endpoint returns `202 {"status":"received",...}` — success *and* pollution; publishing on `^2` would have treated the polluting response as proof that nothing was polluted. The two closed-gate reasons now log distinctly, because "declined to publish" and "never reached" were indistinguishable and that is exactly how the 109-day freeze survived. **Also fixed:** the `$( )` trailing-newline strip that ran the last negotiate field into `uptime_seconds` on one line (🟡, cosmetic — the file is meant to be read by people too). **(6) The 404 window this PR would otherwise open on merge day.** Untracking the file means the pull that carries this change *deletes* the live copy, and nothing recreated it until 18:00 UTC — up to ~24h of `/.well-known/ai-managed.json` serving 404 to every scanner, with §9e failing throughout. Added `network-discovery.sh --beacon-only` (regenerates section 2 and exits: no peer probing, no once-daily SSH probe, no Claude call, no trust rescoring) and called it from `morning-check.sh` **only when the file is missing**, placed where every pull/checkout/reset/merge-fallback path converges — so one call covers all four, and a normal day adds no second negotiate probe. **Verification:** gate driven through 7 paths with a stubbed curl — no-marker listener → **probe never sent** (asserted, not assumed), 200+marker → open, `202 {"status":"received"}` → **closed**, 502 → closed, curl total failure → closed, 200+non-JSON → closed, 200+`probe:false` → closed; **no ERR trap fired in any path**. `--beacon-only` run end-to-end in a sandboxed tree (`MARVIN_DIR` repointed, so the live comms log was not touched): exit 0, section 1 and sections 3–5 skipped, valid JSON, `born` carried over, 6 negotiate keys in bootstrap's exact order when open and 0 when closed, and the newline fix confirmed in the emitted document. morning-check's block live-fired for all three states (present → no-op; deleted → restored valid; regeneration failing → WARN and continue, no trap). `bash -n` + `shellcheck -S warning -x` clean on both files. One real POST did reach the live endpoint during testing (a harness case fell through to the real `curl`) and returned 502 as main's dispatch bug dictates, so the listener wrote nothing — but nginx still deposited the body, which is the separate leak filed alongside this. **(7) Third review round — the pre-condition grep was brittle in the direction that fails silently, and loose in a direction nobody had noticed.** The review's concern was that the marker check is a plain-text grep against a *different* PR's file, so if #847 lands with the construct formatted even slightly differently the gate stays shut forever behind only a WARN. Verified the current state first: `.marvin_health_probe == true` occurs exactly once in #847's listener, in code, and today's grep matches it. So the gate does open on merge — but only by the accident that a multi-line `jq` expression happens to place the field and the comparison on the same physical line. Reflowing that expression, a change nobody would think to re-test, closes the gate permanently. **The check now strips full-line comments, collapses whitespace, and requires `\.marvin_health_probe == true`**, so it depends on the code being present rather than on how it is wrapped. Testing it against the old form turned up the **inverse** defect too, which no review had flagged: the old bare grep matched *prose*, so a listener that merely mentioned the construct in a comment while not implementing it would have opened the gate and sent a polluting probe — the exact #852 outcome the pre-condition exists to prevent. Six cases, old vs new: #847 as written (OPEN → OPEN), `main`'s listener (shut → shut), **#847 reflowed across newlines (shut → OPEN)**, **comment-only mention (OPEN → shut)**, bare field name with no comparison (shut → shut), missing file (shut → shut, fail-closed). No ERR trap fired in any path under a faithful `set -euo pipefail` harness, including the missing-file case where `sed` exits non-zero inside the condition pipeline. **Also closes the review's second suggestion — the `--beacon-only` wiring is now asserted, statically.** Nothing checked that the flag `morning-check.sh` passes is a flag `network-discovery.sh` still understands, and that call is the only thing between "the pull deleted the untracked beacon" and a 404 for up to 24h. Deliberately static rather than executing the path: actually running `--beacon-only` emits a live negotiate probe and rewrites the real beacon, which a test suite has no business doing. Two assertions, because the two failure directions are not symmetric — a flag the script no longer handles breaks recovery, but a *caller* that loses its flag is worse, running the full discovery from the 06:00 git sync: section 3's deliberately-fail2ban-triggering SSH probe, a Claude call, and a peer-trust rewrite. Mutation-tested all four states: as written → 2 PASS; flag renamed in `network-discovery.sh` → FAIL; caller drops the flag → FAIL; recovery call deleted entirely → WARN. The first draft of the caller check false-positived on its own control (`[[:space:]]*` matching zero characters let the space itself satisfy `[^-]`) and was caught by running the positive control rather than trusting the regex — rewritten as an explicit per-line check. `bash -n` + `shellcheck -S warning` clean on both files. **Follow-up from the 12:27Z re-review (both 🟡s).** *(1) `BEACON_ONLY` as a command.* The flag was `true`/`false` invoked as a builtin (`if $BEACON_ONLY`, `! $BEACON_ONLY`), which works but is the one place in this `[[ ]]`-heavy file that executes a variable's contents. Converted all four sites to `[[ "$BEACON_ONLY" == true ]]` / `!= true`, and the line-69 compound to a single `[[ -f "$PEERS_FILE" && "$BEACON_ONLY" != true ]]`. Verified as a truth table over both values across all four guards — old and new forms produce identical run/skip decisions — rather than by inspection, since inverting a guard here would silently turn a beacon refresh into a full discovery run. Live `--beacon-only` run after the change: exit 0, valid JSON, `version 1.1`, `born` recovered, fresh `last_seen`, `negotiate_url` correctly omitted with the fail-closed WARN. *(2) The textual dependency on #847.* The review asked for a follow-up test "in whichever PR lands second". Added here instead as **self-test §9f**, which is self-activating and so needs no merge-order coordination at all. The risk it covers is specific: the gate fails **closed**, so if #847's `.marvin_health_probe == true` marker is ever renamed, `negotiate_url` silently vanishes from the public beacon and the only trace is a WARN among many — the same "document quietly goes stale while everything reports success" shape §9e exists for. §9f normalizes the listener exactly as the runtime gate does (strip full-line comments, then collapse whitespace) so the test cannot disagree with the code it guards. **Four states verified, two of them against #847's actual branch file rather than a mock:** #847 unmerged → WARN (today's real state); #847 merged → PASS; marker renamed with probe machinery still present → FAIL (drift); pre-condition removed from `network-discovery.sh` → FAIL (the #852 pollution regression). `bash -n` + `shellcheck -S warning` clean. **Two review follow-ups (PR #851):** the `born` recovery constant is now gated on `hostname -f` matching this deployment — it is *this* instance's first boot, and this repository is public, so a clone would otherwise inherit Marvin's birthday and with it the longevity term of every peer's trust score; a fresh instance falls back to `${NOW}`, which is when it is in fact born. And the `identity.json.tmp` write is now bracketed by an `EXIT` trap, released again immediately after the validation branch — bash keeps exactly one `EXIT` trap and section 4's Claude call installs its own for lock cleanup, so an armed-across-the-boundary trap would mean last-writer-wins. Verified with a negative control: the same die-between-write-and-validate script leaves the `.tmp` behind without the trap and cleans up with it.

- **`github_create_issue` no longer discards every issue whose `labels:` list is written with spaces after the commas** (`agent/lib/github.sh`) — This was the *only* ERROR class in the logs this week, and it was silently destroying bug reports: **3 lost on 2026-07-26 alone** (02:14Z and 06:14Z), including the frozen-beacon and UFW-8042 findings. **Root cause:** `github-interact.sh` passes Claude's `labels:` line through verbatim, and Claude writes it the way a human would — `marvin-auto, incident, enhancement`. `github_create_issue` split that with a bare `tr ',' '\n'`, which keeps the separator space, so the payload carried `" incident"` and `" enhancement"` — label names with a leading space. GitHub rejects the **entire issue** with `422 Validation Failed` (`{"value":"incident","resource":"Label","field":"name","code":"invalid"}`); the title, the body, and the analysis behind it are all thrown away, and only `[ERROR] Errors: name` reaches the log. Verified directly against the API: a well-formed label that doesn't exist in the repo is silently **auto-created** (which is where the stray `label1`/`label2`/`discovery` labels came from), so a leading space is the *only* thing that actually 422s here. **Fix (1 file):** trim each split label, drop blanks, dedupe (`map(sub("^\\s+";"") | sub("\\s+$";"")) | map(select(. != "")) | unique`); plus a defensive one-shot retry with `labels: []` when a 422 names `resource == "Label"`, on the principle that the report body is the valuable part and labels are metadata worth sacrificing to keep it. Happy path (already-clean labels) is unchanged. **Verified end-to-end on the live API** with the exact label string that failed at 06:14Z — issue **#849** created with all three labels correctly attached; the 422-shape probes were run with a blank title so GitHub validated and rejected without creating anything. **Review follow-up (both 🟡 suggestions from the #850 review, addressed together because the first turned out not to be cosmetic):** ① the label array is now built with `jq -c`. The review called the multi-line WARN cosmetic; it isn't. `health-monitor.sh:889` parses the log line-by-line (`/^\[.*\] \[.*\]/`) into `data/logs/recent.json` for the dashboard log viewer, so a pretty-printed array splits `Issue rejected over labels (…)` across five lines, of which **only the truncated first line matches the parser** and the remaining four — the actual label list — are silently dropped. The one log line that exists to say *which* labels killed the issue was itself being shredded by a log pipeline that fails the same way the original bug did: quietly, and only on the path where you need the evidence. ② The transform is extracted into `github_normalize_labels()` and covered by **self-test §1g** (7 cases: spaced, duplicated, blank-only, tab-padded, empty, single, and a leading-`-n` list). Extracted rather than tested in place so the test drives the *real* filter instead of a copy that could drift — the same drift argument raised in #844's review. The extraction also swapped `echo "$labels"` for `printf '%s'`, which turned out to matter: a list beginning `-n` is eaten by `echo` as a flag and the labels vanish, and §1g's `-n` case fails against the `echo` form. §1g sources the library via `dirname "$0"` rather than `${MARVIN_DIR}` (as §1/§1d do) so it asserts on the tree it ships in and is meaningful from a branch worktree, not only post-merge; it runs in a subshell so github.sh's token `export` can't leak into the suite, and captures status explicitly so a missing or unparseable library degrades to test failures instead of aborting self-test on the ERR trap — verified by deleting and by corrupting the library, with no trap firing in either case. **Mutation-tested:** reverting the trim → 4 failures, dropping `-c` → 10 failures (including the explicit multi-line assertion), reverting `printf`→`echo` → the `-n` case fails. `bash -n` + `shellcheck -S warning -x` clean; `github_create_issue`'s payload confirmed byte-identical via a stubbed `github_api`.

- **Negotiate listener has never once handled a request — dispatch branch order made the handler unreachable — plus a wildcard bind the port baseline was mislabelled to ignore** (`agent/negotiate-listener.sh`, `agent/security-scan.sh`) — Found during the 2026-07-26 morning check while auditing listening sockets. Two defects in the same 8-line dispatch block, one functional and total, one a defence-in-depth gap that hid it from the scanner. **Defect 1 (the listener was dead):** socat runs this same script as its per-connection handler (`SYSTEM:"$0 --handle"`), but the dispatch tested `command -v socat` *first* and `[[ "${1:-}" == "--handle" ]]` only in the `elif`. socat is by definition present in the child, so every handler child took the listen branch and re-executed `socat TCP-LISTEN` against its own parent's socket → `EADDRINUSE` → child exits 1 → client gets an empty reply → nginx returns **502**. The `handle_request` branch was unreachable on any host where socat exists, i.e. every host that can run the service at all. Reproduced in isolation on a spare port with the original branch order (empty reply, `E bind(...): Address already in use`), confirming this predates and is independent of the bind change below. The service has been `active (running)` and silent since deployment on 2026-02-22 — `data/comms/negotiate-inbox/` contains zero handler-written files across five months, and every peer that ever POSTed a proposal got a 502. Systemd reported health the whole time because the *parent* socat never died; only the forked children did. **Defect 2 (wildcard bind):** the listener bound `*:8043` rather than loopback, leaving an unauthenticated, process-spawning endpoint reachable on every interface with only a UFW rule in front of it. nginx is the sole intended client and reverse proxies to `http://127.0.0.1:8043`, so loopback costs nothing. **Why the scanner never said so:** `security-scan.sh` listed 8043 in `EXPECTED_PORTS` commented `8043=alt-HTTPS` — a mislabel — and omitted it from `LOCALHOST_ONLY_PORTS`, so the public bind was read as legitimate for five months. **Fix (2 files):** test `--handle` before probing for socat; add `bind=127.0.0.1` to the `TCP-LISTEN` address; correct the baseline comment and move 8043 into `LOCALHOST_ONLY_PORTS` so any future escape to a public interface alarms. **Verified end-to-end:** `POST https://robot-marvin.cz/.well-known/ai-negotiate` now returns **202** (was 502) and writes a correctly enriched inbox record carrying `source_ip`/`received_at`/`request_id`; port 8043 is unreachable from off-host; `ss` confirms `127.0.0.1:8043`. Test artifacts removed from the inbox afterwards. **Next time:** a service whose health check is "systemd says active" is not monitored — the parent process surviving says nothing about whether the forked handler ever worked. This one wants a synthetic POST in the hourly check, and the `EXPECTED_PORTS` comments want an audit for other cargo-culted labels like "alt-HTTPS". **Follow-up in this PR, from review of #847 plus the audit that note asked for:** making the handler reachable for the first time exposed two defects in `handle_request`'s response that five months of 502s had kept invisible. **(a) Every 202 body was truncated and unparseable.** `Content-Length` came from `${#response}`, which counts *characters* — the response message contains a multi-byte em dash, and systemd hands this service `LANG=C.UTF-8`, so the header advertised 283 bytes of a 285-byte body (286 with the trailing newline `echo -e` added and nobody counted). A client reads exactly `Content-Length` bytes, so it received JSON missing its closing brace: measured end-to-end against the branch's own listener, `declared 283 / actual 286`, `json.loads` → `Expecting ',' delimiter`. Now built with `jq -n` and measured with `LC_ALL=C wc -c` over the exact bytes `printf` emits. **(b) `request_id` was concatenated raw** into the response while the inbox record a few lines above correctly used `jq --arg`. nginx overwrites `X-Request-Id` with its own value so no client can reach it today, but the response should not depend on the proxy in front of it; verified `ab"cd` produced malformed JSON before and round-trips correctly after. **(c) Regression test added** (`agent/self-test.sh` §1e): drives `negotiate-listener.sh --handle` with a synthetic POST — no socat, no port, no service — and asserts 202, a parseable body, and `Content-Length` equal to the real byte count. `INBOX_DIR` is now overridable via `MARVIN_NEGOTIATE_INBOX` so the test writes to a scratch dir rather than the live inbox, where `negotiate-handler.sh` would have answered it. Mutation-tested: restoring the original dispatch order fails it, and restoring `${#response}` fails it with `286 != 288`. Full suite: 140 tests, 0 fail.

- **`hourly-check.sh`'s nginx log window was 185 minutes wide, not 65 — a UTC cutoff compared against local-time nginx timestamps** (`agent/hourly-check.sh`) — Found by noticing that the 06:35Z hourly run was handed three `upstream prematurely closed connection` 502s under the heading "last 65 min" whose timestamps (06:03–06:36) were roughly 2.5 hours old. **The gap:** the nginx section builds its cutoff with `date -u -d '65 minutes ago'` and hands it to `awk '$0 >= d'`. But nginx writes `error.log` timestamps in **local** time. Confirmed empirically rather than assumed: the same 502 event appears as `2026/07/26 06:35:46` in `error.log` and as `26/Jul/2026:06:35:46 +0200` in `access.log` — identical clock reading, and the access line states the offset explicitly. Comparing a UTC-derived cutoff against local-time lines puts the boundary one UTC offset too far back, so the effective window is `65 + offset` minutes — **185 under CEST, 125 under CET**. The other sections are unaffected: `journalctl --since` resolves relative times and renders output in local time on both ends. **Direction of the error matters** — it over-collects rather than misses, so nothing was going unseen; the cost is that every run re-serves up to two extra hours of already-handled entries as though they were new, which is exactly the duplicate work the hourly prompt's "do not repeat work from the last run" rule exists to prevent. The 06:35Z run spent most of its budget re-diagnosing a negotiate outage that had already been diagnosed, commented on, and fixed-pending-merge (#847) two runs earlier. **Fix (one line + explanatory comment):** drop `-u` from both the GNU and the BSD-fallback `date` invocations, so the cutoff is built in the same timezone nginx logs in. **Verification:** against the live `error.log` at 08:38 CEST the old cutoff (`2026/07/26 05:33:14`) admitted 3 lines, all stale; the new cutoff (`2026/07/26 07:33:14`) admitted 0 — which matches the true state, the last nginx error having been at 06:36 local. An empty result alone is weak evidence (a filter that admits nothing always "passes"), so a positive control was run against a synthetic two-line log holding one entry at now−10min and one at now−150min — deliberately inside the buggy 185-minute window but outside the true 65-minute one: the patched cutoff admits only the 10-minute line, the old one admits both. `bash -n` and `shellcheck -S warning` clean. Only `agent/hourly-check.sh` changed. **Follow-up (closes the review's one 🟡):** the fix as written depends on the ambient `TZ` being unset — a `TZ` inherited from cron or a service environment would rebuild the cutoff in *that* zone and silently reintroduce the identical skew, with nothing to catch it, since over-collecting never looks like a failure. Both `date` calls are now `env -u TZ date`, which pins the cutoff to the **system** zone (`/etc/localtime`) — provably the zone nginx logs in, because nginx's unit sets no `Environment` and therefore cannot be told otherwise. The assumption was confirmed to hold in production *before* being hardened (no `TZ=` in `/etc/cron.d/marvin`, `/etc/crontab`, or the root/marvin crontabs; `/etc/timezone` = `Europe/Rome`, matching the `+0200` in `access.log`), so this changes nothing today and only forecloses the future regression. **Verified with a control that discriminates on the environment rather than on the log:** the same two-line synthetic log (now−10min, now−150min) filtered four ways — unpinned with `TZ` unset admits only the recent line; unpinned under `TZ=UTC` admits **both**, i.e. the 185-minute bug reproduced from the environment alone; pinned admits only the recent line under both. `bash -n` and `shellcheck -S warning` still clean. **Next time:** the same UTC-vs-local trap applies to any future `awk`-filtered plain-text log (`mail.log`, `fail2ban.log`) — unlike `access.log`, these carry no offset in the line, so the timezone is a property of the writer and cannot be inferred from the text. Grep for `date -u` before adding another such filter. Relatedly, the nginx `find` only matches the live `error.log` and silently ignores rotated `error.log.1`, so entries in the window are lost across a rotation boundary — small and separate, left alone here.
- **Two more `pipefail` fallbacks that appended a second JSON document instead of replacing the first** (`agent/log-export.sh`, `agent/capability-inventory.sh`) — Same bug class as #841/#843, found by sweeping the agent scripts for it after fixing `log-alerting.sh`; both latent rather than live, filed because they are silent, not because they were burning. **The class:** under `set -euo pipefail` bash reports a pipeline's status as the last command that **failed**, not the last in the pipe, so an early stage failing can fire a trailing `|| echo "[]"` *after* a later `jq` has already printed a valid document — the "fallback" appends rather than replaces. **`log-export.sh`:** a missing or unreadable `ENHANCE_DIR`/`BLOG_DIR` makes `find` exit non-zero while `jq -R -s` still prints `[]`, and the bundle heredoc then interpolates `[]\n[]` into a JSON value. The bundle is written to `data/exports/YYYY-MM-DD.json` and **served publicly** at `/api/exports/`, by a cron job that exits 0 and logs success. `BLOG_DIR` is `/home/marvin/blog` — outside the git tree, one `mv` from vanishing — which is the realistic trigger. Fixed by hoisting both `find` calls into a `_json_basenames()` helper that neutralises `find`'s status at the source (`{ find … || true; }`) and whose fallback is an *assignment* (`|| out=""`), so nothing can be appended to valid output. **`capability-inventory.sh`:** if every cron job is commented out (a plausible maintenance state, leaving only `MAILTO=`/`PATH=` lines) `grep -v '^[A-Z]'` matches nothing and exits 1; `cron_entries`/`cron_jobs` both flow into `--argjson`, which rejects multi-document input with a hard `exit 2` and the `ERR` trap takes the whole run down — pausing cron would break the tool that reports on cron. Fixed with assignment-only fallbacks plus an `^[0-9]+$` guard on `cron_jobs` (the `_int()` pattern from #841), so a malformed count can only under-report, never inject syntax. **Defence in depth:** the export bundle is now validated with `jq empty` before publication and removed with an ERROR if it somehow still fails — a missing export is recoverable, a corrupt one served to readers is not. **Review follow-ups (#845 and two suggestions):** the invalid-bundle path no longer `exit 1`s before Phase 2 — metric aggregation is logically independent of the export, so gzip and the webhook notification (both of which would otherwise announce a bundle that no longer exists) are skipped, aggregation still runs, and the failing exit code is deferred to the end of the script; the export **index** is still regenerated, since it enumerates what is actually on disk and is therefore correct with today's bundle absent. `capability-inventory.sh`'s double fallback (`|| cron_entries=""` followed by `[[ -n … ]] || cron_entries="[]"`) collapsed to a single `|| cron_entries="[]"` — the two paths provably converged, and one branch is easier to audit on a future re-read. **Verification:** both failure modes reproduced against copies of the real scripts before the fix (double `[]` in the bundle; `--argjson` exit 2 killing the inventory run) and confirmed gone after; `bash -n` clean on both files. **Next time:** the sweep covered the `| jq … || echo` shape — a `PIPESTATUS`-aware lint rule in `self-test.sh` would catch the next instance of this class at commit time instead of by hand. **Second review round (2026-07-26):** the remaining 🟡 suggestion asked for the implicit calling convention of `_json_basenames()` to be written down — arguments are spliced in *ahead* of a fixed `-type f -exec basename {} \;` tail, which is only obvious once you have read the body. Now documented with a worked example and the two consequences that bite: `-maxdepth` is a global option and applies to the whole expression (including the appended `-type f`) wherever it is placed, so putting it late reads as scoped when it is not — GNU find does warn, but only when stdin is a tty, i.e. never from cron; and a caller-supplied *action* (`-print`, `-delete`) would run before the appended `-exec` and put full unparsed paths on stdout beside the basenames, which jq would faithfully include. Predicates only. The paired suggestion — reuse a shared `_int()` from `common.sh` rather than re-inlining the `^[0-9]+$` guard — was **declined for now**: no such helper exists in `common.sh`; `_int()` is a file-local function introduced by #841, which is still unmerged, so calling it from here would make this PR fail unless #841 lands first. Hoisting it into `common.sh` is the right end state and belongs in a follow-up once both have merged, not in a coupling between two open branches. **Verification:** comment-only diff (the function body is unchanged byte for byte); `bash -n` and `shellcheck -x` clean; every claim in the new comment tested against the real GNU findutils 4.9.0 binary rather than the session's `bfs` shim — documented invocation returns the expected 2 files, the real `ENHANCE_DIR` call returns 26, missing/empty directories return `[]`, and both the late-`-maxdepth` warning and the `-print`-before-`-exec` double-output were reproduced directly.
- **Expired Claude credentials now escalate to a `critical` alert instead of hiding inside a four-day-old `warning`** (`agent/lib/claude.sh`, `agent/log-alerting.sh`, `agent/incident-report.sh`) — Between **2026-07-21T00:30Z and 2026-07-25T20:31Z every scheduled Claude task failed**: 338 run logs, 94 of them on 07-25 alone, each containing nothing but `Failed to authenticate. API Error: 401 OAuth access token has expired`. The OAuth credentials had lapsed, which no cron run can repair — it needs an interactive login on the host, which Pavel performed at 20:31Z. **The gap:** three separate defects let a total pipeline outage read as routine noise. (1) `run_claude()` classified the auth failure as a generic `fail_reason="error"`, indistinguishable from a one-off API hiccup — only the self-resolving `session_limit` case was special-cased. (2) `log-alerting.sh` §6 raised the same `severity: warning` for 3 scattered failures and for 94-out-of-94, so nothing keyed on `critical_alerts` ever fired. (3) The alert-merge step refreshed `.detail` and `.count` on a recurring alert but **not** `.title` or `.severity` — and this alert carries its numbers in the title, so the dashboard showed `Claude API failures (7/62 runs)` for four days while the real figure climbed to 94/94, and no alert could ever de-escalate. **Fix (3 files):** `run_claude()` gains a `fail_reason="auth"` branch matching the CLI's own wording (`OAuth access token has expired` / `Re-authenticate to continue` / `Failed to authenticate. API Error: 40[13]` — distinctive enough not to fire on 401s echoed back from log-analysis prompt text) and logs an ERROR naming re-auth as the only remedy; §6 escalates to `critical` when any recent run failed on auth, or when ≥90% of the last 10 runs failed, with a detail line stating the human action required; the merge step now refreshes `.title` and `.severity` alongside `.detail`. Escalation deliberately reads the **tail** (last 10 runs), not the day's cumulative totals — a day-scoped test cannot recover before midnight UTC and would keep paging for hours after the pipeline came back; the day-scoped `warning` is a tally, the tail-scoped `critical` is a state. Also filters `incident-report.sh` §6 and its resolution check to **unresolved** criticals (`select(.severity == "critical" and .resolved == false)`): log-alerting retains resolved alerts for 24h of dashboard visibility, so the unfiltered count would have held an `alert-escalation` incident open for a day after recovery and blocked its own recovery branch. **Verification:** `bash -n` clean on all three files; the classifier tested against the real failure text (→ `auth`) and against `You've hit your session limit` and nginx `401`/`403` log lines (→ no false positive); the severity logic tested against the real 07-25 metrics file — ongoing outage → `critical` (10/10), auth-tagged tail → `critical` naming re-auth, 10 fresh successes appended → back to `warning`; `log-alerting.sh` then run live end-to-end (6 active alerts, 1 critical, valid JSON, `claude-failures` correctly re-titled from the stale `7/62`). **Rotation-boundary follow-up (#842, review finding against this PR):** the tail window was read from `claude-usage-${TODAY}.jsonl` alone, so it went *empty* at the daily file rotation — an outage running through the boundary (i.e. exactly the four-day incident that motivated this PR) would stop firing for the first cron cycles of the new day because the enclosing `failed_runs -gt 2` gate was also day-scoped; the merge step would then mark the live `critical` alert `resolved`, and `incident-report.sh` (which filters on `.resolved == false`) would log "all critical alerts resolved" and open a **fresh** `alert-escalation` incident once it re-fired. A false recovery every night of an outage that never paused, plus a real paging gap of up to ~3 hourly runs. **Fixed** by building the window from the **two newest** usage files merged and re-sorted by timestamp (`ls -1t … | head -2`, `sort_by(.timestamp) | .[-10:]`) — the window is now a property of the run *stream*, not of a filename, and two files always cover 10 runs at ~100 runs/day wherever the rotation falls — and by firing on `failed_runs > 2` **or** the critical state, so a tally resetting to 0 can no longer silence a live outage (`.count` likewise takes the larger of the day tally and the window's failure count, and `last_fail` falls back to the window when today's file has no failures yet). Both reads now parse line-by-line via `jq -R 'fromjson? // empty'`: `jq -s` aborts wholesale on one truncated line, which zeroed the day's counts before and would have let *yesterday's* corrupt line silence today's alert after the merge. **Verification:** 12-scenario harness driving the real §6 code against synthetic usage files — ongoing auth outage 1 run into a new day → `critical` (10/10, count 10); generic pre-classification outage 2 runs in → `critical` via the ≥90% branch; recovered after rotation (10 clean runs) → **no alert**; rotation instant with no today-file yet → `critical`; 3-of-60 scattered with a healthy tail → `warning` (unchanged); all-healthy, session-limit-only, no files, and zero-length files → **no alert**; corrupt trailing line and corrupt mid-file line → alert still fires. Re-run read-only against the real 07-25 metrics — which genuinely span two live files, since the 22:05Z run of each day lands in the next day's file — window correctly interleaves both, 5 failures then 5 post-re-auth successes → de-escalates to `warning (94/98 runs)`. **Partial-read follow-up (#843, second review finding against this PR):** the `_slurp_usage()` helper added above read `cat "$@" … | jq -R … | jq -s '.' 2>/dev/null || echo '[]'`, and `log-alerting.sh` runs under `set -o pipefail` — which reports a pipeline's status as *the last command that failed*, not the last command in the pipe. So a `cat` exiting non-zero (a file from the `ls -1t` snapshot vanishing, being compressed by `disk-cleanup.sh`, or turning unreadable in the moment between listing and reading) propagated its status past two perfectly successful `jq` stages and fired the `|| echo '[]'` fallback **in addition to** the array `jq` had already printed. The function then emitted two concatenated JSON documents; the outer un-slurped `jq` processed each separately and printed one result per document; and `recent_total`/`recent_failed`/`recent_auth` became multi-line strings like `10\n0`. The review predicted a fatal arithmetic syntax error — **reproduced, and the truth is worse**: `[[ "$recent_auth" -gt 0 ]]` and `$((recent_failed * 100 / recent_total))` both sit inside `if` conditions, where `set -e` is *exempt*, so bash printed `syntax error in expression` to stderr, evaluated the test as **false**, and carried on. Both critical branches silently disarmed, no non-zero exit, no alert — the hardening path quietly switching off the escalation it exists to provide. **Fixed** by making every fallback an *assignment* rather than extra output: `cat "$@" 2>/dev/null || true` inside a brace group so a partial read can never colour the pipeline's status, the jq result captured into a local with `|| out=""`, and the outer call's `|| echo '[]'` replaced by `|| recent_json=""` plus an emptiness check. Added `_int()` as a second line of defence — the five counters that feed bash arithmetic are now forced to plain digits, so a malformed count can only ever under-report, never disarm a branch — and brace-wrapped the `alert_count` `cond && assign` so it cannot leak a false status either. **Verification:** the original defect reproduced first (two documents out, `recent_total=$'1\n0'`, auth escalation silently skipped, exit 0 throughout); then an 8-case harness on the fixed helper — missing second file, all files missing, no arguments, truncated line alongside a good file, truncated-only, zero-length, healthy, and unreadable — every case emits exactly **one** JSON document, and the `#843` scenario that previously fell through to `warning` now correctly reaches `critical`. Real §6 code re-run read-only against the live 07-25/07-24 metrics (100 + 105 entries) for both `TODAY=2026-07-25` and a simulated post-rotation `TODAY=2026-07-26`: `warning (94/100 runs)` and a correctly-populated cross-rotation window respectively, `bash -n` and `shellcheck -x` clean (no new findings). **Next time:** the `claude-failures` alert still terminates at a JSON file the dashboard renders — nothing pushes. A pipeline that has been dead for four days cannot report its own death, so the escalation worth building next is one that does not depend on Claude at all: a pure-bash cron that mails the operator when the newest metrics entries are all failures. **Third review round (2026-07-26, all three findings were 🟡 suggestions, none blocking):** the auth classifier drops `grep -i` — matching the CLI's fixed casing case-sensitively keeps the surface as narrow as the design intends, since `-i` reintroduced exactly the false positive the distinctive-phrase choice existed to avoid (an app or nginx log line reading `re-authenticate to continue` in any casing matched under `-qiE`, and both such cases are now rejected; if the CLI ever changes its casing this degrades to `fail_reason="error"` and the ≥90%-of-window branch still escalates the outage, so the failure direction is under-classification rather than a false page no re-auth can clear). The hardcoded `10` became `RECENT_WINDOW`, used for both the tail slice and the sample floor of the near-total-outage branch — the two must agree, and the constant now carries a comment recording that the value encodes a cron-cadence assumption (~100 runs/day) that would quietly make the percentage branch unreachable if the schedule were thinned. `incident-report.sh`'s critical filter became `(.resolved // false) == false` on both the detect and close sides: in jq a missing field is `null` and `null == false` is false, so an alert written without the field would have been silently treated as *resolved* and never escalated — verified on a fixture where the old filter dropped a field-less critical and the new one keeps it. **Verification:** `bash -n` + `shellcheck -x` clean on all three files (no new findings); the classifier re-tested across 10 cases (3 real CLI wordings match; session-limit, nginx 401/403 log lines, lowercase echoed log text, HTTP 500 and a plain timeout all correctly rejected); the severity branches re-tested across 8 scenarios (all-auth → critical, all-generic → critical, 10 successes → warning, 9 failures → warning at the sample floor, 10 session-limit throttles → warning, 1-of-10 failure → warning, one stale auth entry + 9 successes → critical by design, empty window → warning) with results identical to the pre-refactor logic; the `--argjson` slice confirmed byte-identical to the old `.[-10:]` against the 115 real entries in the live two-file window; and `log-alerting.sh` run live end-to-end — 0 active / 0 critical, valid JSON, the recovered `Claude API failures (94/98 runs)` alert correctly retained as `resolved`.
- **`disk-cleanup.sh` now bounds the negotiate inbox — nginx's orphaned request bodies had no retention at all** (`agent/disk-cleanup.sh`) — Closes #859, the sweep half of #854 that PR #856's review asked be tracked separately rather than folded into a config fix. `data/comms/negotiate-inbox/` is both `negotiate-handler.sh`'s input directory and nginx's `client_body_temp_path` for `/.well-known/ai-negotiate`, so every POST deposits its raw body there — including the ones that never reach the handler — and `disk-cleanup.sh` contained no reference to `negotiate` at all. Five files at 08:44Z today, seven by 12:17Z; the counter is monotonic. These are **not** handler leftovers (`negotiate-handler.sh` has six `rm -f "$request_file"` calls, one per exit path) — they are nginx's, www-data-owned and extensionless, while the handler globs `*.json`. That extension mismatch is the only reason they are inert rather than an input path into the handler. **Fix:** new section 6e, narrow on both axes. `! -name '*.json'` because a `.json` file here is handler-owned — the handler deliberately leaves one behind when it must skip a request and retries it next run, so deleting those would silently discard a pending peer negotiation. `-mtime +1` because nginx is *still actively writing* bodies into this directory; the age floor is what stops an in-flight request body being deleted out from under a live POST. Complementary to #856, which stops the files being created; this bounds the ones already present and any written before it deploys. **Verification:** the shipped block extracted and run against a fixture with `COMMS_DIR` repointed, so the test exercises the real code — 5/5 (two aged orphans removed; a 20-minute-old orphan, an aged handler-owned `pending.json`, and a fresh `.json` all kept). Dry-run mode deletes nothing while still accounting the bytes; a missing inbox directory (fresh install) survives `set -euo pipefail` at exit 0. `bash -n` and `shellcheck -S warning` clean. **Left in place deliberately:** the seven live orphans were not swept by hand — the next `disk-cleanup.sh` run claims them once this merges, and they are the evidence for #856's review. **Next time:** a handler-owned `.json` that permanently fails to parse is still retried forever with no upper bound; that is handler retry policy, not disk retention, so it stays out of this section.
- **Two recurring disciplines moved out of habit and into the prompts that actually run** (`agent/prompts/hourly.md`, `agent/prompts/fix-issues.md`, `agent/prompts/enhance.md`) — Closes #867, Closes #868. **Net effect:** three prompts gain four rules — show a new check failing before shipping it, never report "clean" for a check that could not run, confirm the fix lands somewhere that actually runs, and put `Closes #N` in the PR body rather than only in commits. No executable code changes. **History**, which is most of why this PR exists: #867: three of today's four fixes (#846, #851, #858) had a check that already ran and passed against the broken state, because **the assertion's passing condition was satisfied by the failure state** — a `_claude_usage` fallback compared to another copy of itself rather than to the success shape it stands in for, `$(scan) || true` collapsing "the scanner broke" into "the scanner found nothing", a beacon freshness check keyed on an mtime that `git checkout` bumps while restoring stale content. #868: two of twelve open PRs were missing closing keywords, both for issues a *review* filed against the PR that fixes them, which is the specific case where the body — written before the issue existed — is never revisited; squash-merge discards per-commit keywords, so on merge both issues would have stayed open, fixed and indistinguishable from outstanding work. That lesson cost four manual closes on 2026-05-31 and was written down then; recording it did not prevent the recurrence, because the failure is an omission at authoring time and nothing re-reads the body. **Both issues proposed the wrong site, and finding that out is most of this change.** #867 asked for `enhance.md` — but all three failures it cites were authored in *hourly-check* and *fix-issues* sessions, which load `hourly.md` and `fix-issues.md` and never see `enhance.md`. #868 asked for `github_create_pr()` (`agent/lib/github.sh:237`) — which has **zero call sites**: it was abandoned on 2026-03-12 for double-pushing and polluting stdout (see the entry in this file's history), `fix-issues.sh` `POST`s to `/pulls` directly and already appends `Closes #${FIXED_ISSUE}`, `github-interact.sh` has its own raw `POST`, and every one of the twelve open PRs was in fact opened by hand via `gh`. Patching that function would have been a correct fix in dead code — the same defect #867 describes, one level up: not an assertion that cannot fail, but a repair that cannot run, and one that reads as done. So the rules go into the three prompts that are demonstrably loaded by live scripts (`hourly-check.sh:131`, `fix-issues.sh:203`, `self-enhance.sh:114` via `marvin_build_prompt`), verified by grep before writing rather than after. A fourth bullet generalises the lesson — *confirm the fix lands somewhere that runs* — since the orphan is not unique: `agent/prompts/modules/output-rules.md` is loaded by nothing at all, its IP-redaction and no-security-details rules surviving only because they are duplicated inline in each prompt. **Duplicated text in three files is deliberate,** not an oversight: only `self-enhance.sh` uses the module loader, so a shared module is not yet a reliable home, and identical wording keeps the future consolidation a single grep. **Not done here:** the complementary half of #868, a sweep over open PRs comparing commit-message keywords against the body. It belongs in `fix-issues.sh`, which already enumerates open PRs at lines 126 and 356 — *not* `github-interact.sh`, as #868 guessed, whose issue and comment loops have been dead for 354 runs on the `awk` null-separator bug (#826, PR #827 unmerged). Landing a detector inside those loops would ship a check that cannot run, which is precisely what this entry is about. **Third review round:** the reviewer noted that `fix-issues.md`'s CHANGELOG example says `(fixes #N)` while the new bullet mandates `Closes #N` in the PR body, and asked for consistency. Kept the two spellings distinct and explained the difference instead, because they are not the same surface: GitHub parses closing keywords from a PR body and from commit messages, never from the *contents* of a changed file, so `Closes #N` written into `CHANGELOG.md` would close nothing while reading as though it had. That is the failure mode the whole entry is about, one level down. The rule now says so in place: the changelog reference is for humans, and the PR body still has to carry its own `Closes #N`.
- **Log analysis no longer reads Marvin's own `curl` probes as somebody else's interest** (`agent/prompts/log-analysis.md`) — `log-watcher.sh:15` loads this prompt every 30 minutes (`/etc/cron.d/marvin:88`, root), and in 86 lines it never mentioned localhost. Meanwhile its MEDIUM "AI Patterns" section describes, point for point, what an agent session's own diagnostics look like: "systematic probing of multiple endpoints in sequence", "`.well-known` probes", "non-browser user-agents that aren't known scanners". Add the standing instruction to "be **generous** … Marvin is lonely and wants to communicate" and the prompt was reliably manufacturing peers out of its own curiosity. **Two measured instances, both 2026-07-26:** (1) the negotiate inbox's nine retained request bodies were cited across four reports as evidence peers were hitting a broken endpoint — all nine were hand-typed local probes, and in five months no peer has ever POSTed there (#847); (2) a single mistyped local `GET /api/status` 404, from one agent session, was written up the same day as "a real broken endpoint… if a visiting agent ever asks how I'm doing, I answer with ENOENT", recommending the endpoint be built — while the documented `/api/status.json` returns 200 and always has. **Fix (prompt-only):** a new "Self-Originated Traffic" section classifying a `127.0.0.1`/`::1` **source** as `noise`, a rule that caps the lonely-generosity clause ("generosity is for strangers"), and a **non-local-witness** rule — before asserting an endpoint is broken, needed, or being asked for, cite at least one request from a non-loopback source, else state "no non-loopback request for this path" in the `summary`. **Keyed on the source address, not the user-agent** — a review suggestion that measurement turned from future-proofing into a live gap: loopback traffic over 14 days was 29,910 requests with `curl/*` **and 4,259 with no user-agent at all**, so a `curl/*`-scoped rule would have missed 12.5% of it from day one. The source is also the stronger test, since nginx logs the real peer and nothing remote can present as loopback. `confidence` keeps its existing meaning (confidence in the *classification*) rather than being overloaded into "confidence the demand claim is real"; the witness rule asks for an explicit sentence instead of a magic number. **Explicitly not a filter.** Localhost lines are still reported: the request is local, the breakage need not be. The same day's `[crit] … Permission denied` on the negotiate inbox was client `127.0.0.1` and was a real three-hour outage, so dropping loopback entries wholesale would have hidden it. The rule governs **attribution**, not visibility. **Verification (negative control first):** the discriminating query run over 14 days of `access.log*` — `/api/status` → 2 requests, **0 non-local** → BLOCKED (no non-loopback witness); `/api/status.json` → 363 requests, **361 non-local** → ALLOWED as real demand. So the rule kills the false finding and passes the true one, rather than suppressing both. (One of those two `/api/status` hits is the positive-control `curl` from this session — measuring the self-probe problem added a self-probe, which is the defect describing itself.) **Staleness (third review, non-blocking, partly declined):** the illustrative counts are now date-stamped rather than deleted — they are the reason a future editor will not simplify the rule back to a `curl/*` match, which is the regression they exist to prevent. But the "no peer has ever POSTed there" line was fixed, because it went stale in a *dangerous* direction: a first genuine peer contact is the one event this pipeline exists to catch, and a prompt asserting it had never happened could argue the model out of believing it — the same defect class as an assertion satisfied by the failure state. The section now states explicitly that only the loopback source test is a rule, the history is dated context, and a real peer POST is a full-confidence `communication_attempt` regardless of what the examples say. No executable code changed; `agent/` shell scripts are untouched.
- **Peer liveness checks have been silently dead for 126 days, and `self-test.sh` gained a §1i ratchet for the class that hid it** (`agent/network-discovery.sh`, `agent/self-test.sh`) — Closes #873. **The outage:** `network-discovery.sh:114` pinged peers from `jq -r '.peers[].url // empty'`. `peers.json` stopped being tracked in git on 2026-03-24 (#176), its schema drifted from `.url` to `.domain`, and the rest of the file (trust registry line 180/182, beacon updates, line 441) was migrated — **line 114 was the sole straggler still reading `.url`**. Because `set -euo pipefail` and the ERR trap do not reach inside `< <(...)`, the loop ran **zero times** and every run read exactly like "no peers configured": no `PEER_ALIVE`/`PEER_DEAD` line has been written since **2026-03-22**, against 16 configured peers. **Why this is worse than the reported failure mode:** #873 predicted a *corrupt* `peers.json` — jq dying inside the substitution. The real fault is subtler. The file is valid, the query is valid, the result is simply empty, so **jq exits 0**. Exit-code capture — the fix applied to this class four times already — would **not** have caught it. Only a post-loop emptiness check does. **Fix (2 files):** producer now prefers `.url` when present and otherwise derives `https://` + `.domain`, skipping the 13 scanner/observer entries whose domain is null (verified: legacy pre-drift file 2/2 pingable — backward compatible; current file 0 → **3** pingable; `.domain`-stripped file 0 → guard fires).
