Post-loop guard logs `ERROR` when `PEER_COUNT > 0` but nothing was pinged. **A defect found in the fix itself, by running it:** the first guard keyed off `PEER_COUNT=$(jq '.peers | length' … || echo "0")` — on a *corrupt* file that fallback yields `0`, so `PEER_COUNT > 0` is false and the guard can never fire; the harness reported "0/0 peers pinged" as INFO and exited clean. The guard reproduced the exact bug it was written to catch, one level up in the same block. `peers.json` is now validated with `jq empty` first, so unreadable is distinguishable from empty. **§1i ratchet:** flags `done < <(…)` sites whose producer contains `jq` or a real pipeline and which carry no `# procsub-guarded` marker; baseline **24**, may fall, never rise. The scan walks each site to its matching close paren, so **multi-line** producers are classified on their whole text — a line-based grep scores §1d's own site safe (it pipes on a continuation line) and misses it. **The issue's "27" was wrong:** that figure came from a line-based `grep` which counts `|| true` as a pipe (3 false positives: `codebase-health.sh:66`, `fix-issues.sh:44,320`) and misses the multi-line case (1 false negative). True count under the stated criterion is **25**, now 24 guarded-adjusted. **Known gap, stated not hidden:** `git`-only producers (`fix-issues.sh:44,320`) are failure-capable but not yet counted; 33 plain-`find` producers remain deprioritised per the issue. The scan uses command substitution (which *does* propagate exit codes) rather than the construct it polices, and treats **empty output as FAILURE** — blocks always exist, so "found nothing" means the scan did not run, which is #858's exact defect and is deliberately not repeated. **Verification:** `bash -n` + `shellcheck -S warning` clean on both files; §1i extracted from source and run against four negative controls, each **demonstrated failing** per #867 — new unguarded `jq` producer added → FAIL 25; guard marker stripped → FAIL 26; no scripts to enumerate → FAIL "scan did NOT run"; `awk` forced to exit 127 → FAIL "enumerator produced no output" (neither degrades to a silent PASS). Guard logic tested via a harness generated from the real source block rather than a hand-copy, across legacy/current/drifted/corrupt `peers.json`, with `curl` stubbed so no live peer was contacted. **Next time:** the 126-day gap was invisible because nothing alerts on a *metric that stopped moving* — `PEER_ALIVE` count going to zero and staying there is the signal none of the ratchets watch; extend §1i's criterion to `git` producers and consider a staleness check on `peers.json`'s `last_checked` versus actual ping evidence. **From the review round that produced #892, and two things found while fixing it.** The §1i replacement in this branch was not additive: it overwrote *weekly-analytics fallback/success shape parity* wholesale — the `_wa_extract`/`eval` harness and the `_claude_usage` ⇆ `_zero_claude_usage` key comparison deleted, not moved — while the PR body and this entry described only the new content. That check exists because the two shapes had already drifted once, omitting `total_prompt_chars`/`total_output_chars`, and the drift is invisible in normal operation because the fallback only renders on an I/O fault. **Restored verbatim from `main` as §1i; the new process-substitution ratchet is renumbered §1j** (free on `main` and on every open branch), with its three in-tree cross-references updated in `self-test.sh` and `network-discovery.sh`. Re-verified rather than assumed: unmutated → PASS; `total_prompt_chars` deleted from `_zero_claude_usage()`'s literal → **FAIL printing both key lists** and no ERR trap; `weekly-analytics.sh` unreadable → WARN, not PASS. **The ratchet was red on its own branch and nobody had run it.** Against its own tree the branch reported `27 unguarded failure-capable producers, baseline 26 — a new one was added`, and 28 against `main`; merging it would have turned `main` red on the very check it introduces. The second merge of main (`eba2aad`) moved the count and the baseline was never re-based. Net +1, accounted per-site at the baseline itself in the same shape as the 24 → 26 note: `security-scan.sh:284` and `:288` arrived with #879's UFW audit, and the old `security-scan.sh:483` outbound loop left with #884, which replaced its producer with a herestring into `marvin_outbound_classify` — no pipeline, no jq, so it falls out of scope. Recorded as a **departure, not a fix**: nothing was guarded there, the site stopped matching. Baseline now **27**; verified by scanning four trees with this same section and diffing per file — `df6690d` (26, PASS), this branch (27, PASS), `main` (28, the extra being the `network-discovery.sh:133` site this PR fixes), deployed (29) — and the ratchet still **FAILs at 28** when a new unguarded `jq` producer is appended to `report-card.sh`. **And the reason the discrepancy existed at all is the trap §1i names two sections above it:** §1j enumerated `${MARVIN_DIR}/agent`, which `common.sh` hardcodes to the live tree and overrides from the environment, so the ratchet measured whatever is *deployed* rather than what the branch ships — the same defect that made §1h's evidence worthless in #855, reintroduced one section away from the comment warning about it. Now `$(dirname "$0")`. Full suite from the branch after all three changes: **161 total / 153 pass / 0 fail / 8 warn**, security A 95/100.

- **`/api/` is now an allowlist instead of a denylist — internal reports, build artifacts and the capability inventory were world-readable** (`setup/nginx-site.conf`, `setup/bootstrap.sh`) — Found from a single nginx 404 in the hourly log window: a scanner's `GET /api/auth/validate-sso` resolved to `/home/marvin/git/data/auth/validate-sso`, which showed that `location /api/ { alias /home/marvin/git/data/; }` publishes **all** of `data/`. Access was restricted by four deny rules (`/api/security/`, `/api/email/`, `/api/comms/`, `~* ^/api/lessons-`) — a denylist, so every subdirectory added to `data/` since those rules were written has been public by default. **What was actually served (verified, HTTP 200):** `data/logs/` — the hourly-check reports, which the hourly-check prompt itself classifies as *"internal only"* and *"no security details in public"*, containing fail2ban ban counts, disk/load posture and open-defect discussion; `data/tests/` weekly suite results; `data/codebase/capabilities.json` (15 KB capability + cron inventory); `data/web-backup/` — `server.js.bak` and 5.8 MB Next.js build tarballs, reachable by fixed names (`BUILD_ID.bak`); plus `self-test.json`, `runaway-procs.json`, `backup-status.json`, all 732 files in `metrics/`, all 33 in `alerts/`, all 53 in `reports/`, and `incidents/history/`. Directory listing is off, but names are predictable (`/api/logs/hourly-YYYY-MM-DD-HH.md`, `/api/tests/weekly-YYYY-MM-DD.md`) and `/api/.env` has already been probed 13 times in the retained access log — this surface is being scanned. **Not** a credential leak: the tarballs contain no `.env` and `server.js.bak` no secret-shaped identifiers (checked); this is information disclosure, not compromise. **Fix:** replaced the catch-all alias with a regex allowlist of the 20 endpoints that are either documented public in `data/openapi.yaml` or actually consumed — the allowlist was derived empirically from the retained nginx access log, not guessed, so no real consumer is cut off. Everything else under `/api/` now returns 403. The capture `$1` can only match a literal alternative, so it is not a traversal vector. **Two latent contract bugs fixed as a side effect,** both because a regex location outranks a prefix location: `/api/blog-index.json` is documented public but returned **404** (the `location /api/blog` prefix swallowed it and proxied it to Next.js), and `/api/security/security-score.json` is documented public but returned **403** (caught by the `/api/security/` deny). **Fixed in both sources:** `setup/bootstrap.sh` writes its own `/api/` block, and that copy had **no deny rules at all** — a bootstrap re-run would have additionally exposed `security/`, `email/` and `comms/`. A one-file fix would have left a re-run to silently reinstate a wider surface than the site config serves (same two-source trap as PR #856). **Verification:** positive *and* negative control — the modified server block was extracted verbatim from the shipped file and run under a throwaway nginx on `127.0.0.1:8098`, so the test exercises what is shipped rather than a hand-copy. 37 probes: 17 internal paths → **403**, 20 public paths → **200**. The same probes against the current `main` config return **200 for all 8** internal spot-checks and 404/403 for the two documented-public endpoints, confirming the test detects the defect rather than passing vacuously. `nginx -t` clean on both. **Live config deliberately NOT reloaded** — `/etc/nginx/sites-available/marvin` is byte-identical to the tracked copy (no drift), and applying an unreviewed access-control change to a public server is Pavel's call; deploy is the three-line recipe in the file header. Until then the exposure stands. **Rate limiting preserved on the deny path (#863, from this PR's review):** splitting the single `/api/` block dropped `limit_req zone=api` from the catch-all that now absorbs all scanner traffic. The review's proposed one-line fix — add `limit_req` back beside `return 403` — **does not work, and I only found that by measuring it.** `return` is handled by the rewrite module in the REWRITE phase, which runs *before* the PREACCESS phase where `limit_req` lives, so the request is already finished before the limiter executes. At `rate=1r/s burst=40`, `return 403` served **60/60** requests unthrottled; a control against an allowlisted alias path in the same config shed 19 of 60, proving the harness could throttle. Switched to `deny all` (access module, ACCESS phase, which runs *after* PREACCESS): **41 served, 19 shed**, matching the control. Both spellings return an identical 403, which is precisely what makes the inert one hard to spot. Not mirrored into `setup/bootstrap.sh` — that file defines no `limit_req_zone` at all, so referencing `zone=api` there would fail `nginx -t` and break a fresh install. **Next time:** the allowlist duplicates knowledge held in `data/openapi.yaml`; a self-test check asserting the two agree (and that no `location /api/` block carries a bare `alias …/data/;`) would stop the next `data/` subdirectory from quietly becoming public.
- **nginx no longer keeps every raw request body to the public negotiate endpoint inside the handler's own inbox** (`setup/nginx-site.conf`, `setup/bootstrap.sh`, `agent/self-test.sh`) — Issue #854, found while verifying that PR #851's beacon health probe was harmless. The `location /.well-known/ai-negotiate` block set `client_body_temp_path` to `data/comms/negotiate-inbox` **and** `client_body_in_file_only on`, and nginx's documented behaviour for `on` is that the temp file is *never removed after request processing*. So every POST to a public, unauthenticated endpoint deposited a permanent, raw, caller-controlled file (≤16k, per `client_max_body_size`) into the exact directory `negotiate-handler.sh` globs for proposals — 7 such files (`0000000005`…`0000000011`) were sitting there when this was written, and **14 by 2026-07-26** — the endpoint is still accruing them, because the fix below has still not been deployed, one per probe, including those from requests that **502'd** (the body is written before the upstream is reached). **Why it was inert, and why that is not a defence:** the handler globs `*.json` and nginx's temp names have no extension, so nothing ingested them — one character of luck between "inert" and "the public endpoint feeds the handler unsanitized input". Growth was also unbounded and swept by nothing: `disk-cleanup.sh` has no `negotiate-inbox` sweep at all, and `zone=sensitive` allows 2 r/s per IP, so the retention is a slow unauthenticated remote disk/inode-exhaustion path as well as a sanitization bypass waiting for a glob change. **Root cause is archaeological, not accidental:** the surrounding bootstrap comments (`# Write request body to inbox`, `# Use lua or a simple proxy to save — fallback: use a tiny CGI`) show both directives belong to an abandoned first design in which *nginx itself* was to deliver the proposal file. That design was superseded by the socat listener, which reads the body from stdin (`negotiate-listener.sh`: `head -c "$content_length"`) — so since then the directives have fed nothing while still retaining everything. **Fix:** both directives removed, so the body reverts to default handling (in memory; spilled to `/var/lib/nginx/body` only above `client_body_buffer_size`, and removed after the request) — which still forwards the body to the upstream, the only thing this location actually needs. Removed in **both** tracked sources: `setup/nginx-site.conf` (currently byte-identical to live, so a deploy is a straight copy + `nginx -t` + reload) and the `setup/bootstrap.sh` heredoc that writes the same block — without the second edit a bootstrap re-run would have reinstated the retention, and §9d only diffs `nginx-site.conf` against live, so nothing would have noticed. **New self-test §9h** (numbered `9h`, not `9f`: PR #851 landed a §9f of its own (the beacon negotiate gate ⇆ listener marker agreement) and open PR #862 already claims §9g, both while this branch sat open, so the section was renumbered on rebase rather than leaving sections sharing a letter) asserts the retention cannot return, as two separate invariants: FAIL if either tracked source sets `client_body_in_file_only on` (the one value nginx never removes the file for) and FAIL if either sets a `client_body_temp_path` under the project data dir (the actual defect — untrusted input landing in the handler's directory, which also catches the `clean` variant, whose file is transient but still lands there); `clean` with a temp path outside the tree stays legal, because #856's review correctly pointed out that banning it under a *"retains"* message would fail a future legitimate config with a false reason. WARN if the *live* config still trips either (that is a deploy step, and §9d already tracks the drift, so merging this cannot turn the suite red), WARN with a count on residual non-`*.json` files in the inbox. Placed in the §9 config family rather than §1 because it asserts configuration rather than script syntax — and because that keeps it out of the §1 insertion zone #847, #850 and the #855 detector are all editing. **Verification:** the modified site config validated with a real `nginx -t` against a full copy of `/etc/nginx` (`syntax is ok`, `test is successful`); the bootstrap heredoc re-rendered to confirm the escaped backticks survive `<< EOF` intact; §9h driven standalone against both trees — **4 FAIL on the pre-fix sources (both invariants × both files, each named individually), 1 PASS on the fixed branch** — with the live-drift and residue WARNs firing in both; `bash -n` + `shellcheck -S warning` clean. **Deliberately not done:** nginx was **not** reloaded and the live config **not** edited — deploying unreviewed config to a public web server is the human's call, and §9h/§9d both name the outstanding step; the 7 existing body files were left in place as the evidence #854 rests on (they are inert, and new ones stop appearing at the reload). **Next time:** `bootstrap.sh` writing its own copy of the site config instead of installing `setup/nginx-site.conf` is a second source of truth that already differs (bootstrap's block has no `limit_req`) and that no drift check compares — worth collapsing to one file; and `disk-cleanup.sh` still has no retention policy for the handler's own `*.json` records in that inbox.
- **`fix-issues.sh` now skips a run when it can't reliably fetch open PRs, instead of collapsing the failure to `[]` and running dedup-blind** (`agent/fix-issues.sh`) — Input-side complement to the 2026-07-20 output-side duplicate guard, and the exact "distinguish 'no open PRs' from 'fetch failed' at the source" belt-and-suspenders that entry's *Next time* flagged as still open. Clean day otherwise (in sync with origin, **0 open PRs**; the sole open issue #834 is Marvin's own informational milestone post, already reviewed by hourly-check — not a defect; the day's only WARNs were benign: a 2.2σ net-TX variance, 6 routine actionable security updates, and the expected `claude-lock-timeout` cron-overlap skip). **The gap:** the pile-up guard fetched open PRs with `open_prs=$(github_list_prs 10 2>/dev/null || echo "[]")`. That `|| echo "[]"` collapses a **transient** `github_list_prs` failure into an empty array indistinguishable from "genuinely no open PRs" → `open_pr_count=0` → **both** the pile-up guard **and** the entire per-issue dedup block (`if [[ "$open_pr_count" -gt 0 ]]`) are skipped, leaving Claude free to re-pick an issue that already has an open PR. This is the root cause the 2026-07-19 #835 incident (three duplicate PRs #836/#837/#838) exercised; yesterday's output-side re-fetch guard catches the *consequence* before push, but the input snapshot could still silently disable dedup. **Fix (1 file, additive):** capture the fetch exit code with the set-e-safe `open_prs=$(github_list_prs 10 2>/dev/null) && _prs_fetch_ok=true || _prs_fetch_ok=false` and validate the payload is a JSON array (`jq -e 'type == "array"'`); on either a non-zero exit **or** a non-array body (e.g. a 4xx error object) the run logs a WARN and `exit 0`, so the next 2-hourly cron simply retries. A genuine empty result (`[]`, exit 0) still proceeds normally, so healthy "no open issues/PRs" days are unchanged. **Deliberately scoped:** the *output*-side guard's `github_list_prs 20 ... || echo "[]"` stays best-effort by design (it falls through to proceed and never blocks a legitimate first PR) — only the input path, where an empty array silently disables dedup, needed to fail closed. **Verification:** `bash -n` + `shellcheck -S warning` clean (0); the guard tested in isolation under a faithful `set -euo pipefail` + ERR-trap harness across four conditions (healthy-with-PRs → proceed/dedup active; genuinely-0-PRs → proceed; transient total failure → **skip**; 4xx error-body → **skip**) with **no ERR trap firing** in any case (confirming the `&& … || …` capture is exit-code-masking-safe); full `agent/self-test.sh` **138 pass / 0 fail / 1 warn** (the intentional `.gnupg` #737 tripwire, Pavel-gated), grade A, security A 90/100. Did **not** run `fix-issues.sh` live mid-session (it spends a Claude call, mutates git, and creates real PRs on the every-2h cron); the change is pure exit-code control flow validated in isolation, applied by the next scheduled run. Only `agent/fix-issues.sh` changed. **Next time:** the secondary HTTP 422 on `github_create_issue` (label validation, field `name`) is now diagnosable via `.errors[]` logging but a `github_create_issue` fallback that retries without labels on a `name`-field 422 would salvage the report if it recurs; long-standing carry-overs unchanged — split `agent/security-scan.sh` (~830 LOC) into `agent/lib/security/*.sh`; Phase 3 ECHO/gossip items (parked); Pavel-gated `.gnupg/` cleanup (#737).
- **`fix-issues.sh` no longer creates duplicate PRs for the same issue — an output-side dedup guard now re-checks open PRs before pushing** (`agent/fix-issues.sh`) — Today's fix-failure target: fix-issues created **three** PRs (#836 20:25, #837 00:25, #838 02:25) for the *same* issue #835 across ~6 hours, while earlier PRs for it were still open — noise compounded when the hourly github-interact run then tried to file a bug about it and hit an unrelated HTTP 422. Root cause exactly as the github-interact analysis diagnosed: *fix-issues validates its input against open PRs but not its output.* The per-issue dedup (top of run) filters candidate issues against **a single snapshot** of open PRs taken at line 88 *before* Claude runs. That snapshot is fragile: `open_prs=$(github_list_prs 10 2>/dev/null || echo "[]")` collapses a **transient** `github_list_prs` failure to `"[]"`, making `open_pr_count=0` and **skipping the entire dedup block**, so `#835` was never filtered and Claude re-picked it. (Confirmed against the run logs: the 22:25 and 04:xx runs deduped #835 correctly via PR title `#835`; only the 00:25/02:25 runs re-created it — consistent with the initial fetch returning empty on those two runs. The extraction itself is correct: `#835` in PR #836's title yields `835`.) Claude also runs for minutes, during which a new PR can appear. **Fix (defense-in-depth, additive):** after Claude reports `FIXED_ISSUE` and *before* staging/commit/push, **re-fetch** open PRs and abort (`exit 0`, letting the `EXIT` cleanup trap return to clean `main` and drop the unpushed local branch) if any open PR already references that issue number. This validates the run's **output**, not just its input, so a stale/empty initial snapshot can no longer produce a duplicate. Factored the three-source issue-number extraction (PR titles `#NNN`/`issue NNN`, bodies `Fixes/Closes/Resolves #NNN`, branches `issue/NNN`) out of the inline input-dedup into a shared `_extract_pr_issue_numbers()` helper used by **both** dedup paths, so they can never disagree on what "already has a PR" means (a 1:1 behavioural replacement of the previous inline logic, verified identical). Best-effort by design: if the re-fetch *also* fails (`"[]"`), it falls through — no worse than the pre-existing input path's identical failure mode, and it never blocks a legitimate first PR. Deliberately checks **open** PRs only: a closed/rejected PR means the fix was declined, and #835 is separately covered by open PR #833. **Verification:** `bash -n` + `shellcheck -S warning` clean (0); the real helper tested in isolation against the reconstructed incident snapshot (PR #836 open → extracts `835` → guard **ABORTS** the would-be duplicate) and edge cases (empty/transient-fail snapshot → proceed; unrelated PR → proceed; substring safety: comma-bracketing means issue `83` does **not** match `835` → proceed); full `agent/self-test.sh` **138 pass / 0 fail / 1 warn** (the intentional `.gnupg` #737 tripwire, Pavel-gated), grade A, security A 95/100. Did **not** run `fix-issues.sh` live mid-session (it spends a Claude call, mutates git, and creates real PRs on the every-2h cron); the guard is pure control flow + a validated read-only helper, applied by the next scheduled run. Only `agent/fix-issues.sh` changed. **Next time:** the input-side fragility remains (a transient `github_list_prs` failure at line 88 still silently disables input dedup for that run) — the output guard now catches its consequence, but distinguishing "no open PRs" from "fetch failed" at the source would be a cleaner belt-and-suspenders; and the secondary HTTP 422 on `github_create_issue` (error field `name` — a label-validation failure in github-interact) is now diagnosable via the recent `.errors[]` logging but was left for a focused pass since it was a one-off, not a recurring code defect.
- **A transient GitHub 503/network hiccup no longer logs at `[ERROR]` or aborts the run — `github_check_token()` now retries and degrades to `WARN`** (`agent/lib/github.sh`) — Weekly-session finding: this week's only ERROR/CRITICAL lines were three isolated `[ERROR] GitHub token validation failed (HTTP 503)` on 2026-07-16 (22:25 / 22:35 / 23:35 UTC, one each in `fix-issues.sh` and two `hourly-check.sh` cycles), every one of which recovered on the very next cron cycle — the signature of a brief upstream GitHub outage, **not** a bad token. Yet `github_check_token()` did a **single** `curl` with no retry and logged *any* non-200 — including transient `5xx` and curl connection failures (`000`) — at `[ERROR]`, even though the core `github_api()` right below it already retries. So a ~30-second GitHub wobble inflated the ERROR/CRITICAL count, fed `log-alerting.sh` (it raised a "Persistent warning" alert that auto-resolved), and read on the dashboard/exports as "Marvin is broken" when nothing of ours was. This is the GitHub-token analog of the `run_claude` exit-1 sweep (#814/#821/#823/#824): a transient external failure should degrade gracefully, not escalate. **Fix:** the check now retries up to 3× (3s backoff) on `5xx`/`000`, and classifies by severity — a transient upstream failure (after retries) logs `WARN` ("will retry next cycle"); a genuine auth failure (`4xx`, e.g. an expired/revoked token) keeps `ERROR`, because that one *is* ours to fix. The return-code contract is unchanged (`0` on success, `1` on failure), so every caller's existing behaviour is preserved: `hourly-check.sh` / `morning-check.sh` still take their graceful "skip issue fetch" branch, `github-interact.sh` still skips, `fix-issues.sh` still exits its cycle — they just no longer do so off the back of a spurious ERROR. The happy path is byte-identical: a valid token returns 200 on the first `curl` with no retry and no sleep. **Verification:** `bash -n` + `shellcheck -S warning` clean (0); logic unit-tested across all five cases (200 → rc 0, single curl; 503 → 3× WARN, rc 1, no ERROR; 000 → 3× WARN, rc 1; 401 → 1× ERROR, rc 1; empty token → ERROR, rc 1); live happy-path confirmed against the real PAT (rc 0, single curl). Only `agent/lib/github.sh` changed — no cron, runtime, or web side effects. **Follow-up (folds in #835):** the new retry loop's `curl` is now time-bounded with `--connect-timeout 10 --max-time 20`, so a stalled/half-open connection times out to `000` (already handled as transient) rather than hanging — otherwise the 3× loop could compound an unbounded hang and block a cron-triggered run. This is the same fix PR #836 proposed against the *pre-retry* single-`curl` form; folding it here (where the retry loop actually lives) resolves #835 at its real source and lets #836 be retired instead of racing this PR over the same lines. Verified: `bash -n` + `shellcheck -S warning` clean; happy path still 200 on first bounded curl. **Also bounds `github_api()`** — its own 3× retry loop issued an unbounded `curl` too, so the same stalled-connection hang could compound there; added `--connect-timeout 10 --max-time 30` to its `curl_args`, folding in the one extra improvement the issue-fixer's duplicate PRs #837/#838 carried so both can be closed against this single PR rather than conflicting over `github_check_token`'s curl.

- **Weekly analytics "Pending CVEs" reflects reality instead of always 0, and the orphaned `cve-monitor.sh` can no longer clobber the shared CVE report** (`agent/weekly-analytics.sh`, `agent/cve-monitor.sh`) — Two related `cve-status.json` integrity fixes on a clean day (in sync with origin; 0 failures in today's log — the only WARN was the 02:04 scan's benign `4 actionable` pending security updates, all four openssh/libssh2 packages applied by 06:01; the three open issues #826/#827/#828 all already have PRs in flight and were deliberately left untouched to avoid duplicate/competing PRs). **(1) Data-correctness bug (primary):** `weekly-analytics.sh:206` read `.security_updates_available` from `cve-status.json` — a field produced by **no writer anywhere in the codebase**. `security-scan.sh` (the active daily owner of that file) writes the pending-security count as `.upgradable_security_actionable` / `.upgradable_security`. So the read returned `null` → `0` every week, and the weekly report's `pending_cves` JSON field and `**Pending CVEs:** N` markdown line have silently shown `0` regardless of the real number since the reader was written. **Fix:** read `.upgradable_security_actionable // .upgradable_security // 0`, mirroring the proven fallback `self-test.sh` already uses for the same file. Verified against today's live `cve-status.json`: old read → `0`, new read → `4` (matching the 02:04 scan's "4 actionable" WARN). **(2) Clobber landmine (secondary):** `agent/cve-monitor.sh` is orphaned — invoked by nothing (not cron, not `security-scan.sh`), despite a header falsely claiming *"Cron: integrated into daily security-scan.sh"* and *"Can run standalone or be called from security-scan.sh"*. It writes a **different, richer schema** to the *same* `cve-status.json` that `security-scan.sh` owns, so a single manual run would corrupt the file that `self-test.sh` §9c and `weekly-analytics.sh` read. **Fix (non-destructive, reversible):** repointed its output to a separate `cve-status-standalone.json` so it can never overwrite the shared report, and rewrote the header to state honestly that it is a standalone/experimental tool not wired into cron. The script is left in place (it's still listed as a capability in `capability-inventory.sh`); only its output target and misleading docs changed. **Verification:** `bash -n` + `shellcheck -S warning` clean (0) on both files; field fix demonstrated against live data (0 → 4); confirmed no remaining writer/reader of `cve-status.json` was affected (`security-scan.sh` still owns it, `self-test.sh` §9c still validates it, `weekly-analytics.sh` now reads the correct field); full `agent/self-test.sh` **137 pass / 0 fail / 1 warn** (the intentional `.gnupg` #737 tripwire, Pavel-gated), grade A, security A 90/100. Did **not** run `weekly-analytics.sh` (Sundays 11:30 UTC) or `cve-monitor.sh` live mid-session — both are pure read-path / output-target changes validated in isolation against the live file, applied by their next scheduled runs. **Next time:** `capability-inventory.sh:85` still points the "CVE Monitoring" capability at the orphaned `cve-monitor.sh` rather than the active `security-scan.sh` — a truthful pointer would better reflect where the capability actually lives; deferred to keep scope tight. Long-standing carry-overs unchanged — split `agent/security-scan.sh` (~830 LOC) into `agent/lib/security/*.sh`; Phase 3 ECHO/gossip items (parked); Pavel-gated `.gnupg/` cleanup (#737).

- **Per-day comms `log-analysis-*.json` files now have a retention policy — closing a 13 MB unbounded-growth gap** (`agent/disk-cleanup.sh`) — `log-watcher.sh` writes one `data/comms/log-analysis-YYYY-MM-DD.json` per day (Claude's classification of that day's incoming signals / attack attempts). Every consumer — `log-watcher.sh`, `evening-report.sh`, `update-website.sh` — reads only `${TODAY}`'s file by exact constructed name; nothing reads a historical date, `/api/comms/` is `deny all` (not dashboard- or export-served), and `log-export.sh` does not bundle them. Yet **no** disk-cleanup section matched them: section 6c's COMMS_DIR sweep only catches `log-analysis-raw-*.txt` / `pending-log-review.txt`, and sections 3/4/5 don't cover COMMS_DIR `.json`. So the family grew one file/day since 2026-02-28 — **137 files / 13 MB** by 2026-07-15. This was explicitly deferred on 2026-06-26 as "actively consumed (dashboard)", but that was a conflation with the *separate* `data/logs/analysis-*.json` family; a full grep across `agent/`, `web/`, `setup/`, plus confirming the `/api/comms/` 403 and that no `log-analysis-latest.json` pointer exists in COMMS_DIR, proves nothing reads a historical comms file. **Fix:** new section 6d mirroring the section-5 policy — compress `log-analysis-????-??-??.json` at >30 days, delete the `.gz` at >180 days — bounding growth while keeping a 180-day compressed forensic history of AI-contact/attack classifications (never deleting logging data outright, per the security rules). The `????-??-??`-anchored glob never matches today's live file, the `raw-*.txt` dumps 6c already handles, or any `*-latest` pointer; the misleading "consumed by the dashboard" comment in 6c is corrected to point at 6d. **Verification:** `bash -n` + `shellcheck -S warning` clean (0); empirical find-selection confirmed 105 of 137 files are >30d (9.6 MB) with today's file excluded and zero `raw-`/`latest` false matches; `--dry-run` reports "Compressed 105 comms log-analysis JSON (>30d)" (~4.8 M estimated, real gzip savings higher) with **zero mutation** (137 `.json` intact, 0 `.gz` created); full `agent/self-test.sh` 137 pass / 0 fail / 1 warn (the intentional `.gnupg` #737 tripwire), grade A, security A 90/100. Did not run the live (non-dry-run) cleanup mid-session — it mutates `data/` and runs from the 06:00 UTC cron; selection is pure `find` filtering validated read-only, next scheduled run applies it safely. Only `agent/disk-cleanup.sh` changed.

- **`security-scan.sh` SUID/SGID count no longer double-outputs and corrupts `latest-scan.json`** (`agent/security-scan.sh:130`) — Latent `find … | wc -l || echo 0` double-output landmine, the exact `grep-c-double-output` anti-pattern class the 2026-05-01 sweep fixed at six other sites in this same file but missed here. Under `set -o pipefail`, if `find` exits non-zero (a missing path, or EACCES on an unreadable dir — e.g. inside a container, or if `/usr/libexec` is ever dropped) the pipeline fails, so `|| echo 0` fires *in addition to* `wc` having already printed the real count, yielding `"N\n0"`. That value is spliced straight into the scan JSON at `"suid_sgid_count": ${suid_count}` (line ~746), producing invalid JSON that would corrupt `latest-scan.json` — read by `self-test.sh §9c`, `incident-report.sh`, and the dashboard. **Latent, not live:** on this root-run, all-paths-present host `find` exits 0 and the fallback never fires (verified: `suid_count=24`, single value, unchanged). **Fix:** the lesson's canonical capture-then-fallback — `suid_count=$(find … | wc -l || true); suid_count=${suid_count:-0}` — keeps `wc`'s count and swallows only the exit code, so a failing `find` yields a clean single `"N"` instead of `"N\n0"`. **Verification:** reproduced the corruption directly (OLD form on a forced find-failure → `"10\n0"` / 4 bytes; NEW → `"10"` / single value; NEW happy path → `24`, identical to current output); `bash -n` + `shellcheck -S warning` clean; full `agent/self-test.sh` 137 pass / 0 fail / 1 warn (the intentional `.gnupg` #737 tripwire, Pavel-gated), grade A, security A 90/100, all config-sync tripwires green. Did **not** run `security-scan.sh` live mid-session (it runs rkhunter/chkrootkit and mutates `data/` from the 02:00 cron); the change is isolated control flow validated in isolation, and the next scheduled scan applies it. Only `agent/security-scan.sh` changed.
- **Grep-in-command-substitution no longer crashes three scripts on a non-match** (`agent/github-interact.sh`, `agent/backup.sh`, `agent/enhancement-tracker.sh`) — Same `set -euo pipefail` + `marvin_error_trap` ERR-trap crash class as the recent `run_claude` sweep, but via a different mechanism: a field-extraction `VAR=$(echo "$x" | grep -oP '…' | head -1)` where `grep` finds no match exits 1, `pipefail` propagates it, the command substitution fails, and the ERR trap + `set -e` abort the script **before** the empty-value guard on the very next line ever runs. **Observed in production:** `github-interact.sh:236` crashed a real run on 2026-07-11 20:10 UTC (a malformed `===ISSUE===` block with no `title:` line), killing the remaining COMMENT/CLOSE parse phases and the summary. **Fix:** appended `|| true` to the four github-interact block-field extractions (`title`/`labels`/`issue`/`reason`, lines 236/237/270/305) and to the two date-from-filename extractions whose own `[[ -z ]]`/`[[ -n ]]` guards were already written to handle empty but were unreachable on the crash path (`backup.sh:271` backup-rotation date, `enhancement-tracker.sh:47` report-date grouping). All three scripts keep their downstream empty-value handling; a non-match now yields an empty string instead of a crash. Verified: `bash -n` + `shellcheck -S warning` clean on all three; a `set -euo pipefail` + ERR-trap reproduction confirms OLD crashes (exit 1, loop aborts mid-iteration) and NEW survives (empty value, downstream guard prunes it) while the matching case still extracts correctly; full self-test 137 pass / 0 fail / 1 warn (the intentional `.gnupg` #737 tripwire), grade A.

- **`negotiate-handler.sh` survives a transient Claude exit 1 instead of crashing the negotiation session** (`agent/negotiate-handler.sh`) — The last remaining bare `raw_output=$(run_claude ...)` call site (line 173), missed by the run_claude crash sweep that guarded `hourly-check.sh` / `morning-check.sh` / `github-interact.sh` / `evening-report.sh` / `network-discovery.sh` (#814) / `self-enhance.sh` (#821) / `weekly-enhance.sh` / `fix-issues.sh` (#823). The call sits inside the per-request negotiation loop under `set -euo pipefail` + `trap marvin_error_trap ERR` (lines 8/10), so a transient Claude exit 1 (`claude-exit-code-1-transient`) or a lock timeout (exit 2 on the `*/15` cron overlap) fired the ERR trap and killed the **whole** negotiation session mid-loop — dropping every still-unprocessed peer request, not just the failing one. **Fix:** capture the exit code (`&& CLAUDE_RC=0 || CLAUDE_RC=$?`) and, on any non-zero exit, log (INFO for exit 2 lock timeout, WARN otherwise) and `continue` to the next request, **leaving `"$request_file"` in place** so the next `*/15` cron cycle retries the skipped request — unlike the parse-failure branch below, a transient infra failure isn't the request's fault, so it shouldn't be discarded. Mirrors the merged guard pattern; the `A && B || C` form always assigns `CLAUDE_RC` before it's read, so it's `set -u`-safe. Verified: `bash -n` clean. Found during the 2026-07-12 morning check — the outlier a prior session's memory claimed to have filed an issue for, though no such issue existed on GitHub. **Follow-up (addressing the bot review's non-blocking suggestion):** the per-IP daily rate counter (`MAX_PER_IP_PER_DAY=5`) was previously incremented *before* the Claude call, so once transient failures became a retry-in-place path (this fix) a peer hitting flaky infra would burn one budget slot per `*/15` retry rather than per genuine request. Moved the increment into a `_bump_rate` helper called *only* at the three consume points that `rm -f "$request_file"` (keyword rejection, unparseable response, successful answer) — never on the paths that leave the request in the inbox for a retry (Claude unavailable / transient exit). Net effect: infra flakiness no longer counts against a peer's budget, while genuine rejections and answers still do (an attacker spamming dangerous-keyword requests is still charged). The helper reassigns the loop-scoped `rate_limits` accumulator, so multi-request batches from one IP still accumulate correctly within a run. Verified: `bash -n` + `shellcheck -S warning` clean; simulated `_bump_rate` accumulation (two hits on one IP → count 2, distinct IP → count 1, both dated today).
- **`weekly-enhance.sh` / `fix-issues.sh` survive a transient Claude exit 1 instead of crashing the session** (`agent/weekly-enhance.sh`, `agent/fix-issues.sh`) — The last two `OUTPUT=$(run_claude ...)` call sites still using the bare form (flagged in #822, same bug class fixed for `self-enhance.sh` in #821 and earlier for `hourly-check.sh` / `morning-check.sh` / `github-interact.sh` / `evening-report.sh` / `network-discovery.sh`). Under `set -euo pipefail` + the `marvin_error_trap` ERR trap, a transient Claude exit 1 (`claude-exit-code-1-transient`) or a lock timeout (exit 2) fired the trap and killed the script mid-run, before graceful skip / validation / rollback could run. **Fix:** `weekly-enhance.sh` (Sundays 12:00 UTC only — a crash loses a *full week* of deep enhancement, the highest-cost call site) now uses `run_claude_with_retry "weekly-deep-enhance" "$DEEP_PROMPT" 2` + graceful `exit 0`, mirroring `morning-check.sh`; `fix-issues.sh` (every 2h — the next cron cycle is a cheap retry) captures the exit code (`&& CLAUDE_RC=0 || CLAUDE_RC=$?`) and skips gracefully, mirroring `hourly-check.sh`, including the `hourly-check.sh` precedent's explicit exit-2 branch (lock held by another task → INFO, not a WARN that reads like a failure). The pre-run `git stash` checkpoint (weekly) and the `EXIT` `cleanup()` trap that returns to clean `main` (fix-issues) mean the early `exit 0` strands nothing the existing paths don't already. Verified: `bash -n` + `shellcheck -S warning` clean on both. Closes #822.
- **Self-enhance no longer crashes its own session on a transient Claude exit 1 — retries then skips gracefully** (`agent/self-enhance.sh`) — Yesterday's own self-enhance run died: `[2026-07-10T08:00:11Z] [ERROR] self-enhance.sh:214 — command failed (exit 1): OUTPUT=$(run_claude "self-enhance" "$FULL_PROMPT")`. The call site was a bare `OUTPUT=$(run_claude ...)` under `set -euo pipefail` + `trap marvin_error_trap ERR`, so a transient Claude exit 1 (the documented, self-resolving `claude-exit-code-1-transient` condition) fired the ERR trap and `set -e` killed the session **before** the post-enhance validation/rollback logic could run — losing the entire day's enhancement. This is the exact bug class already fixed for `hourly-check.sh` (2026-06-12), long handled in `github-interact.sh`, and in-flight for `network-discovery.sh` (PR #814); `self-enhance.sh` was the remaining once-a-day outlier. **Fix:** mirror the sibling once-a-day tasks `morning-check.sh` / `evening-report.sh` — call `run_claude_with_retry "self-enhance" "$FULL_PROMPT" 2` (2 retries, escalating 15s/60s backoff) capturing the exit code with `|| CLAUDE_EXIT=$?`, then on any non-zero exit log a WARN and `exit 0` cleanly. A transient exit 1 is rejected before Claude does any work, so no changes are applied and there is nothing to roll back; the next daily run retries. Exit 2 (lock timeout) is also caught by the same `-ne 0` guard → graceful skip. The happy path (exit 0) is unchanged: `OUTPUT` still carries Claude's output straight into `_validate_post_enhance`. **Not weakening the rollback safety net** — the validation/rollback below is untouched; this only prevents the crash *before* it. **Verification:** `bash -n` + `shellcheck -S warning` clean (0); a `set -euo pipefail` + ERR-trap reproduction confirmed the OLD form crashes before validation on exit 1 **and** exit 2 (matching the log) while the NEW form survives (exit 0) for exit 0/1/2 with the happy path reaching validation with output intact; full `agent/self-test.sh` 136 pass / 0 fail / 1 warn (the intentional `.gnupg` #737 tripwire, Pavel-gated), grade A, security A 95/100. Only `agent/self-enhance.sh` changed. **Next time:** a tiny self-test that greps for bare `OUTPUT=$(run_claude ...)` call sites (no exit-code capture) would catch this positional class structurally rather than by a crash in the logs.
- **certbot deploy hook reloads TLS services after renewal — dovecot no longer serves a stale in-memory cert** (`setup/letsencrypt-deploy-hook.sh` new, `setup/bootstrap.sh`, `agent/self-test.sh`) — Root-cause fix for the day's flagged signal: `IMAPS SSL cert expires in 13 days` logged **28× between 15:55–18:05 UTC on 2026-07-08**, then stopped. Investigation showed the on-disk cert had been healthy all along (renewed 2026-06-22, valid until 2026-09-20 = 73 days), but **dovecot — running since 2026-06-21 and never reloaded after the renewal — kept its old in-memory SSL context and served the stale Apr-23 cert** (`fullchain2`, expiring ~2026-07-22 = "13 days" from 07-08). HTTPS (nginx/443) and SMTPS (postfix/465) never warned: postfix re-reads the cert per short-lived `smtpd` worker, and nginx has its own reload path — only dovecot cached it. The `/etc/letsencrypt/renewal-hooks/deploy/` directory was **empty**, so nothing guaranteed any TLS service would pick up a renewal; the stale dovecot cert would have actually **expired on ~2026-07-22** had a manual reload not happened to fire at 20:08 UTC (after which the WARN stopped). **Fix:** added the documented, deterministic remedy — a certbot **deploy hook** (`reload-services.sh`) that runs after any renewal and gracefully reloads dovecot, postfix, and nginx (belt-and-suspenders). Best-effort by contract: each service is reloaded only if active, nginx config is `nginx -t`-validated before reload (a bad config can't take the web server down as a renewal side effect), and the hook always `exit 0` so a single reload failure can't mark the already-issued renewal as failed. Version-controlled in `setup/`, installed live to `/etc/letsencrypt/renewal-hooks/deploy/reload-services.sh` (mode 0755), wired into `bootstrap.sh` (installed right after certbot, independent of cert-issuance success), and guarded by a new `self-test.sh` §9d source⇆live drift check (`config in sync: letsencrypt-deploy-hook`) so a rebuild reproduces it and any hand-edit is caught. **Verification:** `bash -n` + `shellcheck -S warning` clean (0) on the hook, bootstrap, and self-test; ran the installed hook end-to-end with synthetic `RENEWED_*` env — all three services reloaded gracefully (zero-downtime SIGHUP), and port 993 now serves the fresh 73-day cert; live copy byte-identical to source; full `agent/self-test.sh` **137 pass / 0 fail / 1 warn** (the intentional `.gnupg` #737 tripwire, Pavel-gated), grade A, security A 90/100. Did **not** wait for a real renewal (next is ~73 days out) — the hook is validated in isolation and the drift check keeps it honest until then.
- **Daily digest stops counting `session_limit` throttles as Claude errors + surfaces a throttle count** (`agent/daily-digest.sh`) — Completes the consistency sweep started for the session-limit classification. `log-alerting.sh` §6, `weekly-analytics.sh`, and `perf-analytics.py` were all taught to exclude benign `fail_reason == "session_limit"` throttles from their failure counts, but `daily-digest.sh` was missed — it still computed `claude_usage.error_runs` (and the per-task `errors`) as `select(.exit_code != 0)`, so on any throttle day the daily digest (written under `data/logs/`, served at `/api/logs/digest-latest.json`) over-reported Claude errors: a false signal on the public export surface that disagreed with every other consumer of the same JSONL. **Fix:** both digest error counts now use the identical `select(.exit_code != 0 and (.fail_reason // "") != "session_limit")` filter, so the digest agrees with the alerting/weekly/perf definition of a failure. The `(.fail_reason // "")` guard keeps pre-classification rows (null/absent field) counted as errors, so no genuine historical failure is hidden. **Observability follow-up (from the 2026-07-05 weekly "Next time"):** added a `claude_usage.session_limit_throttles` field to the digest — throttle visibility *without paging*, exactly the "surface a low-key session_limit occurrence count" the weekly session asked for. Deliberately did **not** add a run_claude()-level backoff (the second "Next time" idea): that would touch the shared Claude entry path and risk suppressing legitimate once-a-day runs (morning-check / evening-report) — proposed but not applied, per the "if a change seems risky, propose it but don't apply it" rule. Verified: `bash -n` + `shellcheck -S warning` clean; filter unit-tested on a mixed dataset (1 ok + 1 error + 2 session-limit + 1 legacy no-field → 2 genuine errors, 2 throttles; old filter → 4 errors, the over-count removed); end-to-end run against live 2026-07-05/06 data produced valid single-doc JSON with the new field; `digest-latest.json` repointed back to the true-latest day after the manual runs; full `self-test.sh` 136 pass / 0 fail / 1 warn (intentional `.gnupg` #737 tripwire), security A 90/100.
- **Automated PR review workflow can now actually post its review comment** (`.github/workflows/claude-review.yml`) — Requested by Pavel (sole codeowner) on PR #814: *"Claude Code will need a permission to write comments, please fix this."* The `claude-review` GitHub Action ran to a `success` conclusion on #814 but **posted no comment** — the run log shows it stalled on its very first step: `gh pr view 814 --json …` returned `Error: This command requires approval`, because the `--allowedTools` list only permitted `Bash(gh pr comment:*)`, `Bash(gh pr review:*)`, and `Bash(gh pr diff:*)` — **not** `gh pr view`. Running headless in CI there is no human to grant that approval, so the review ended with *"I need your approval to run `gh pr view`"* and never reached the comment-posting step (`No buffered inline comments`). The action's Bash parser additionally rejects any piped/compound command (`… | head`, `cat …; gh …`) with *"This Bash command contains multiple operations. The following part requires approval"*, so even the fallback attempts failed. **Fix:** (1) added the `mcp__github__get_pull_request` tool so the reviewer can read PR metadata without an interactive prompt; (2) re-pointed the comment write from `gh pr comment` to the structured `mcp__github__add_issue_comment` tool — an MCP tool call bypasses the Bash compound-command approval parser entirely, so the comment posts reliably in headless mode; (3) the prompt now tells the reviewer it runs headless, to prefer the MCP read/post tools, and never to pipe or chain shell commands (each part of a compound command needs its own un-grantable approval). Job-level `permissions:` already granted `pull-requests: write` + `issues: write`, so the token was never the blocker — the tool allowlist was. **Verification:** `python3 -c 'import yaml; yaml.safe_load(...)'` parses clean and confirms the `--allowedTools` string is intact. Pushing this to the #814 branch retriggers the workflow on `synchronize`, so the fix is validated live by whether the next run posts a review comment on the PR itself. **Least-privilege follow-up (from the #814 automated review):** dropped `Bash(gh pr comment:*)` and `Bash(gh pr review:*)` from `--allowedTools` — the reviewer now writes only through `mcp__github__add_issue_comment` (the prompt already forbids `gh pr comment`), so these residual Bash *write* scopes were dead weight and a path a prompt-injected PR diff could otherwise try to exploit to post/approve directly. Read-only `gh pr diff` remains as the sole Bash fallback (the `Bash(gh pr view:*)` entry added in an earlier iteration of this fix was dropped in a later cleanup as unreferenced — PR metadata now comes solely from `mcp__github__get_pull_request`). **Second least-privilege pass (from the follow-up #814 automated review):** dropped `mcp__github__list_pull_requests` — a single-PR reviewer has no reason to enumerate every PR, and it was allowlisted but never referenced in the prompt. Kept `mcp__github__list_issues` but *wired it into the prompt*: the reviewer now checks for an existing open issue with `list_issues` before it opens a new one with `create_issue`, so it can't file a duplicate for a repeat Critical/Warning finding — turning a dead-weight allowlist entry into a purposeful dedup step. Every remaining allowlisted tool is now referenced by the prompt. **PR-number discoverability (folds in #816):** the reviewer prompt now opens with `Review pull request #${{ github.event.pull_request.number }} in ${{ github.repository }}` and tells the reviewer to use that interpolated number for every `gh pr …` / `mcp__github__*` call — fixing the detached-HEAD failure (`actions/checkout` leaves `pull_request` runs on a detached HEAD, so a bare `gh pr view` / `gh pr diff` with no number fails with *"could not determine current branch"*, and `mcp__github__get_pull_request` can't be called because nothing supplied a `pullNumber`) that #814's own automated review raised as a 🟠 Warning. This lands the fix in the PR that already edits this file's prompt block, superseding the standalone PR #817.

- **Claude "session limit" throttles no longer counted as "Claude API failures"** (`agent/lib/claude.sh`, `agent/log-alerting.sh`) — On 2026-07-04, five Claude runs (`log-analysis` ×3, `hourly-check`, `github-interact`) between 13:00–14:05 UTC exited with code 1 and a 59-char body: `You've hit your session limit · resets 4:30pm (Europe/Rome)`. That is a benign, **self-resolving subscription throttle** — the runs recovered on their own at 14:30 UTC the instant the usage window rolled over — but `log-alerting.sh` section 6 counts every `exit_code != 0` as a genuine failure, so it raised the `Claude API failures (3/50 runs)` warning alert (which then auto-resolved overnight). The usage JSONL recorded only `exit_code`, so the alerting layer had no way to tell a throttle apart from a real API/tooling error without digging into per-run `.md` logs. **Fix:** `run_claude()` now classifies a non-zero exit by inspecting the response — `session_limit` when it matches `(session|usage) limit`, else `error` — and records it as a new nullable `fail_reason` field in `claude-usage-*.jsonl` (plus a clear INFO line: *"Claude session/usage limit reached … benign, resets automatically"*). `log-alerting.sh` section 6 now excludes `fail_reason == "session_limit"` from the failure count, so a throttle window can no longer trip the API-failure alert. The generic `[WARN] Claude exited with code N for task:` line is deliberately unchanged — `lessons-learned.sh` keys its `claude-exit-code-1-transient` expected-recurrence lesson on that exact string. Backward-compatible: the `(.fail_reason // "")` guard means pre-classification entries (no field → null) are still counted, so no real historical failure is hidden. **Consistency follow-up (from PR #813 review):** the same exclusion is now applied in `weekly-analytics.sh` (overall + per-task error counts and `error_rate_pct`) and `perf-analytics.py` (`analyze_claude` error count), so the daily alert and the weekly/perf error-rate metrics all agree on what counts as a failure — otherwise the weekly metric would silently re-introduce the exact throttle miscount this fix removes from the alert. Both use the identical `fail_reason == "session_limit"` guard with the same pre-classification fallback (jq `(.fail_reason // "")`; Python `r.get("fail_reason")`). Verified: `bash -n` + `shellcheck -S warning` clean; classification unit-tested (session-limit / API-500 / usage-limit strings); alerting filter tested against a mixed dataset (3 session-limit + 1 error + 1 legacy no-field → 2 genuine, no alert; old filter → 5, would have alerted); the weekly (jq) and perf (python) exclusions independently re-tested against the same mixed dataset → 2 errors each (session-limit excluded, legacy no-field counted); full `self-test.sh` 136 pass / 0 fail / 1 warn (intentional `.gnupg` #737 tripwire), security A 90/100.

- **`network-discovery.sh` no longer crashes mid-run on a transient Claude `exit 1` — captures the exit code and skips the analysis gracefully while still running peer trust scoring** (`agent/network-discovery.sh`) — Fix-failure target from the 2026-07-02 log: `[ERROR] network-discovery.sh:220 — command failed (exit 1): OUTPUT=$(run_claude "network-discovery" ...)`. Section 4 (communication-strategy analysis) invoked Claude with a bare `OUTPUT=$(run_claude ...)`. Under `set -euo pipefail` + `trap marvin_error_trap ERR`, a transient Claude `exit 1` (the documented, intermittently-recurring `claude-exit-code-1-transient` condition — and also the benign session-limit throttle that #813 addresses on the alerting side) made the command-substitution's non-zero status fire the ERR trap, and `set -e` killed the script **before section 5 (peer trust scoring) and the public registry regeneration could run**. Every other Claude-calling script already guards this — `github-interact.sh`, `hourly-check.sh`, `log-watcher.sh`, `morning-check.sh`, `evening-report.sh` — `network-discovery.sh` was the lone remaining outlier, and it crashed on 2026-07-02 (a transient exit 1). Verified the crash directly: a top-level `OUTPUT=$(failing)` under `set -euo pipefail` + ERR trap fires the trap and exits 1 (`SURVIVED` never prints), matching the logged `:220 — command failed (exit 1)`. **Fix:** mirrored the established handler — `export CLAUDE_LOCK_TIMEOUT=60` then `OUTPUT=$(run_claude ...) && CLAUDE_RC=0 || CLAUDE_RC=$?`; write the analysis only on `CLAUDE_RC == 0 && non-empty output`, log an INFO on `CLAUDE_RC == 2` (lock timeout on cron overlap), and a WARN otherwise. Crucially, **unlike** github-interact/hourly-check (where the Claude call *is* the task and they `exit 0`), here the Claude call is only section 4 of a larger script — so on failure it **skips writing the analysis but falls through** to trust scoring and registry generation rather than exiting, preserving the day's peer-observability work. **Verification:** `bash -n` + `shellcheck -S warning` clean (0); an isolation harness confirmed the new form reaches "section 5" on all three cases (exit 1 → WARN+continue, exit 2 → INFO+continue, exit 0 → analysis written+continue) while the old top-level form crashes on exit 1; full `agent/self-test.sh` 136 pass / 0 fail / 1 warn (the intentional `.gnupg` #737 tripwire, Pavel-gated), grade A, security A 95/100, all config-sync tripwires green. Did **not** run `network-discovery.sh` live mid-session (it makes outbound beacon fetches, fires the once-daily SSH probe, spends a Claude call, and contends for the lock); the change is pure exit-code control flow validated in isolation, applied by the next 16:00 UTC cron run. Only `agent/network-discovery.sh` changed. **Clarity follow-up (from the #814 automated review):** added an explicit `CLAUDE_RC == 0` branch for the empty-output case — it now logs an INFO (*"Claude returned empty output — nothing to log"*) instead of falling through to the `else` and logging a misleading *"Claude exit 0 — skipping analysis"* WARN that read as if exit 0 were a failure.
- **Security scan suspicious-connection check (3b) no longer flags Docker bridge traffic as "unusual remote ports"** (`agent/security-scan.sh`) — On a clean day (in sync with origin, 0 open issues, 0 open PRs; today's only non-expected WARN), `security-scan.sh` section 3b logged `[WARN] Found 2 connection(s) to unusual remote ports`. The two flagged connections were `172.18.0.3:4317` (OTEL-gRPC) and `172.19.0.6:3100` (marvin-brain-mcp) — both `docker-proxy`→container traffic on active Docker bridge networks (`172.18.0.0/16`, `172.19.0.0/16`) from the Marvin-Brain + monitoring stacks, not outbound destinations worth flagging. Section **3d** (the outbound audit) already skips active Docker bridge subnets via `_ip_in_docker_cidr()` (fixes #591) — which is why its `outbound_unexpected` was correctly `0` — but section **3b** only skipped loopback, so it flagged the exact bridge traffic 3d ignores. The inconsistency meant the two checks disagreed about what counts as "outbound", producing intermittent false-positive WARNs whenever the daily scan happened to catch active bridge traffic (transient — only 1 firing today). **Fix:** relocated the shared `_docker_bridges` collection + `_ip_in_docker_cidr()` helper to a single definition *before* section 3b (previously it lived inside 3d, after 3b ran), and added the same Docker-bridge skip to 3b's loop, right after the existing loopback skip. The helper is now defined once and reused by both connection checks instead of being duplicated — so the two can't drift. **Not a monitoring weakening:** it makes the same trust decision 3d already established (#591) — Docker assigns these private RFC1918 subnets and an attacker would need to already control a container or the daemon to place traffic there; genuinely external outbound connections (public IPs) to unusual ports are still flagged. **Verification:** `bash -n` + `shellcheck -S warning` clean (0); the relocated helper, extracted from the live file and tested against today's two flagged IPs + adversarial cases, correctly **skips** `172.18.0.3`/`172.19.0.6`/`172.17.0.5` (active bridges) while still **flagging** `8.8.8.8` (external) and `172.20.0.1` (private but not an active bridge); definitions (lines 212/221) provably precede both usages (3b at 289, 3d at 445), so no reference-before-definition; full `agent/self-test.sh` 136 pass / 0 fail / 1 warn (the intentional `.gnupg` #737 tripwire, Pavel-gated), grade A, security A 95/100. Did **not** run `security-scan.sh` live mid-session (it runs rkhunter/chkrootkit and mutates `data/` from the 02:00/04:00 cron); the 3b classification is validated in isolation against live Docker + connection state, and the next scheduled scan applies it. Only `agent/security-scan.sh` changed (`connections-latest.json` is runtime `data/`). **Next time:** surface the Docker-bridge distinction on the dashboard network panel; the two long-standing carry-overs are unchanged — split `agent/security-scan.sh` (~810 LOC) into `agent/lib/security/*.sh`, and the parked Phase 3 ECHO-protocol items.

### Security

- **`/api/comms-summary.json` now anonymizes IPs at the publication boundary** (`agent/common.sh`, `agent/network-discovery.sh`, `agent/update-website.sh`) — Issue #983. The public endpoint sliced the **private** `data/comms/incoming-signals.json` verbatim. The masked values visible in production were a side effect of how the analysis step happened to write each entry — a convention, not a control — while the private source holds **47 full IPv4 addresses**; any one of them reaching the trailing-10 window published it. `anonymize_ips()` (battle-tested in #70, #271) moved from `network-discovery.sh`, where only that script could reach it, into `common.sh`, and now runs on the slice. Because it is a `sed` pass the result is re-validated as JSON rather than trusted, falling back to `[]` with a WARN. Verified: `203.0.113.77` → `203.0.113.X`; a malformed source file publishes `[]` and exits 0 rather than aborting the run.

- **`anonymize_ips()`'s two IPv6 branches disagreed with each other, and the weaker one leaked a /64** (`agent/common.sh`, `agent/self-test.sh` §1l, `agent/lib/self-test-sections.txt`) — Issue #986, filed against the #983/#984 work above and deliberately deferred there rather than guessed at. The compressed-form rule (`::`) redacted an address entirely; the explicit 8-group form only masked its last 4 groups, leaving the leading `/64` — a standard single-LAN allocation, i.e. one subscriber line — intact. Two spellings of the same address therefore got different protection depending on whether it happened to contain a run of zero groups, and the explicit-form rule was *weaker* than the IPv4 rule three lines below it (which masks to a /24, spanning many subscribers). Fixed by having both branches redact the whole address, matching the compressed branch's existing (and simplest) behaviour rather than attempting to reconstruct a fixed-bit-length prefix (e.g. /32) across every valid IPv6 compression spelling — that reconstruction is exactly the kind of regex surgery that produced the mangling/over-matching bugs the 2026-03 cluster (#263, #264, #267, #270) had to fix in these same patterns. New self-test §1l asserts both spellings of the same address (`2001:0db8:0000:0000:0000:0000:0000:0001` and `2001:db8::1`) now redact identically, plus the exact examples from the issue report; full suite from the branch is 173 total / 165 pass / 0 fail / 8 warn (pre-existing, unrelated: stale `.gnupg` #737 tripwire, live-host nginx config drift, and one unrelated unrecorded section from a concurrently open PR).

- **`anonymize_ips()`'s IPv4 catch-all spared any IP preceded by `/`, so a slash-prefixed IP passed through unredacted** (`agent/common.sh`, `agent/self-test.sh` §1l) — Issue #1003, filed against this PR by the automated reviewer. The generic IPv4 rule excluded matches preceded by `/` so that a User-Agent version string like `Chrome/140.0.0.0` wouldn't get mangled — but that exemption can't tell a version string apart from a path-like field such as `"referrer": "/proxy/203.0.113.77"`; both have an alphanumeric character immediately before the `/`. A real IP sitting after any single `/` (outside `://`, which the two rules above this one already handle) reached the public `comms-summary.json` endpoint verbatim. There's no cheap regex distinction between the two shapes, and this function guards a privacy boundary rather than a cosmetic one, so the exemption is gone outright — both cases now redact, and a stray version string rendering as `Chrome/140.0.0.X` is an accepted, harmless side effect. New case added to self-test §1l (`/proxy/203.0.113.77` → `/proxy/203.0.113.X`); verified directly against the version-string, path, and existing `://`-form cases side by side. Only `agent/common.sh` and `agent/self-test.sh` changed. `bash -n` + `shellcheck -S warning` clean.

### Added

- **`/.well-known/ai-negotiate` answers a `marvin_health_probe` body without creating a negotiation — and deliberately leaves no record** (`agent/negotiate-listener.sh`) — A POST whose JSON body is an object with `marvin_health_probe: true` short-circuits before the inbox write and returns `200 {"status":"alive","probe":true}`. This exists so `network-discovery.sh` can confirm the endpoint is genuinely up before advertising `negotiate_url` in the public beacon, without paying for a Claude call and forging a peer entry in the public negotiation history once a day, forever (#852). **Two properties worth stating out loud, because neither is visible from the endpoint's behaviour:** (1) the branch is reachable by *any* client that reaches nginx, not just loopback — it is deliberately not IP-gated, on the reasoning that an external caller learns nothing the public beacon does not already advertise, whereas gating on a proxy-supplied `X-Real-IP` would buy that non-benefit with a silent failure mode where the beacon quietly stops advertising a perfectly healthy endpoint because a header changed; and (2) **it is the one POST to this endpoint that produces no inbox file and no audit trail**, so "every request to `/.well-known/ai-negotiate` leaves a record in `negotiate-inbox/`" stops being true — do not treat the inbox as a complete request log for this path. Raised as a 🟡 on the PR #847 review; documented rather than changed, because both properties are intentional. **Two further 🟡s from the 09:42Z review, both taken:** the probe response now carries `Access-Control-Allow-Origin: *` like the 202 beside it — no browser caller is expected (the prober is curl), but one endpoint returning two responses that differ in CORS for no stated reason is exactly the kind of detail that reads as deliberate to whoever finds it next; and the socat address is now quoted as a single argument (`"TCP-LISTEN:${PORT},bind=${BIND_ADDR},reuseaddr,fork"`) per the project's quote-your-variables convention — argv-equivalent here since neither value contains whitespace or globs, confirmed by binding the quoted form on a scratch port. **Re-verified after both edits** by driving `--handle` directly with a scratch inbox: probe body → `200`, CORS present, **0** inbox records; real proposal → `202`, CORS present, 1 record; and the four near-miss bodies (`"true"`, `1`, `false`, and a bare `{"probe":true}`) all still take the normal 202 path, so the short-circuit remains pinned to the exact boolean. Also confirmed the cross-PR contract with #851 in *both* directions, since that PR's beacon gate now refuses to probe unless the deployed listener implements the marker: this branch's listener matches the marker grep, `origin/main`'s does not — so the gate opens only once this lands, and stays shut until then.

- **OpenAPI spec now documents the two public peer-discovery endpoints** (`data/openapi.yaml`) — On a genuinely clean day (in sync with origin; today's only WARNs both benign — a legitimate heavier-traffic `Net RX MB = 420 (3.1σ)` full-day anomaly, which is a *monitoring* signal the security rules forbid weakening, and the expected `claude-lock-timeout` cron-overlap skip; no failures to fix). Closed the pre-existing documentation gap the 2026-06-29 entry flagged: `/api/peers/registry.json` (the sanitized public peer registry, live since 2026-04-11) and `/api/peer-health.json` (the peer health-exchange snapshot, live since 2026-04-07) were both served publicly via the `location /api/` → `data/` nginx alias but were **absent from the OpenAPI spec** (`data/openapi.yaml`, served at `/.well-known/openapi.yaml`), so an external AI peer discovering Marvin through the spec could not find the very endpoints designed for peer-to-peer discovery. **Change:** added two `paths` entries (`getPeerRegistry`, `getPeerHealth`, both under the existing `AI Discovery` tag) and two `components/schemas` (`PeerRegistry`, `PeerHealth`) built from the live responses — including the `beacon_status` enum (`no_domain | skipped_cidr | skipped_invalid | skipped_private | unreachable_dns | unreachable_http | reachable_no_json | valid_json`) and the `beacon_summary` count object — matching the spec's existing style verbatim. Bumped `info.version` 1.0.0 → 1.1.0 (additive, backward-compatible; no consumer pins the string). Both endpoints are already sanitized (no IPs, operator notes, or internal trust breakdowns), so documenting them exposes nothing new — it only makes the existing public surface discoverable. **Verification:** the spec parses as valid YAML (`yaml.safe_load`), openapi 3.0.3; both new paths + both new schemas present; all `$ref`s resolve to defined schemas; no duplicate `operationId`s; endpoint paths and JSON shapes confirmed against live `curl` responses (registry protocol `marvin-peer-registry` v1.2, 16 peers; peer-health protocol `marvin-peer-health/1.0`). Only `data/openapi.yaml` changed — a tracked spec file (explicitly whitelisted in `.gitignore`, not runtime data), no `.sh` script, no web rebuild, no cron/runtime side effects, and no overlap with any of the 5 in-flight PRs (#827–#831). **Next time:** surface these peer endpoints (and the `beacon_status` reachability badge) on the dashboard peer panel — deferred as a web change requiring a rebuild; the long-standing carry-overs are unchanged (split `agent/security-scan.sh` into `agent/lib/security/*.sh`; parked Phase 3 ECHO/gossip items; Pavel-gated `.gnupg/` cleanup #737).
- **Daily digest surfaces session-limit throttles at the log + per-task layer** (`agent/daily-digest.sh`) — Completes the throttle-observability follow-up the 2026-07-07 session left open ("`session_limit_throttles` is queryable via the export API but not yet visualised") at the safest layer, without a dashboard rebuild. Two additive changes: (1) the human-readable **completion log line** now appends `, N session-limit throttle(s)` **only when N > 0**, so a clean day's line stays byte-identical (no new noise) while a throttle day stands out to an operator grepping `data/logs/`; and (2) each `claude_usage.by_task` entry now carries a `throttles` count symmetric with the existing per-task `errors`, so the digest JSON shows *which* high-frequency task (e.g. `log-analysis`, 44 runs/day) happened to run inside a session-limit window rather than only a global total. Both are additive and backward-compatible — the global `session_limit_throttles` field (from #815) is unchanged, no consumer reads the new per-task field yet, and older digest JSON without it stays valid. A numeric-regex guard (`=~ ^[0-9]+$`) keeps a non-integer `claude_throttles` (jq failure → `echo 0` fallback) from ever tripping a `[[ -gt ]]` syntax error under `set -euo pipefail`. **Deliberately not done:** the dashboard badge itself (a web change means a `marvin_rebuild_web()` rebuild for a value that is 0 on healthy days — poor risk/reward on a clean day) and the `run_claude()`-level backoff (touches the shared Claude entry path; still proposed-not-applied per the "if a change seems risky, propose it but don't apply it" rule). **Verification:** `bash -n` + `shellcheck -S warning` clean; the `by_task` jq and the completion-line conditional unit-tested on a synthetic mixed dataset (log-analysis → errors=2 [genuine error + legacy no-field row], throttles=2; hourly-check → errors=0, throttles=1; global throttles=3) and the conditional across `0` / `3` / empty inputs (suffix only for `3`); an end-to-end run against live 2026-07-07 data produced valid single-doc JSON carrying `by_task[].throttles` and a byte-identical completion line (07-07 had 0 throttles), with `digest-latest.json` left at the true-latest date 07-07; full `agent/self-test.sh` 136 pass / 0 fail / 1 warn (the intentional `.gnupg` #737 tripwire, Pavel-gated), grade A, security A 95/100. Only `agent/daily-digest.sh` changed (the regenerated `digest-*.json` are gitignored runtime `data/`). Remaining next-step: the dashboard badge (defer to a web session), and the two open issues #816/#818 are already resolved in the in-flight PR #814 — deliberately not duplicated here.
- **Distinguish phased-deferred security updates from genuinely actionable ones in the daily CVE scan** (`agent/security-scan.sh`, `agent/self-test.sh`) — On a genuinely clean day (in sync with origin, 0 open issues, 0 open PRs), the only recurring log noise is the daily `[WARN] Found N pending security update(s)` from `security-scan.sh`. Investigation showed today's `12` had **already been applied by the time I looked** (the 02:00 snapshot cleared within hours), the count fluctuates day to day (1→17 over the last month), and `apt-get -s upgrade` reports the remaining ones are "deferred due to phasing" — i.e. Ubuntu is rolling the update out to a fraction of machines at a time, unattended-upgrades correctly holds it back until this host's phase is reached, then applies it automatically. So the WARN was firing daily on **benign, self-healing phasing** — and, worse, a *genuinely* stuck security update (unattended-upgrades failing, a dependency-blocked package, a manual hold) would have looked **identical** to routine phasing and been dismissed as noise. **Change 1 (`security-scan.sh` CVE section):** classify each pending `*-security` package as phased-deferred or actionable by intersecting the security-pending set with the phased set parsed from `apt-get -s upgrade` (`awk` on the "deferred due to phasing:" block). The scan now emits a `WARN` only when `actionable > 0` (`"N actionable, M phased-deferred by Ubuntu"`) and a quiet `INFO` when all pending updates are phased (`"all M phased-deferred … will auto-apply"`) — killing the daily false alarm while making a real stuck update stand out. Two additive fields (`upgradable_security_phased`, `upgradable_security_actionable`) are written to both the `cve-status.json` and the `latest-scan.json` `cve_monitoring` block; the existing `upgradable_security` total is untouched. **Change 3 (review follow-up — `overall_status`):** the automated code review of this PR flagged that `overall_status` (~line 700) still gated on the raw `upgradable_security` total, so an all-phased day would log `INFO: all N phased-deferred (will auto-apply)` and then contradict itself a few lines later with `INFO: Overall status: warnings`. Gated it on `upgradable_security_actionable` instead — resolving the self-contradiction; still fail-safe (actionable == total on any parse failure, so a genuinely stuck update still flips the status to `warnings`). **Fail-safe:** any parse failure leaves the phased set empty, so every pending update counts as actionable → WARN — the classifier can never *hide* a security update, only ever fail toward the current loud behavior. The extra `apt-get -s upgrade` simulation runs only when security updates are actually pending. **Change 2 (`self-test.sh` security score):** the `pending_security_updates` dimension now docks −5 only for **actionable** updates (reading `upgradable_security_actionable`, falling back to the total `upgradable_security` for older scan files, then 0) — a phased-deferred update is Ubuntu throttling a rollout, not a hardening deficiency, so penalizing the grade for it is a false penalty (same accuracy spirit as the 2026-06-17 fix that split `rootkit_scan` out of `overall_status`). A clean-except-phased day will now correctly score 95 instead of 90. **Verification:** `bash -n` + `shellcheck -S warning` clean (0) on both files; the classifier unit-tested across 6 synthetic paths (all-phased→INFO, mixed→WARN, empty/parse-fail→all-actionable→WARN, unrelated-phased→WARN) with `phased+actionable == total` in every case; the `self-test` jq fallback verified against new-schema / old-schema / missing-field scan JSON (1 / 0 / 5 / 0 respectively); an **end-to-end run against live apt** correctly split a synthetic `[iproute2 (live-phased), docker-ce (not phased)]` pair into 1 phased / 1 actionable; full `agent/self-test.sh` 136 pass / 0 fail / 1 warn (the intentional `.gnupg` #737 tripwire, Pavel-gated), grade A, security 90/100 (unchanged today — the live scan file predates the new code, so the fallback still docks the current pending updates; the score lifts to 95 automatically on the next all-phased scan). Did **not** run the full `security-scan.sh` live mid-session (it runs rkhunter/chkrootkit and mutates `data/` from 02:00 cron, and no security updates are pending right now to exercise the actionable path); the CVE block is validated in isolation + live-apt end-to-end, and the next scheduled scan applies it. Only the two scripts changed (`cve-status.json` / `latest-scan.json` are runtime `data/`). **Next time:** the security score still reads a single scan file; a future pass could surface the phased/actionable split on the dashboard CVE panel. Long-standing carry-overs unchanged — the orphaned `agent/cve-monitor.sh` (never invoked by cron or `security-scan.sh` despite its header claim, and it writes the same `cve-status.json` with a *different* schema — a latent clobber landmine worth retiring or wiring in properly); split `agent/security-scan.sh` (now ~800 LOC) into `agent/lib/security/*.sh`; Phase 3 ECHO-protocol items; Pavel-gated `.gnupg/` cleanup (#737).

- **Reconciled the live `/.well-known/agent.json` deploy into the committed bootstrap source + tracked the served file** (`setup/nginx-site.conf`, `web/public/.well-known/agent.json`) — Fix-failure target from today's log: `[2026-06-30T02:04:25Z] [WARN] File integrity: CHANGED — /etc/nginx/sites-enabled/marvin` + `File integrity ALERT: 1 changed, 0 missing since baseline (2026-06-25T04:00:08Z)`. This is the same "version-controlled config silently diverged from the running config" class as the 2026-06-07 `/llms.txt` (#777) and 2026-06-19 cron reconciliations. **Root cause:** yesterday evening (file mtime `2026-06-29 18:01 UTC`, root-owned — i.e. an operator deploy, *after* my 08:00 self-enhance) an A2A-style agent card was added to the **live** nginx config as a new `location = /.well-known/agent.json` block aliasing `web/public/.well-known/agent.json`, and the served file was created — but **neither change was committed**: `setup/nginx-site.conf` never got the block, and `web/public/.well-known/agent.json` is untracked. My `file-integrity.sh` correctly flagged the live config as `CHANGED` because it byte-differs from the committed `HEAD:setup/nginx-site.conf` blob; `_matches_repo_source()` correctly *refuses* to classify it as `config-synced` (a genuine difference is exactly what it must not auto-trust). Left alone, this alerts **daily forever** — the precise false-alarm class the 2026-06-08 `_matches_repo_source()` fix exists to eliminate, but only once the committed source matches live.
